Nonlocal operators with Neumann conditions Thanks: The study has been supported by the Opus grant 2017/27/B/ST1/01339 of the National Science Center (Poland).
Krzysztof Bogdan, Damian Fafuła, Paweł Sztonyk

Abstract
. We construct a strong Markov process corresponding to the Dirichlet form of Servadei and Valdinoci and use the process to solve the corresponding Neumann boundary problem for the fractional Laplacian and the half-line.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 60J50; Secondary 60J40, 47D06

1.
Introduction and Preliminaries

1.1.
Motivation

Many nonlocal operators considered in PDEs arise as infinitesimal generators of transition semigroups of jump Markov processes, even Lévy processes.
In particular, the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
is
the infinitesimal generator of the isotropic

\alpha
-stable Lévy process; see Sato
[
60
, Section 31]
, Kwaśnicki
[
50
]
, Nezza et al.
[
31
]
, or Silvestre
[
66
]
for more information.

Although they form a narrow class of Markov processes, Lévy processes are rather representative and play an important role in modeling real-world phenomena: For their applications to financial markets, see Barndorff–Nielsen et al.
[
4
]
, Cont and Tankov
[
28
]
, and Schoutens
[
62
]
.
For applications to genetics, see, e.g., Blomberg et al.
[
7
]
, Gjessing et al.
[
40
]
and Landis et al.
[
51
]
. For applications to fluid mechanics, solid state physics, and polymer chemistry, see, e.g., Barndorff–Nielsen et al.
[
4
]
.

In specific problems, it is important to consider
boundary conditions
. For instance, the Dirichlet problem for
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
and open set
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is

(1.1)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u=f,&\textrm{ in }D,\\
\phantom{(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}}u=f,&\textrm{ in }\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\overline{D}.\end{cases}

The problem has been studied in various settings for many years, see, e.g., the surveys by Ros–Oton
[
58
]
and Bucur, Valdinoci
[
21
]
and the papers by Felsinger et al.
[
36
]
, Rutkowski
[
59
]
, and Servadei, Valdinoci
[
63
,
64
]
.
Here we focus on the
variational setting
,
using quadratic forms and Sobolev-type function spaces.
To this end, we recall the Servadei–Valdinoci form

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{D}(u,v):=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus D^{c}\times D^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y,

where
u,v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathrm{d}x)
, and
\nu
is the integral kernel of
(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}
; see, e.g.,
[
32
]
. The form (
1.2
) first appeared in Servadei and Valdinoci
[
64
]
, see also
[
14
,
36
,
55
,
63
,
64
,
69
]
. Its main virtue for the Dirichlet problem is that (finiteness of) the form imposes minimal smoothness assumptions on the values of
f
on
D^{c}

[
14
]
. As we shall see below, the form is also natural in the variational setting of the
Neumann problem

(1.3)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\hfill(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u&=~f,\quad\mathrm{in}~D,\\
\hfill\mathcal{N}_{\alpha/2}u&=~f,\quad\,\mathrm{in}~\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus{D},\end{cases}

introduced by Dipierro et al.
[
32
]
. The Neumann problem is the main subject of this paper. Note that (
1.3
) is a set of two nonlocal equations because
the
nonlocal normal derivative

\mathcal{N}_{\alpha/2}
is defined as

(1.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\alpha/2}u(x):=\int_{D}(u(x)-u(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\overline{D}.

Note that (
1.3
) appeared in Dipierro et al.
[
32
]
;
see Grube and Hensiek
[
41
]
for a recent discussion.
The authors of
[
32
]
introduce the following tentative description of a Markov process corresponding to the Neumann problem (
1.3
). Consider a particle moving randomly inside
D
according to the
\alpha
-stable Lévy motion. When the particle attempts to exit
D
to
x\in D^{c}
, it immediately returns to
D
according to
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}
. To wit, the distribution of the return position is proportional to
\nu(x,y)\mathrm{d}y
for
y\in D
, so in fact, it is
\nu(x,\mathrm{d}y)/\nu(x,D)
. However suggestive, this description has a problem: The operator

\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}
has a
finite
integral kernel so in reality the particle should spend
positive
time at
x
, before returning to
D
. To resolve this difficulty, Vondraček
[
70
]
considers the form (
1.2
) on a suitable
weighted

L^{2}
space on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Upon exit, the particle in the model of Vonraček stays at
x\in D^{c}
for an exponential time with mean one. This may be considered as a
change of clock
in the model of Servadei–Valdinoci. Put differently,
[
70
]
replaces
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}f(x)
by
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}f(x)/\nu(x,D)
.

Fig. 1.1.
Trajectory of
X
with
X_{0}=1
,
\alpha=1.3
(left) and its zoom-in at the lifetime (right).

Fig. 1.2.
Trajectory of
X
with
X_{0}=1
for
\alpha=1
(left) and its zoom-out (right).

Fig. 1.3.
Trajectory of
X
with
X_{0}=1
for
\alpha=0.9
(left) and its zoom out (right).

Our variant of the Servadei–Valdinoci process
X
on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
preserves the original nonlocal normal derivative
\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}
and the time the process
X

spends at
x\in\overline{D}^{c}
is exponential with intensity
\nu(x,D)
. Thus, starting in
D
,
X
moves as the isotropic
\alpha
-stable Lévy process until the first exit time
\tau_{D}
from
D
. At this moment, the process jumps from
X_{\tau_{D}-}\in D
to a point
y\in\overline{D}^{c}
according to the density function
\nu(X_{\tau_{D}-},y)/\nu(X_{\tau_{D}-},D^{c})
. Then it spends at
y
an exponential time with mean
1/\nu(y,D)
, after which it jumps back to
z\in D
according to density function
\nu(y,z)/\nu(y,D)
, and then starts afresh.
Typical trajectories of the process
X
are presented in Figures
1.3
,
1.3
and
1.3
.
The main question that arises for this model is whether
X
can undergo infinitely many reflections in finite time. As we shall see, it depends on
\alpha
.

In what follows, we specialize to the one-dimensional setting of the real line
\mathbb{R}
, so
d:=1
, and let

D:=(0,\infty),

the positive open half-line.
In this setting, we construct the genuine Servadei–Valdinoci process
X=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, investigate the lifetime of
X
, show that
\mathcal{E}_{D}
is the Dirichlet form of the process, and give solutions to the Neumann boundary problem (
1.3
).
Similar results should follow for general
smooth
open sets in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
—see
[
11
]
—but such extensions are nontrivial and are outside of the scope of the paper.

The structure of the paper is as follows. Below in this section, we state main results and provide necessary definitions. Section
2
presents a construction of
X
as a strong Markov process by using
concatenation of Markov processes

[
71
]
. Then we study the transition semigroup
K=(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
of
X
and examine its excessive functions. They yield the lifetime and limiting behavior of
X
, see Theorem
1.2
, which is proved in Section
3
, Proposition
4.15
describes how the infinitesimal generator of
K
acts on power functions. In Theorem
1.1
, proved in Section
5
, we discuss the Hardy inequality for the quadratic form
\mathcal{E}
of
K
. In Section
5
, we also verify that
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{D}
, propose various characterizations of the domain of the form and, in Proposition
5.8
and Corollary
5.9
, we show that the form is regular. Theorem
1.3
, proved in Section
6
, gives solutions to the Neumann boundary problem (
1.3
).

Acknowledgments.
We thank Wolfhard Hansen and Markus Kunze for discussions on the topic of the paper.

1.2.
Main results

Let us state our main results announced above.
For
t>0
, we define

\mathcal{E}^{(t)}(u,v):=\frac{1}{t}\langle u-K_{t}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},\qquad u,v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Let
\mathcal{E}[u]:=\sup_{t>0}\mathcal{E}^{(t)}(u,u)
for
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,

\mathcal{F}:=\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}):\mathcal{E}[u]<\infty\}
, and

\mathcal{E}(u,v):=\lim_{t\to 0}\mathcal{E}^{(t)}(u,v)
, for
u,v\in\mathcal{F}
. Note that
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{D}
on
\mathcal{F}
.

Theorem 1.1

(Hardy inequality)
.

For
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
,

(1.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}[u]\geq(\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}+\mathcal{D}_{\alpha})\int_{D}u^{2}(x)|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}\int_{D^{c}}u^{2}(x)|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x,

where

\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\Big[\alpha^{-1}-\frac{\big(\Gamma(\tfrac{\alpha+1}{2})\big)^{2}}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}\Big]>0\qquad and\qquad\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-t^{(\alpha-1)/2})^{2}}{(1-t)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}t>0.

The proof of Theorem
1.1
is given in Section
5.2
.

Let
R_{\infty}
be the lifetime of the Servadei–Valdinoci process
X
.

Theorem 1.2
.

The following statements hold
\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. for every
x\neq 0
.

(1)

If
\alpha\in(0,1)
then
R_{\infty}=\infty
and
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}|X_{t}|=\infty
.

(2)

If
\alpha\in(1,2)
then
0<R_{\infty}<\infty
and
\lim\limits_{t\nearrow R_{\infty}}X_{t}=0
.

(3)

If
\alpha=1
then
R_{\infty}=\infty
and
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}|X_{t}|
does not exist. In particular,

\liminf\limits_{t\to\infty}|X_{t}|=0\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\limsup\limits_{t\to\infty}|X_{t}|=\infty.

The result is a close counterpart of Bogdan et al.
[
11
, Proposition 4.2]
for the censored stable processes. The proof of Theorem
1.2
is given in Section
3.4
.

Let
G
be the 0-potential operator of
K,
i.e.,

Gf(x):=\int_{0}^{\infty}K_{t}f(x)\,{\rm d}t.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
and
f\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})
. Then,
G|f|<\infty
on
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and
u=Gf
is the solution of the following Neumann problem for the fractional Laplacian

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u&=f,\quad\mathrm{in}~D,\\
\phantom{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,}\mathcal{N}_{\alpha/2}u&=f,\quad\mathrm{in}~\overline{D}^{c}.\end{cases}

The proof of Theorem
1.3
is given at the end of the paper.

1.3.
Notation

For
A\subset\mathbb{R}
, we let
A^{c}:=\mathbb{R}\setminus A
.
The
open ball
of radius
r>0
and centered at
x\in\mathbb{R}
is denoted by
B(x,r):=\{y\in\mathbb{R}:~|x-y|<r\}
. We let
D=(0,\infty)
and
\mathbb{R}_{*}:=\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.
Below we only consider Borel sets, functions, and measures. As usual,
\ln{x}:=\log_{e}{x}
,
x>0
. For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we set
a\wedge b:=\min(a,b)
,
a\vee b:=\max(a,b)
,
a_{+}:=a\vee 0
and
a_{-}:=(-a)\vee 0
.

For functions
f,g\geq 0
, we write
f(x)\lesssim g(x)
if there exists a number
C\in(0,\infty)
, called
constant
, such that
f(x)\leq Cg(x)
for all the considered arguments
x
. We denote constants by
C,C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
and they may change value from place to place. We are rarely interested in exact values of the constants, but we write
C=C(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
if the constant
C
can be so chosen to depend only on
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
; we may write
C_{a}
if
C=C(a)
. We similarly interpret the inequality
f(x)\gtrsim g(x)
and we may write
f(x)\approx g(x)
if
f(x)\lesssim g(x)
and
f(x)\gtrsim g(x)
.

If
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathbb{R}
is a nonempty open set, then
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{U})
denotes the class of all the Borel measurable functions on
\mathcal{U}
. By
\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathcal{U})
and
\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{U})
, we denote the subclasses of
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{U})
which consist of non-negative and bounded functions, respectively.
Moreover,
\mathcal{B}_{b}^{+}(\mathcal{U}):=\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{U})\cap\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathcal{U})
. The class of all the real-valued continuous functions on
\mathcal{U}
will be denoted by
C(\mathcal{U})
. We also consider subclasses of
C(\mathcal{U})
. Namely,
C_{b}(\mathcal{U})
is the class of all the bounded continuous functions on
\mathcal{U}
,
C_{c}(\mathcal{U})
is the class of all the compactly supported functions on
\mathcal{U}
,
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{U})
is the class of all smooth compactly supported functions on
\mathcal{U}
, and
C_{0}(\mathcal{U})
is the class of all the continuous functions
vanishing at infinity
, i.e.,

C_{0}(\mathcal{U})=\{f\in C(\mathcal{U}):~\forall\,{\varepsilon>0}~~\exists\,\mathrm{compact}\,K\subset\mathcal{U}\,\mathrm{such\,that}\,|f(x)|<\varepsilon\,\,\mathrm{for}\,\,x\notin K\}.

In particular,
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
consists of all the continuous functions
f
on
\mathbb{R}_{*}
such that
\lim\limits_{|x|\to\infty}f(x)=0
and
\lim\limits_{x\to 0}f(x)=0
.
Of course,
C_{0}(\mathcal{U})
is a Banach space with the supremum norm
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}:=\sup\limits_{x\in\mathcal{U}}|f(x)|
. By
L^{2}(\mathcal{U})
, we denote the class of all the (Borel) square integrable functions on
\mathcal{U}
equipped with the norm
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathcal{U})}=\sqrt{\int_{\mathcal{U}}|u(x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x}
, and we identify functions equal a.e. with respect to
\mathrm{d}x
.

Let
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
and
(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
be two measurable spaces. As usual (see, e.g., Getoor
[
39
]
, Bliedtner and Hansen
[
6
, Section II.1]
), a (
probability
or
Markov
)
kernel
from
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
to
(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
is a function
K:\mathscr{X}\times\mathcal{B}\to[0,\infty)
such that

(i)

for every
B\in\mathcal{B}
, the function
x\mapsto K(x,A)
is
\mathcal{A}
-measurable,

(ii)

for every
x\in\mathscr{X}
, the function
B\mapsto K(x,B)
is a (probability) measure on
(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
.

If
K(x,\mathscr{Y})\leq 1
for every
x\in\mathscr{X}
, then
K
is called
subprobability
or
sub-Markov
kernel. By a kernel on
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
, we simply mean a kernel from
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
to
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
.

For every kernel
K
,
x\in\mathscr{X}
and
f\in\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathscr{Y})
,

(1.6)

\displaystyle Kf(x):=\int_{\mathscr{Y}}f(y)K(x,\mathrm{d}y),

defines the corresponding
integral operator
from
\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathscr{Y})
to
\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathscr{X})
, which is additive, positively homogeneous and monotone.
1
1

1

Monotone means here that
f_{n},f\in\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathscr{X})
and
f_{n}\uparrow f
imply
Kf_{n}\uparrow Kf
.
Conversely, every additive, positively homogeneous and monotone operator on
\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathscr{X})
is of the form (
1.6
) for some kernel
[
6
, Section II.1]
. If
K
is subprobability kernel, then (
1.6
) defines a linear bounded operator from
\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathscr{Y})
to
\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathscr{X})
. If the kernel
K
has a Radon–Nikodym density
k
with respect to a measure
m
on
\mathscr{Y}
, that is
K(x,A)=\int_{A}k(x,y)m(\mathrm{d}y)
,
A\in\mathcal{B}
, then we call
k
a
kernel density
.

Let
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A}),(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
and
(\mathscr{Z},\mathcal{C})
be measurable spaces. Assume that
K
is a kernel from
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
to
(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
and
L
is a kernel from
(\mathscr{Y},\mathcal{B})
to
(\mathscr{Z},\mathcal{C})
. Then the
composition
of kernels
K
and
L
is defined as the following kernel
KL
from
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
to
(\mathscr{Z},\mathcal{C})
,

(KL)(x,C):=\int_{\mathscr{Y}}K(x,\mathrm{d}y)L(y,C),

for
x\in\mathscr{X}
,
C\in\mathcal{C}
. If
K
and
L
are probability (resp. subprobability) kernels, then
KL
is a probability (resp. subprobability) kernel.

A family
(T_{t})_{t\geq 0}
of probability (resp. subprobability) kernels on
(\mathscr{X},\mathcal{A})
is called a
probability
(resp.
subprobability
)
transition kernel
if the kernels satisfy the semigroup property
T_{t+s}=T_{t}T_{s}
,
t,s\geq 0
. The corresponding family of integral operators, also denoted by
(T_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, is called
Markov
(resp.
sub-Markov
)
semigroup of operators
.

A sub-Markov semigroup
(T_{t})_{t\geq 0}
of operators is called a
Feller semigroup
(see, e.g., Kallenberg
[
47
, p. 369]
) if for
0\leq f\leq 1
, we have
0\leq T_{t}f\leq 1
,
t\geq 0
, and

(P1)

for
f\in C_{0}(\mathscr{X})
and
t\geq 0
,
T_{t}f\in C_{0}(\mathscr{X})
,

(P2)

for
f\in C_{0}(\mathscr{X})
and
x\in\mathscr{X}
,
T_{t}f(x)\to f(x)
, as
t\to 0^{+}
.

It is well known (see
[
47
, Chapter 19]
), that (P1), (P2) and the semigroup property yield the strong continuity of
(T_{t})_{t\geq 0}
:
\lim\limits_{t\to 0^{+}}\left\lVert T_{t}f-f\right\rVert_{\infty}=0
for
f\in C_{0}(\mathscr{X})
.

We also consider
strong Feller
semigroups of operators
(T_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, i.e. semigroups with the property
T_{t}\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathscr{X})\subset C_{b}(\mathscr{X})
,
t\geq 0
. Furthermore,
double Feller semigroups
of operators mean the semigroups with both Feller and strong Feller property (see, e.g., Chen and Kuwae
[
24
]
).

The
Euler beta
function may be defined by

\mathfrak{B}(x,y):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{x-1}}{(1+t)^{x+y}}\,{\rm d}t,\qquad x>0,~y>0.

It is well known that

\mathfrak{B}(x,y)=\frac{\Gamma(x)\Gamma(y)}{\Gamma(x+y)},

where
\Gamma
denotes the Euler gamma function.

1.4.
Isotropic
\alpha
-stable Lévy process

In this section, we introduce the isotropic
\alpha
-stable Lévy process (as a general reference, see Kwaśnicki
[
50
]
).

Let
\alpha\in(0,2)
. For
t>0
, let

(1.7)

\displaystyle p_{t}(x):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i\xi x}e^{-t|\xi|^{\alpha}}\,\mathrm{d}\xi,\quad t>0,~x\in\mathbb{R}.

From (
1.7
), we see that the function
x\mapsto p_{t}(x)
is smooth, being the inverse Fourier transform of a rapidly decreasing function. Furthermore, it is well known that
p_{t}\geq 0
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=1
,

(1.8)

\displaystyle p_{t}(x)=t^{-1/\alpha}p_{1}(xt^{-1/\alpha}),\quad t>0,~x\in\mathbb{R},

and

(1.9)

\displaystyle p_{t}(x)\approx t^{-1/\alpha}\wedge\frac{t}{|x|^{1+\alpha}},\quad t>0,~x\in\mathbb{R},

see Blumenthal and Getoor
[
8
]
or Pòlya
[
56
]
; see also
[
46
]
.
For
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote
p_{t}(x,y):=p_{t}(y-x)
. By (
1.7
),

\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{s}(x,y)p_{t}(y,z)\,\mathrm{d}y=p_{t+s}(x,z),\quad s,t\geq 0,~x,z\in\mathbb{R}.

It is a kernel density and defines the transition kernel,

P_{t}(x,A):=\int_{A}p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad x\in\mathbb{R},~A\subset\mathbb{R}.

Of course, the corresponding Markov semigroup of operators is defined by

\displaystyle P_{t}f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathbb{R})\cup\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathbb{R})\cup L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

It has the fractional Laplacian as infinitesimal generator (see Kwaśnicki
[
50
]
):

(1.10)

\displaystyle-(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}\varphi(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{P_{t}\varphi(x)-\varphi(x)}{t}=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}~\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\varphi(x+y)-\varphi(x)}{|y|^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{|y|>\varepsilon}\frac{\varphi(x+y)-\varphi(x)}{|y|^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\quad\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}),

where

\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}:=\frac{2^{\alpha}\Gamma((\alpha+1)/2)}{\sqrt{\pi}|\Gamma(-\alpha/2)|}.

The
isotropic
\alpha
-stable Lévy process

\big((Y_{t})_{t\geq 0},(\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y})_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big)
on
\mathbb{R}
may be defined as a càdlàg Markov process satisfying
\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}(Y_{0}=x)=1
with the transition probability:

P_{t}^{Y}(x,A):=P_{t}(x,A),\quad t>0,~x\in\mathbb{R},~A\subset\mathbb{R}.

Here and in what follows,
\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}
and
\mathbb{E}_{x}^{Y}
denote the distribution and expectation for the process
Y
starting from
x\in\mathbb{R}
, respectively. Of course,
(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
has independent increments; see (
1.7
). It is well known that
(Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a strong Markov process with respect to the standard filtration
[
9
]
. Moreover, for
a>0
,
aY_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{d}}}{{=}}Y_{a^{\alpha}t}
as processes (in distribution with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{Y}
), which follows from (
1.8
).

For (Borel) set
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, we let

\tau_{B}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:~Y_{t}\notin B\},

be the time of the first exit of
Y
from
B
. If
r>0
and
t>0
, then, with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{Y}
,

\displaystyle(Y_{t},\tau_{B(0,r)})

\displaystyle=\big(Y_{t},\,\inf\{s\geq 0:~|Y_{s}|\geq r\}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(r\cdot\tfrac{1}{r}Y_{t},\,\inf\{s\geq 0:~|\tfrac{1}{r}Y_{s}|\geq 1\}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{d}}}{{=}}\big(r\cdot Y_{r^{-\alpha}t},\,\inf\{s\geq 0:~|Y_{r^{-\alpha}s}|\geq 1\}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(r\cdot Y_{r^{-\alpha}t},\,r^{\alpha}\inf\{r^{-\alpha}s\geq 0:~|Y_{r^{-\alpha}s}|\geq 1\}\big)

(1.11)

\displaystyle=\big(r\cdot Y_{r^{-\alpha}t},\,r^{\alpha}\tau_{B(0,1)}\big).

Similarly we prove that, with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{Y}
,

(1.12)

\displaystyle Y_{\tau_{B(0,r)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{d}}}{{=}}rY_{\tau_{B(0,1)}}.

The measure
\nu(\mathrm{d}x):=\nu(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
, with the density function

(1.13)

\displaystyle\nu(x):=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}|x|^{-1-\alpha},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}_{*},

is the Lévy measure of
Y
. In particular,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1\wedge|x|^{2})\nu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
. As in the case of
p_{t}
, we slightly abuse the notation and write
\nu(x,y):=\nu(y-x)
. Furthermore, for
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
A\subset\mathbb{R}
, we let

(1.14)

\displaystyle\nu(x,A):=\nu(A-x)=\int_{A}\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Recall that
D=(0,\infty)\subset\mathbb{R}
. For later use, we note that

(1.15)

\displaystyle\nu(kx,ky)=k^{-\alpha-1}\nu(x,y),\quad k>0,~x,y\in\mathbb{R},

(1.16)

\displaystyle\nu(kx,D)=k^{-\alpha}\nu(x,D),\quad k>0,~x\in\mathbb{R},

(1.17)

\displaystyle\nu(x,D)=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}|x|^{-\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{(1+y)^{\alpha+1}}=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\alpha^{-1}|x|^{-\alpha},\qquad x<0,

and

(1.18)

\displaystyle\nu(x,D^{c})=\nu(-x,D)=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\alpha^{-1}x^{-\alpha},\qquad x>0.

Of course,
\nu(0,D)=\infty
.

1.5.
Potential theory

Using
\tau_{D}
we define the transition density of the
process killed
upon leaving
D
:

(1.19)

\displaystyle p_{t}^{D}(x,y)=p_{t}(x,y)-\mathbb{E}_{x}^{Y}\big[\tau_{D}<t;~p_{t-\tau_{D}}(Y_{\tau_{D}},y)\big],\quad t>0,~x,y\in\mathbb{R}.

The above is called the
Hunt formula
(references for this section and more details can be found in
[
13
]
,
[
23
]
, or
[
27
, Chapter 2]
). We have the scaling property

(1.20)

p_{t}^{D}(x,y)=t^{-1/\alpha}p_{1}^{D}(t^{-1/\alpha}x,t^{-1/\alpha}y),\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R},~t>0,

or, equivalently,

(1.21)

p_{k^{\alpha}t}^{D}(kx,ky)=k^{-1}p_{t}^{D}(x,y),\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R},~k,t>0.

We note that
p^{D}
is jointly continuous in
t,x,y
. This follows from the scaling property (
1.20
) and the continuity of the function
(x,y)\mapsto p_{t}(x,y)
by the same proof as in Theorem 2.4 in Chung and Zhao
[
27
]
. Moreover, for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
and
t>0
,
p^{D}
is
symmetric
, i.e.
p^{D}_{t}(x,y)=p^{D}_{t}(y,x)
,

(1.22)

\displaystyle 0\leq p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\leq p_{t}(x,y),

and
p_{t}^{D}(x,y)=0
whenever
x\leq 0
or
y\leq 0
. Furthermore,
p^{D}
satisfies the Chapman–Kolmogorov equations:

(1.23)

\displaystyle p_{t+s}^{D}(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t}^{D}(x,z)p_{s}^{D}(z,y)\,{\rm d}z,\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R},~s,t>0.

The function
p^{D}
is called
Dirichlet heat kernel
of the half-line
D
for the fractional Laplacian, or transition density of the isotropic
\alpha
-stable process killed when leaving
D
, because

(1.24)

\displaystyle P_{t}^{D}(x,A):=\int_{A}p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}(Y_{t}\in A,\tau_{D}>t),\quad t>0,~x\in\mathbb{R},~A\subset\mathbb{R}.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
t>0
, and nonnegative or bounded functions
f
, we as usual denote

\displaystyle P_{t}^{D}f(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)P_{t}^{D}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\mathbb{E}_{x}^{Y}[f(Y_{t});\tau_{D}>t].

We also let
P_{0}^{D}=I
, the identity operator.

By Bogdan and Grzywny
[
13
]
,

(1.25)

p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\approx\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}(\tau_{D}>t)\mathbb{P}_{y}^{Y}(\tau_{D}>t)p_{t}(x,y),\quad t>0,~x,y>0,

and

(1.26)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}(\tau_{D}>t)\approx 1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}},\quad t>0,~x>0.

Thus,

(1.27)

\displaystyle p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\approx\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)\Big(t^{-1/\alpha}\wedge\frac{t}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\Big),\quad t>0,~x,y>0.

The
Green function
of
D
is

(1.28)

\displaystyle G_{D}(x,y):=\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\,{\rm d}t,\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R}.

Obviously,
G_{D}(x,y)=0
whenever
x\leq 0
or
y\leq 0
.
For each function
f\geq 0
, from Tonelli’s theorem and (
1.24
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{D}G_{D}(x,y)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\mathbb{E}_{x}^{Y}\int_{0}^{\tau_{D}}f(Y_{t})\,{\rm d}t,\quad x>0,

hence
G_{D}(x,y)
is the occupation time density of the process
Y
prior to its first exit from
D
. By (
1.21
),

(1.29)

\displaystyle G_{D}(x,y)=x^{\alpha-1}G_{D}(1,y/x),\qquad x,y>0.

It is well known (see
[
54
]
, Corollary 3.5) that
\mathbb{P}_{x}(Y_{\tau_{D}}=0)=0
, for every
x>0
, and from
[
44
]
or
[
19
]
, the joint distribution of the triple
(\tau_{D},Y_{\tau_{D}-},Y_{\tau_{D}})
is given by the
Ikeda–Watanabe formula
:

(1.30)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}\big(\tau_{D}\in I,~Y_{\tau_{D}-}\in A,Y_{\tau_{D}}\in B\big)=\int_{I}{\rm d}t\int_{A}\mathrm{d}y\int_{B}{\rm d}z\,p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z),\quad x>0.

By (
1.24
) and (
1.30
), we thus have

(1.31)

\displaystyle P_{t}^{D}(x,D)=\int_{D}p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\mathbb{P}^{Y}_{x}(\tau_{D}>t)=\int_{t}^{\infty}{\rm d}r\int_{D}{\rm d}a\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}b\,p_{r}^{D}(x,a)\nu(a,b).

We further define the
Poisson kernel
of
D
for the fractional Laplacian:

(1.32)

\displaystyle P_{D}(x,y)=\int_{D}G_{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)\,{\rm d}z,\quad x>0,~y\leq 0.

Then, for
A\subset(-\infty,0]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}(Y_{\tau_{D}}\in A)=\int_{A}P_{D}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad x>0.

Using (
1.29
), we obtain the following scaling property of the Poisson kernel:

(1.33)

\displaystyle P_{D}(x,y)=x^{-1}P_{D}(1,y/x),\qquad x>0,~y\leq 0.

In fact, the Poisson kernel of the half-line is known explicitly (see
[
10
, (3.40)]
):

(1.34)

\displaystyle P_{D}(x,y)=\frac{1}{\pi}\sin\Big(\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\Big)\frac{x^{\alpha/2}}{|y|^{\alpha/2}|x-y|},\quad x>0,~y\leq 0.

The following results assert the double Feller property of
P^{D}
. For the convenience of the reader, we give short proofs.

Lemma 1.4
.

For every
t>0
, we have
P_{t}^{D}\mathcal{B}_{b}(D)\subset C_{b}(D)
.

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(D)
. We define
f=0
on
D^{c}
. For
t>0
, the function
D\ni x\mapsto P_{t}f(x)=p_{t}*f(x)
is continuous, because
p_{t}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. From Vitali’s theorem (see Schilling
[
61
, Theorem 16.6]
), it follows that the functions
y\mapsto p_{t}(x,y)f(y)
are uniformly in
x
integrable with respect to the measure
\ind_{D}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y
. From (
1.22
) it follows that
y\mapsto p_{t}^{D}(x,y)f(y)
are also uniformly integrable (see
[
61
, Definition 16.1 or Theorem 16.8]
). Since the mapping
D\ni x\mapsto p_{t}^{D}(x,y)
is continuous, Vitali’s theorem gives the continuity of the function
D\ni x\mapsto P_{t}^{D}f(x)
. Of course,
\lVert P_{t}^{D}f\rVert_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}
, by (
1.22
).
∎

Lemma 1.5
.

(P_{t}^{D})_{t\geq 0}
is a Feller semigroup on
C_{0}(D)
.

Proof.

We have
P_{t}^{D}C_{0}(D)\subset C_{0}(D)
for every
t>0
. Indeed, let
f\in C_{0}(D)
. The continuity of
P_{t}^{D}f
follows from Lemma
1.4
.

Let
x\to+\infty
. By (
1.22
),

|P_{t}^{D}f(x)|\leq\int_{D}p_{t}^{D}(x,y)|f(y)|\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\int_{D}p_{t}(x,y)|f(y)|\,\mathrm{d}y\to 0,

which follows from the Feller property of
P_{t}
(see
[
47
, Theorem 19.10]
).

Moreover, let
x\to 0^{+}
. By (
1.31
) and (
1.26
),

|P_{t}^{D}f(x)|\leq\int_{D}p_{t}^{D}(x,y)|f(y)|\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}p_{t}^{D}(x,D)=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}(\tau_{D}>t)\approx 1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\to 0,

as needed.

Next, we will verify that
P_{t}^{D}f\to f
in
C_{0}(D)
, as
t\to 0^{+}
. For
x>0
, by (
1.22
),

\displaystyle\big|P_{t}^{D}f(x)-f(x)\big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{D}(f(y)-f(x))p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y-f(x)(1-p_{t}^{D}(x,D))\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{D}|f(y)-f(x)|p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\big|1-p_{t}^{D}(x,D)\big|.

From (
1.31
),
p_{t}^{D}(x,D)\to 1
as
t\to 0^{+}
.

The estimate of the remaining integral is the same as in the proof of Theorem 1.7 in
[
27
]
: Let
\varepsilon>0
. Since
f\in C_{0}(D)
, there is
\delta>0
such that
|f(y)-f(x)|<\varepsilon
for
|y-x|<\delta
. Therefore, from (
1.9
) we get

\displaystyle\int_{D}

\displaystyle|f(y)-f(x)|p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\int_{D\cap\{|y-x|<\delta\}}p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{D\cap\{|y-x|\geq\delta\}}|f(y)-f(x)|p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon P_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})+2\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\int_{D\cap\{|y-x|\geq\delta\}}p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon+2C\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}t\int_{|w|\geq\delta}|w|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\varepsilon+\frac{4C}{\alpha}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}t\delta^{-\alpha}.\qed

1.6.
Markov processes and the strong Markov property

In what follows, we work with Markov processes, so we introduce the basic notions for such processes. Our notation is similar to that of Werner in
[
71
]
, since we use his results below.

Let
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathbb{R}
be an open set. A
Markov process

X
on
\mathcal{U}
is defined as a following sextuple (see, e.g.,
[
9
]
,
[
65
]
):

X=(\Omega,\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0},(X_{t})_{t\geq 0},(\theta_{t})_{t\geq 0},(\mathbb{P}_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{U}}),

where
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a right continuous,
\mathcal{U}
-valued stochastic process on a measurable space
(\Omega,\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})
, adapted to filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and equipped with shift operators
(\theta_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on
\Omega
. Moreover,
(\mathbb{P}_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{U}}
is a family of sub-probability measures such that
\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{0}=x)=1
for all
x\in\mathcal{U}
(
\mathbb{E}_{x}
are the corresponding expectations). Furthermore, for arbitrary
t\geq 0
and
A\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
, the function
x\mapsto\mathbb{P}_{x}(X_{t}\in A)
is measurable, and we have the following
Markov property
: For
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathcal{U})
,

\mathbb{E}_{x}[f(X_{t+s})|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}[f(X_{t})],\quad x\in\mathcal{U},~s,t\geq 0.

We make a few further standard assumptions. We assume that the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is augmented by the null sets and right continuous, and we add an isolated, absorbing
cemetery
point
\Delta
to
\mathcal{U}
. Thus, we let
\mathcal{U}_{\Delta}:=\mathcal{U}\cup\{\Delta\}
. The lifetime of the process is

\zeta:=\inf\{t\geq 0:~X_{t}=\Delta\},

and we have
X_{t}=\Delta
if
t\geq\zeta
. Moreover, there exists a
dead path

[\Delta]\in\Omega
for which
\zeta([\Delta])=0
.
2
2

2

If we identify
\omega
with the trajectory
t\mapsto X_{t}(\omega)
, then
[\Delta]
is the trajectory
X_{t}\equiv\Delta
.
We also assume that
X_{\infty}=\Delta
,
\theta_{\infty}\omega=[\Delta]
for all
\omega\in\Omega
and for any measurable numerical function
f
,
f(\Delta)=0
.

The semigroup
(T_{t})_{t\geq 0}
corresponding to the stochastic process
X
is defined as

T_{t}f(x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}[f(X_{t})],\quad t\geq 0,~x\in\mathcal{U},

where
f
is bounded or non-negative.

Let
\lambda\in[0,\infty)
. We say that measurable function
f
on
\mathcal{U}
is
\lambda
–excessive
(see, e.g.,
[
6
]
[Section II.3]) if
f\geq 0
,
e^{-\lambda t}T_{t}f\leq f
for
t>0
, and
e^{-\lambda t}T_{t}f\to f
as
t\to 0^{+}
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}
be the set of all excessive functions. The process
X
is called
right process
if it is a Markov process equipped with an augmented and right continuous filtration and for all
\lambda>0
,
f\in\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}
, the map
t\mapsto f(X_{t})
is a.s. right continuous.

Right processes have the following
strong Markov property
(see
[
71
]
): for every stopping time
\tau
with respect to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and bounded
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable function
f
,
3
3

3

As usual,
X_{t}\circ\theta_{\tau}(\omega)
means
X_{t}(\theta_{\tau}\omega)
.

(1.35)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}[f(X_{t}\circ\theta_{\tau})|\mathcal{F}_{\tau}]=\mathbb{E}_{X_{\tau}}[f(X_{t})],\quad t\geq 0,~x\in\mathcal{U}.

Here
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is the universal completion of
\sigma(X_{s}:~s\geq 0)
with respect to all
\mathbb{P}^{x}
.
We call
X
a
Feller process
or a
strong Feller process
if the corresponding semigroup
(T_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is Feller or strong Feller, respectively.

It is well known that the isotropic
\alpha
-stable Lévy process killed when leaving
D
, namely,

Y_{t}^{D}:=\begin{cases}Y_{t},&t<\tau_{D},\\
\Delta,&t\geq\tau_{D},\end{cases}

is a right process with lifetime
\xi=\tau_{D}
. Indeed, it is a Feller process, so a Hunt process (see Chung
[
26
]
), and every Hunt process is a right process (see Getoor
[
39
, Section 9]
).

2.
The Servadei–Valdinoci process

In this section, we construct a stochastic (Servadei–Valdinoci) process on
\mathbb{R}_{*}
, which is the main focus of the paper. The construction relies on concatenation of right Markov processes, as presented in
[
71
]
. Further, we give the corresponding transition kernel and semigroup of operators, and study their properties. In particular, we prove they are sub-Markovian, strong continuous, and introduce auxiliary excessive functions for the semigroup.

2.1.
Construction of the process

We start with a construction of the stochastic process. Its behaviour depends on the starting point: starting from
x>0
the process behaves like a certain right process
Z^{1}
, while starting from
x<0
it behaves like a different right process
Z^{2}
. The processes
Z^{1}
and
Z^{2}
are defined as follows. Let

Z^{1}=(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{F}^{1},(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{1})_{t\geq 0},(Z_{t}^{1})_{t\geq 0},(\theta_{t}^{1})_{t\geq 0},(\mathbb{P}_{x}^{1})_{x>0}),

be the isotropic
\alpha
-stable Lévy process killed when leaving
D=(0,\infty)
, i.e.
Z^{1}:=Y^{D}
. We denote the transition kernel of
Z^{1}
by

P^{1}_{t}(x,A):=P_{t}^{D}(x,A)=\int_{A}p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad t>0,~x>0,~A\subset\mathbb{R}.

Of course, the lifetime of
Z^{1}
is
\zeta_{1}^{Z}:=\tau_{D}=\sup\{t\geq 0:~Z^{1}_{t}>0\}
.

The second process we consider lives on
(-\infty,0)
, stays at the starting point
x<0
for an exponential time with mean
1/\nu(x,D)
and afterwards it is killed. The process will be denoted by

Z^{2}=(\Omega^{2},\mathcal{F}^{2},(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{2})_{t\geq 0},(Z_{t}^{2})_{t\geq 0},(\theta_{t}^{2})_{t\geq 0},(\mathbb{P}_{x}^{2})_{x<0}).

Its transition kernel is

P^{2}_{t}(x,A):=\delta_{x}(A)e^{-\nu(x,D)t},\quad x<0,~A\subset\mathbb{R},~t>0,

and its lifetime
\zeta_{2}^{Z}
is the above exponential random variable with mean
1/\nu(x,D)
.

For
t>0
, we define the integral kernel on
\mathbb{R}_{*}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{P}_{t}(x,A)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}P^{1}_{t}(x,A),&\textrm{ if }x>0,\\
P^{2}_{t}(x,A),&\textrm{ if }x<0,\end{cases}

where
A\subset\mathbb{R}_{*}
.
We note that
\widehat{P}_{t}(x,\overline{D}^{c})=0
if
x>0
and
\widehat{P}_{t}(x,D)=0
if
x<0
.

Lemma 2.1
.

For
t>0
,
\widehat{P}_{t}
is a subprobability transition kernel.

Proof.

We clearly have
\widehat{P}_{t}(x,A)\geq 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
A\subset\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,

\widehat{P}_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y=1,\quad x>0,

and

\widehat{P}_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})=e^{-t\nu(x,D)}\leq 1,\quad x<0.

It remains to verify the Chapman–Kolmogorov equations. Let
s,t>0
and
A\subset\mathbb{R}
. For
x>0
, we use (
1.23
). For
x<0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{P}_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\widehat{P}_{s}(y,A)

\displaystyle=e^{-t\nu(x,D)}\widehat{P}_{s}(x,A)=e^{-t\nu(x,D)}e^{-s\nu(x,D)}\delta_{x}(A)

\displaystyle=\delta_{x}(A)e^{-(s+t)\nu(x,D)}=\widehat{P}_{s+t}(x,A),

which completes the proof.
∎

As usual, we let

\widehat{P}_{t}f(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)\widehat{P}_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y),

if
f
is nonnegative or bounded. Additionally, we let
\widehat{P}_{0}:=I
.

Lemma 2.2
.

(\widehat{P}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Feller semigroup on
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.1
,
(\widehat{P}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a sub-Markov semigroup on
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
. Let
f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
. We will show that
\widehat{P}_{t}f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
. The function
D\ni x\mapsto\widehat{P}_{t}f(x)=P_{t}^{D}f(x)
is in
C_{0}(D)
by Lemma
1.5
, and of course,
\overline{D}^{c}\ni x\mapsto\widehat{P}_{t}f(x)=e^{-\nu(x,D)t}f(x)
is in
C_{0}(\overline{D}^{c})
.

To finish the proof, we show that for
f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
,
\widehat{P}_{t}f(x)\to f(x)
, as
t\to 0^{+}
. For
x>0
,

|\widehat{P}_{t}f(x)-f(x)|=|P_{t}^{D}f(x)-f(x)|\to 0,

as
t\to 0^{+}
, which follows from Lemma
1.5
. In the case
x<0
, we have

|\widehat{P}_{t}f(x)-f(x)|\leq|f(x)|\cdot|e^{-\nu(x,D)t}-1|\to 0,

as
t\to 0^{+}
. This proves the lemma.
∎

It follows from Kallenberg
[
47
, Chapter 19]
or Getoor and Blumenthal
[
9
, Theorem I.9.4]
that there exists a Hunt process (hence, a right process
[
65
, (47.3)]
)
Z
on
\mathbb{R}_{*}
, with transition function given by
\widehat{P}_{t}
. The lifetime of this process will be denoted by

\zeta=\begin{cases}\zeta_{1}^{Z},&\textrm{if }Z_{0}>0,\\
\zeta_{2}^{Z},&\textrm{if }Z_{0}<0.\end{cases}

Of course,
\zeta<\infty
a.s. The process
Z
starting from
x>0
is the isotropic
\alpha
-stable Lévy process
Z^{1}=Y
killed when leaving
D
, while starting from
x<0
it is the compound–Poisson type process
Z^{2}
. We will extend
Z
beyond its lifetime by
concatenation
.

A concatenation of functions:
x_{1}
, defined on
[0,t_{1})
,
x_{2}
, defined on
[0,t_{2})
, etc., is a function
x
defined on
[0,t_{1}+t_{2}+\ldots)
by letting
x(t)=x_{n}\big(t-(t_{1}+\ldots+t_{n})\big)
for
t_{1}+\ldots+t_{n}\leq t<t_{1}+\ldots+t_{n}+t_{n+1}
. Here
t_{1},t_{2},\ldots\in[0,\infty)
and if
t_{i}=0
then the function
x_{i}
has no affect on
x
.

We will next concatenate a countable sequence of stochastic processes. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, suppose that the processes
X^{n}=(\Omega^{(n)},\mathcal{F}^{(n)},(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{(n)})_{t\geq 0},(X_{t}^{(n)})_{t\geq 0},(\theta_{t}^{(n)})_{t\geq 0},(\mathbb{P}_{x}^{(n)})_{x\in\mathbb{R}_{*}})
on
\mathbb{R}_{*}
are independent copies of the right process
Z
. By
\zeta^{(n)}
we denote the lifetime of each process
X^{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For each process
X^{n}
, we consider the dead path
[\Delta^{n}]\in\Omega^{(n)}
with the property
\zeta^{(n)}([\Delta^{n}])=0
, and let
\theta_{\infty}^{(n)}(\omega):=[\Delta^{n}]
for all
\omega\in\Omega^{(n)}
.
4
4

4

We distinguish dead paths for each process but the cemetery point attached to
\mathbb{R}_{*}
may be the same, denoted by
\Delta
.

We define the transfer kernel
k
on
\mathbb{R}_{*}
(also called
instantaneous kernel
) to specify jumps between
D
and
\overline{D}^{c}
in the following way: for
A\subset\mathbb{R}
,

k(x,A)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\nu(x,A\cap D^{c})}{\nu(x,D^{c})},&x>0,\\[7.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{\nu(x,A\cap D)}{\nu(x,D)},&x<0.\end{cases}

If
x>0
, then
k(x,\cdot)
gives the starting distribution of the process
Z^{2}
initiated after
Z^{1}
died at
x
. Similarly, for
x<0
,
k(x,\cdot)
is the initial distribution of
Z^{1}
initiated after
Z^{2}
died at
x
. To be more precise, for
k=1,2,\ldots
, we define a
transfer kernel

K^{k}:\Omega^{(k)}\times\mathcal{B}\to[0,1]
by

(2.1)

\displaystyle K^{k}(\omega_{k},A):=k(X^{k}_{\zeta^{(k)}-}(\omega_{k}),A),\quad\omega_{k}\in\Omega^{(k)},~A\subset\mathbb{R}_{*},

where
\mathcal{B}
denotes the
\sigma
-algebra of Borel measurable subsets of
\mathbb{R}_{*}
.

Following Werner
[
71
]
(see also Sharpe
[
65
, Chapter 14]
), we define the process
X
on
\mathbb{R}_{*}
which is called a
concatenation
of the processes
(X^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, as follows.

We set
\Omega=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Omega^{(n)}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}=\bigotimes_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{F}^{(n)}
. For
\omega=(\omega_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\Omega
,
t\geq 0
and
n\geq 1
, we define
\widetilde{R}_{n}(\omega):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\zeta^{(k)}(\omega_{k})
and
\widetilde{R}_{0}(\omega)=0
, and
\widetilde{R}_{\infty}(\omega)=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\widetilde{R}_{n}(\omega)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\zeta^{(k)}(\omega_{k})
. To simplify notation, we make the convention
\zeta^{(k)}(\omega)=\zeta(\omega_{k})=\zeta^{(k)}(\omega_{k})
.

Following the construction in
[
71
]
for all
t\geq 0
,
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\ldots)\in\Omega
, we let

\displaystyle X_{t}(\omega)=\begin{cases}X_{t}^{1}(\omega_{1}),&\widetilde{R}_{0}(\omega)\leq t<\widetilde{R}_{1}(\omega),\\
X_{t-\widetilde{R}_{1}(\omega)}^{2}(\omega_{2}),&\widetilde{R}_{1}(\omega)\leq t<\widetilde{R}_{2}(\omega),\\
X_{t-\widetilde{R}_{2}(\omega)}^{3}(\omega_{3}),&\widetilde{R}_{2}(\omega)\leq t<\widetilde{R}_{3}(\omega),\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
\Delta,&t\geq\widetilde{R}_{\infty}(\omega).\end{cases}

Here
\Delta
is the isolated, absorbing cemetery state attached to
\mathbb{R}_{*}
. We set
\mathbb{R}_{\Delta}:=\mathbb{R}_{*}\cup\{\Delta\}
. Of course,

\widetilde{R}_{\infty}=\inf\{t\geq 0:~X_{t}=\Delta\},

and
X_{t}=\Delta
for all
t\geq\widetilde{R}_{\infty}
.

For
t\in[0,\infty)
and
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\ldots)\in\Omega
, we define the
shift operator
as follows:

\displaystyle\theta_{t}(\omega)=\begin{cases}(\theta^{(1)}_{t}(\omega_{1}),\omega_{2},\omega_{3},\omega_{4},\ldots),&\widetilde{R}_{0}(\omega)\leq t<\widetilde{R}_{1}(\omega),\\
([\Delta^{1}],\theta^{(2)}_{t-\widetilde{R}_{1}(\omega)}(\omega_{2}),\omega_{3},\omega_{4},\ldots),&\widetilde{R}_{1}(\omega)\leq t<\widetilde{R}_{2}(\omega),\\
([\Delta^{1}],[\Delta^{2}],\theta^{(3)}_{t-\widetilde{R}_{2}(\omega)}(\omega_{3}),\omega_{4},\ldots),&\widetilde{R}_{2}(\omega)\leq t<\widetilde{R}_{3}(\omega),\\
\vdots&\vdots\end{cases}

We also let
X_{\infty}=\Delta
and
\theta_{\infty}\equiv[\Delta]
.

Further details of this construction, especially the construction of the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, and the family of measures
(\mathbb{P}_{x},x\in\mathbb{R}_{*})
can be found in Werner
[
71
]
(see also Ikeda et al.
[
43
]
, Meyer
[
53
]
and Sharpe
[
65
, Chapter II.14]
). We only note that the
law
of the concatenated process, i.e. a suitable family of measures
(\mathbb{P}_{x},x\in\mathbb{R}_{*})
on
(\Omega,\widetilde{\mathcal{F}})
, is defined in terms of the distributions
(\mathbb{P}_{x}^{(n)},x\in\mathbb{R}_{*})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and the transfer kernel
K
.

From
[
71
]
it follows that
X=(\Omega,\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0},(X_{t})_{t\geq 0},(\theta_{t})_{t\geq 0},(\mathbb{P}_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}_{*}})
, constructed above, is a right process on
\mathbb{R}_{*}
with the lifetime
\widetilde{R}_{\infty}
. In particular,
X
has the strong Markov property and for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}_{*}
and
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
,

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f(X_{\widetilde{R}_{n}})~\big|~\mathcal{F}_{{\widetilde{R}_{n}}^{-}}\big]=K^{n}f\circ\pi^{n},

where
\pi^{n}
denotes the projection of
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\ldots)
onto the
n
-th coordinate. Moreover, from (
2.1
), for
\omega\in\Omega
,
A\subset\mathbb{R}_{*}
and
f(x)=\ind_{A}(x)
, we have
5
5

5

Recall that
\mathcal{F}_{\tau^{-}}
is generated by
\mathcal{F}_{0^{+}}
and the class of sets
A\cap\{t<\tau\}
, where
A\in\mathcal{F}_{t}
,
t\geq 0
(see
[
25
, p. 16]
).

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\big[X_{\widetilde{R}_{n}}\in A~\big|~\mathcal{F}_{{\widetilde{R}_{n}}^{-}}\big](\omega)=K^{n}(\omega_{n},A)=k(X^{n}_{\zeta^{(n)}-}(\omega_{n}),A).

We call
X
the Servadei–Valdinoci process.

For the process
X
, we define a sequence of stopping times

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle:=\inf\{t\geq 0:\>X_{0}X_{t}<0\}=\tau_{D}+\tau_{\bar{D}^{c}},

\displaystyle R_{n+1}

\displaystyle:=R_{n}+R_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{n}},\quad n=1,2,....

Let
m(\omega)=\min\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\>\omega_{k}\neq[\Delta^{k}]\}
. From the construction,
R_{1}=\widetilde{R}_{m}
.
Hence, for all sets
A\subset\mathbb{R}_{*},T\subset[0,\infty),
by (
2.3
) we get

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[R_{1}\in T,X_{R_{1}}\in A\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[R_{1}\in T,X_{R_{1}}\in A,R_{1}=\widetilde{R}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}\left[\widetilde{R}_{k}\in T,X_{\widetilde{R}_{k}}\in A,R_{1}=\widetilde{R}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\ind_{A}(X_{\widetilde{R}_{k}})\big|~\mathcal{F}_{\widetilde{R}_{k}-}\right];\widetilde{R}_{k}\in T,R_{1}=\widetilde{R}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[k(X_{\widetilde{R}_{k}-},A),\widetilde{R}_{k}\in T,R_{1}=\widetilde{R}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[k(X_{R_{1}-},A),R_{1}\in T\right].

Of course we have
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}R_{n}=\widetilde{R}_{\infty}.
In what follows, we will use the times
R_{n}
and not
\widetilde{R}_{n}
.

2.2.
Transition semigroup of the process

Let

\displaystyle\widehat{\nu}(x,y)=\begin{cases}\nu(x,y),&\textrm{if }x\in D,~y\in D^{c}\textrm{ or }x\in D^{c},~y\in D,\\
0,&\textrm{otherwise.}\end{cases}

By the usual abuse of notation, we also use
\widehat{\nu}
to denote the corresponding kernel on
\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\nu}(x,A)=\int_{A}\widehat{\nu}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad x\in\mathbb{R},~A\subset\mathbb{R}.

Of course,
\widehat{\nu}(x,A)=0
if
x\in D
,
A\subset D
. Similarly,
\widehat{\nu}(x,A)=0
if
x\in D^{c}
,
A\subset D^{c}
.

With the kernel meaning of
\widehat{\nu}
, we consider the following perturbation series:
K_{0}:=I
and for
t>0
,

(2.5)

\displaystyle K_{t}

\displaystyle:=\widehat{P}_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-t_{1}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\widehat{P}_{t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t_{2}-t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-t_{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{2}\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}+\ldots:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}K_{t,n}.

Thus
K_{t,0}=\widehat{P}_{t}
and, for
n\geq 1
,

(2.6)

\displaystyle K_{t,n}:=\idotsint_{0<t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{n}<t}\widehat{P}_{t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t_{2}-t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\ldots\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-t_{n}}\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}t_{n}.

From Bogdan and Sydor
[
20
]
,
K_{t}
is a transition kernel. In what follows, we need a connection of
(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
to right processes, so we will not use
[
20
]
, but the reader interested in a purely analytic approach to
(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
may find
[
20
]
instructive. For the probabilistic proof of the fact that
K_{t}
,
t\geq 0
, is a transition kernel, see Lemma
2.10
. The deficiency of
[
20
]
for our goals is that the strong Markov property of the Markov process given by
(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is not addressed in
[
20
]
.

In what follows, in case of non-negative integrands, we often use Tonelli’s theorem without mention.

Lemma 2.3
.

For
t>0
,
n=0,1,\ldots
,

(2.7)

K_{t,n+1}=\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}K_{t-r,n}\,{\rm d}r=\int_{0}^{t}K_{r,n}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-r}\,{\rm d}r.

Proof.

By the substitution
u_{1}=t_{1}+r
,
u_{2}=t_{2}+r,\ldots,u_{n}=t_{n}+r
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}K_{t-r,n}\,{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{0}^{t-r}{\rm d}t_{1}\int_{t_{1}}^{t-r}{\rm d}t_{2}\ldots\int_{t_{n-1}}^{t-r}{\rm d}t_{n}\,\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t_{2}-t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\ldots\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-r-t_{n}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{r}^{t}{\rm d}u_{1}\int_{u_{1}}^{t}{\rm d}u_{2}\ldots\int_{u_{n-1}}^{t}{\rm d}u_{n}\,\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{u_{1}-r}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{u_{2}-u_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\ldots\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-u_{n}}

\displaystyle=\idotsint_{0<r<u_{1}<u_{2}<\ldots<u_{n-1}<u_{n}<t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{u_{1}-r}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{u_{2}-u_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\ldots\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-u_{n}}

\displaystyle=K_{t,n+1}.

The second equality in (
2.7
) arises similarly.
∎

By (
2.5
) and (
2.7
), we get the following perturbation formula for
K_{t}
.

Corollary 2.4
.

For
t>0
,

(2.8)

K_{t}=\widehat{P}_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}K_{t-r}\,{\rm d}r=\widehat{P}_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{r}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-r}\,{\rm d}r.

From the definition, for
x>0
,
t>0
,
K_{t,0}(x,\mathrm{d}y)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
with density
K_{t,0}(x,y)=p_{t}^{D}(x,y)
. In particular,
K_{t,0}(x,y)=K_{t,0}(y,x)
,
x,y>0
.

Lemma 2.5
.

For
t>0
,
f,g\in\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
,

(2.9)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}(K_{t}f)(x)g(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)(K_{t}g)(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}(K_{t}f)(x)g(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,f(y)g(x)K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,f(y)g(x)K_{t,n}(x,\mathrm{d}y).

It suffices to show that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

(2.10)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,f(y)g(x)K_{t,n}(x,\mathrm{d}y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,f(x)g(y)K_{t,n}(x,\mathrm{d}y).

We proceed by induction.
By the definition and symmetry of
p^{D}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,f(y)g(x)K_{t,0}(x,\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,f(y)g(x)p_{t}^{D}(x,y)+\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D^{c}}\,\delta_{x}(\mathrm{d}y)e^{-\nu(x,D)t}f(y)g(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,f(y)g(x)p_{t}^{D}(y,x)+\int_{D^{c}}f(x)g(x)e^{-\nu(x,D)t}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\,f(y)g(x)p_{t}^{D}(y,x)+\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\int_{\mathbb{R}}\delta_{y}(\mathrm{d}x)e^{-\nu(y,D)t}f(y)g(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}y\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)g(x)K_{t,0}(y,\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)g(y)K_{t,0}(x,\mathrm{d}y).

Put differently,

(2.11)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)g(x)\widehat{P}_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)g(y)\widehat{P}_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y).

Similarly, we prove that

(2.12)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)g(x)\widehat{\nu}(x,\mathrm{d}y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)g(y)\widehat{\nu}(x,\mathrm{d}y).

Assume that (
2.10
) holds for some
n\geq 0
. From Lemma
2.3
, (
2.11
) and (
2.12
),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)g(x)K_{t,n+1}(x,\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)g(x)K_{r,n}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-r}(x,\mathrm{d}y)=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,g(x)\big(\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-r}f\big)(y)K_{r,n}(x,\mathrm{d}y)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,g(y)\big(\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-r}f\big)(x)K_{r,n}(x,\mathrm{d}y)=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\,\big(\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-r}f\big)(x)\big(K_{r,n}g\big)(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}y\,\widehat{\nu}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\big(\widehat{P}_{t-r}f\big)(y)\big(K_{r,n}g\big)(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}y\,\widehat{\nu}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\big(\widehat{P}_{t-r}f\big)(x)\big(K_{r,n}g\big)(y)=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\,\big(\widehat{P}_{t-r}f\big)(x)\big(\widehat{\nu}K_{r,n}g\big)(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\,f(x)\big(\widehat{P}_{t-r}\widehat{\nu}K_{r,n}g\big)(x)=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\,f(x)\big(\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}K_{t-r,n}g\big)(x).

By Lemma
2.3
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)g(x)K_{t,n+1}(x,\mathrm{d}y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,f(x)g(y)K_{t,n+1}(x,\mathrm{d}y),

which completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 2.6
.

For
x\neq 0
,
t>0
and
f\in\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
, we have

(2.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}f(X_{t})=K_{t}f(x).

In order to prove Proposition
2.6
, we need the following auxiliary results.

For
n=1,2,\ldots
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
and Borel sets
T\subset[0,\infty)
and
A\subset\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, we define

P_{n}(x,T,A):=\mathbb{P}_{x}(R_{n}\in T,X_{R_{n}}\in A),

the (joint) distribution of the pair
(R_{n},X_{R_{n}})
under
\mathbb{P}_{x}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

For
x\neq 0
,
T\subset[0,\infty)
and
A\subset\mathbb{R}_{*}
,

\displaystyle P_{1}(x,T,A)

\displaystyle=\int_{T}{\rm d}s\int_{A}(\widehat{P}_{s}\widehat{\nu})(x,{\rm d}a),

\displaystyle P_{n+1}(x,T,A)

\displaystyle=\iint P_{n}(x,{\rm d}s,{\rm d}v)\int_{T}{\rm d}r\int_{A}(\widehat{P}_{r-s}\widehat{\nu})(v,{\rm d}a)\ind_{\{s<r\}},\quad n\geq 1.

Proof.

From
(
2.4
) we have

P_{1}(x,T,A)=\mathbb{P}_{x}(R_{1}\in T,X_{R_{1}}\in A)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\ind_{T}(R_{1}(\omega))k(X_{R_{1}-}(\omega),A)].

For
x>0
, from (
2.1
) and the Ikeda–Watanabe formula (
1.30
),

\displaystyle P_{1}(x,T,A)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\ind_{T}(R_{1})k(X_{R_{1}-},A)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}^{1}\big[\ind_{T}(\tau_{D})k(Z^{1}_{\tau_{D}-},A)\big]

\displaystyle=\int_{T}{\rm d}s\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,p_{s}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,D^{c})k(y,A)=\int_{T}{\rm d}s\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,p_{s}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,A\cap D^{c})

\displaystyle=\int_{T}{\rm d}s\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{A}{\rm d}a\,p_{s}^{D}(x,y)\widehat{\nu}(y,a)=\int_{T}{\rm d}s\int_{A}(\widehat{P}_{s}\widehat{\nu})(x,{\rm d}a).

Similarly, for
x<0
,

\displaystyle P_{1}(x,T,A)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\ind_{T}(R_{1})k(X_{R_{1}-},A)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}^{2}\big[\ind_{T}(\zeta_{2}^{Z})k(Z^{2}_{\zeta_{2}^{Z}-},A)\big]

\displaystyle=k(x,A)\mathbb{P}_{x}^{2}\big[\zeta_{2}^{Z}\in T\big]=\int_{T}{\rm d}s\int_{A}{\rm d}a\,e^{-\nu(x,D)s}\widehat{\nu}(x,a)

\displaystyle=\int_{T}{\rm d}s\int_{A}{\rm d}a\,(\widehat{P}_{s}\widehat{\nu})(x,{\rm d}a).

Furthermore, for
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle P_{n+1}(x,T,A)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{x}(R_{n+1}\in T,X_{R_{n+1}}\in A)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\ind_{T}(R_{n}+R_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{n}})\ind_{A}(X_{R_{n+1}})\big|\mathcal{F}_{R_{n}}\big](\omega)\mathbb{P}_{x}(\mathrm{d}\,\omega).

Let
G(\omega,\omega^{\prime}):=\ind_{T}(R_{n}(\omega)+R_{1}(\omega^{\prime}))\ind_{A}(X_{R_{1}(\omega^{\prime})}(\omega^{\prime}))
,
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega
.
For
\omega^{\prime}:=\theta_{R_{n}}\omega
, we have

X_{{R_{1}}(\omega^{\prime})}(\omega^{\prime})=X_{R_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{n}}(\omega)}(\theta_{R_{n}}\omega)=X_{R_{n+1}(\omega)}(\omega).

Moreover, for
H(\omega):=G(\omega,\theta_{R_{n}}\omega)
we have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}[H|\mathcal{F}_{R_{n}}](\omega)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\ind_{T}(R_{n}+R_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{n}})\ind_{A}(X_{R_{n+1}})\big|\mathcal{F}_{R_{n}}\big](\omega).

Hence,

P_{n+1}(x,T,A)=\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{E}_{x}[H|\mathcal{F}_{R_{n}}](\omega)\mathbb{P}_{x}(\mathrm{d}\,\omega).

From the strong Markov property
[
9
, Exercise 8.16]
, we obtain that

\displaystyle P_{n+1}(x,T,A)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}G(\omega,\omega^{\prime})\mathbb{P}_{X_{R_{n}}(\omega)}(\mathrm{d}\,\omega^{\prime})\mathbb{P}_{x}(\mathrm{d}\,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}\ind_{T}(R_{n}(\omega)+R_{1}(\omega^{\prime}))\ind_{A}(X_{R_{1}(\omega^{\prime})}(\omega^{\prime}))\mathbb{P}_{X_{R_{n}}(\omega)}(\mathrm{d}\,\omega^{\prime})\mathbb{P}_{x}(\mathrm{d}\,\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{E}_{X_{R_{n}}(\omega)}\big[\ind_{T}(R_{n}(\omega)+R_{1})\ind_{A}(X_{R_{1}})\big]\mathbb{P}_{x}(\mathrm{d}\,\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{E}_{X_{R_{n}}}\big[\ind_{T}(s+R_{1})\ind_{A}(X_{R_{1}})\big]\big|_{s=R_{n}}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{P}_{X_{R_{n}}}\big(R_{1}+s\in T,X_{R_{1}}\in A\big)\big|_{s=R_{n}}\big]

\displaystyle=\iint P_{n}(x,{\rm d}s,{\rm d}v)\mathbb{P}_{v}(R_{1}\in(T-s)\cap(0,\infty),X_{R_{1}}\in A).

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{v}(R_{1}\in(T-s)\cap(0,\infty),X_{R_{1}}\in A)

\displaystyle=P_{1}(v,(T-s)\cap(0,\infty),A)

\displaystyle=\int_{(T-s)\cap(0,\infty)}{\rm d}r\int_{A}(\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu})(v,{\rm d}a)

\displaystyle=\int_{T}{\rm d}r\int_{A}(\widehat{P}_{r-s}\widehat{\nu})(v,{\rm d}a)\ind_{\{s<r\}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle P_{n+1}(x,T,A)

\displaystyle=\iint P_{n}(x,{\rm d}s,{\rm d}v)\int_{T}{\rm d}r\int_{A}(\widehat{P}_{r-s}\widehat{\nu})(v,{\rm d}a)\ind_{\{s<r\}},

which is the desired conclusion.
∎

Remark 2.8
.

By induction,
P_{n}(x,(0,\infty),\mathbb{R}_{*})=1
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}_{*}
. In particular,
R_{n}>0
a.s.

Lemma 2.9
.

For
x\neq 0
and
n\geq 2
,

(2.14)

\displaystyle P_{n}(x,{\rm d}r,{\rm d}a)=\idotsint\limits_{0<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n-1}<r}(\widehat{P}_{t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t_{2}-t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\ldots\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{r-t_{n-1}}\widehat{\nu})(x,{\rm d}a)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}t_{n-1}\,{\rm d}r.

Proof.

From Lemma
2.7
we have

\displaystyle P_{2}(x,{\rm d}r,{\rm d}a)

\displaystyle=\iint P_{1}(x,{\rm d}s,{\rm d}v)(\widehat{P}_{r-s}\widehat{\nu})(v,{\rm d}a)\ind_{\{s<r\}}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\iint(\widehat{P}_{s}\widehat{\nu})(x,{\rm d}v)\,{\rm d}s(\widehat{P}_{r-s}\widehat{\nu})(v,{\rm d}a)\ind_{\{s<r\}}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{r}(\widehat{P}_{s}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{r-s}\widehat{\nu})(x,{\rm d}a)\,{\rm d}s\,{\rm d}r.

For
n>2
, we use induction. Assume that the equality (
2.14
) holds for some
n\geq 2
. From Lemma
2.7
we then have

\displaystyle P_{n+1}(x,{\rm d}r,{\rm d}a)

\displaystyle=\iint P_{n}(x,{\rm d}s,{\rm d}v)(\widehat{P}_{r-s}\widehat{\nu})(v,{\rm d}a)\ind_{\{s<r\}}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{r}{\rm d}s\idotsint\limits_{0<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n-1}<s}\int(\widehat{P}_{t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t_{2}-t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\ldots\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{s-t_{n-1}}\widehat{\nu})(x,{\rm d}v)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}t_{n-1}(\widehat{P}_{r-s}\widehat{\nu})(v,{\rm d}a){\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{r}{\rm d}s\idotsint\limits_{0<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n-1}<s}(\widehat{P}_{t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t_{2}-t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\ldots\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{s-t_{n-1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{r-s}\widehat{\nu})(x,{\rm d}a)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}t_{n-1}{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\idotsint\limits_{0<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n}<r}(\widehat{P}_{t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t_{2}-t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\ldots\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t_{n}-t_{n-1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{r-t_{n}}\widehat{\nu})(x,{\rm d}a)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}t_{n}{\rm d}r,

which ends the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.6
.

Let
x\neq 0
,
t>0
and
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}f(X_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}[f(X_{t}),0\leq t<R_{1}]+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}[f(X_{t}),R_{n}\leq t<R_{n+1}].

Obviously, for
x>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}[f(X_{t}),0\leq t<R_{1}]=\mathbb{E}_{x}^{1}[f(Z_{t}^{1}),0\leq t<\tau_{D}]=\int_{D}f(y)p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\widehat{P}_{t}f(x).

Similarly, for
x<0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}[f(X_{t}),0\leq t<R_{1}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}^{2}[f(Z_{t}^{2}),0\leq t<\zeta_{2}^{Z}]=\int_{D^{c}}\delta_{x}(\mathrm{d}y)e^{-\nu(x,D)t}f(y)

\displaystyle=f(x)e^{-\nu(x,D)t}=\widehat{P}_{t}f(x).

Now, let
I_{n}(t,x):=\mathbb{E}_{x}[f(X_{t}),R_{n}\leq t<R_{n+1}]
,
n\geq 1
. We will show that
I_{n}(t,x)=K_{t,n}f(x)
.
Indeed, using the same method as in the proof of Lemma
2.7
, in particular using
[
9
, Exercise 8.16]
, we get

\displaystyle I_{n}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{E}_{x}[f(X_{t}),R_{n}\leq t<R_{n+1}\big|\mathcal{F}_{R_{n}}]\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{E}_{x}[f(X_{t-R_{n}}\circ\theta_{R_{n}}),t<R_{n}+R_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{n}}\big|\mathcal{F}_{R_{n}}],R_{n}\leq t\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{E}_{X_{R_{n}}}[f(X_{t-s},t-s<R_{1}]\big|_{s=R_{n}},R_{n}\leq t\big]

\displaystyle=\iint P_{n}(x,{\rm d}s,{\rm d}v)\mathbb{E}_{v}[f(X_{t-s}),t-s<R_{1}]\ind_{\{s\leq t\}}

(2.15)

\displaystyle=\iint P_{n}(x,{\rm d}s,{\rm d}v)\widehat{P}_{t-s}f(v)\ind_{\{s\leq t\}}.

From Lemma
2.7
and (
2.6
),

\displaystyle I_{1}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int(\widehat{P}_{s}\widehat{\nu})(x,{\rm d}v)\widehat{P}_{t-s}f(v)=\int_{0}^{t}(\widehat{P}_{s}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-s}f)(x)\,{\rm d}s=K_{t,1}f(x).

It remains to show our claim for
n\geq 2
. From (
2.15
), Lemma
2.9
and (
2.6
) it is easy to verify that

\displaystyle I_{n}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\iint P_{n}(x,{\rm d}s,{\rm d}v)\widehat{P}_{t-s}f(v)\ind_{\{s\leq t\}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\idotsint\limits_{0<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n-1}<s}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}t_{n-1}\int(\widehat{P}_{t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t_{2}-t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\ldots\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{s-t_{n-1}}\widehat{\nu})(x,{\rm d}v)\widehat{P}_{t-s}f(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\idotsint\limits_{0<t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n-1}<s}\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}t_{n-1}(\widehat{P}_{t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t_{2}-t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\ldots\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{s-t_{n-1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-s}f)(x)

\displaystyle=\idotsint\limits_{0<t_{1}<t_{2}<\ldots<t_{n}<t}(\widehat{P}_{t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t_{2}-t_{1}}\widehat{\nu}\ldots\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-t_{n}}f)(x)\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}t_{n}=K_{t,n}f(x),

which completes the proof in case of
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
.
For
f\in\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
, we consider
f_{n}:=f\wedge n
and
using the first part of the proof we obtain
\mathbb{E}_{x}f_{n}(X_{t})=K_{t}f_{n}(x).

Then the desired equality follows from the monotone convergence.
∎

Lemma 2.10
.

The family
(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a semigroup of subprobability transition kernels on
\mathbb{R}_{*}
.

Proof.

At first, we will prove the subprobability property. It suffices to show (by induction) that for any
N=0,1,2,\ldots
and
t>0
,
S_{N}(x,t):=\sum_{n=0}^{N}K_{t,n}\mathbf{1}(x)\leq 1
,
x\in\mathbb{R}_{*}
. Here
\mathbf{1}(x)=1
,
x\in\mathbb{R}_{*}
.
For
x>0
, from (
1.24
) it follows that for any
t>0
,
S_{0}(x,t)=K_{t,0}\mathbf{1}(x)=p_{t}^{D}(x,D)=\mathbb{P}^{Y}_{x}(\tau_{D}>t)\leq 1
. For
x<0
, it is obvious that for any
t>0
we have
S_{0}(x,t)=K_{t,0}\mathbf{1}(x)=e^{-\nu(x,D)t}\leq 1
. Hence,
S_{0}(x,t)\leq 1
for
x\neq 0
,
t>0
.

Assume that for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
and all
t>0
,
x\neq 0
we have
S_{N}(x,t)\leq 1
. Then, from Lemma
2.3
,

\displaystyle S_{N+1}(x,t)=\widehat{P}_{t}\mathbf{1}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}S_{N}(x,t-r)\,{\rm d}r\leq\widehat{P}_{t}\mathbf{1}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}\mathbf{1}(x)\,{\rm d}r.

For
x>0
, from (
1.31
) and (
1.30
), we have

\displaystyle S_{N+1}(x,t)\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}(\tau_{D}>t)+\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}(\tau_{D}\leq t)=1.

In case
x<0
, we have

\displaystyle S_{N+1}(x,t)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\nu(x,D)t}+\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,e^{-\nu(x,D)s}\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{\infty}\nu(x,D)e^{-\nu(x,D)s}{\rm d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\nu(x,D)e^{-\nu(x,D)s}{\rm d}s=1,

which ends the induction.

The fact that the transition kernels
(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfy the Chapman–Kolmogorov equation follows directly from Proposition
2.6
. Indeed, from the Markov property and from mentioned theorem we have the following Chapman–Kolmogorov equation for
K_{t}
: for
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}^{+}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
,
s,t>0
and
x\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle K_{t+s}f(x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}f(X_{t+s})=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{E}_{x}\big(f(X_{s})\circ\theta_{t}~|~\mathcal{F}_{t}\big)\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{E}_{X_{t}}f(X_{s})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[K_{s}f(X_{t})\big]=K_{t}K_{s}f(x).

Further, for
f=\ind_{A}
,
A\subset\mathbb{R}_{*}
, we get the equality

(2.16)

\displaystyle K_{t+s}(x,A)=K_{t+s}\ind_{A}(x)=K_{t}K_{s}\ind_{A}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)K_{s}(y,A),

which proves the lemma.
∎

We note that the first part of the above proof is similar to proof of
[
17
, Lemma 3.3]
. For a direct proof of (
2.16
), see

[
20
]
.

The following result is an extension of Proposition
2.6
.

Corollary 2.11
.

For
x\neq 0
,
t>0
, and
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{x}f(X_{t})=K_{t}f(x)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.6
and Lemma
2.10
, both sides are equal and finite for
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}^{+}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
. The result follows by considering
f=f_{+}-f_{-}
.
∎

Lemma 2.12
.

For
u\in\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathbb{R})
,

(K_{t}u)^{2}(x)\leq(K_{t}u^{2})(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}_{*},~t>0,

and

\int_{\mathbb{R}}(K_{t}u)^{2}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Proof.

From the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and Lemma
2.10
, we get

\displaystyle(K_{t}u)^{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(y)K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\Big]^{2}\leq\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}(y)K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\Big]\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}(y)K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)=(K_{t}u^{2})(x).

Moreover, from Lemma
2.5
and Lemma
2.10
we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}(K_{t}u)^{2}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}(K_{t}u^{2})(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}(x)K_{t}\mathbf{1}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,

which completes the proof.
∎

From the above lemma it follows that
K_{t}u(x)
is finite for a.e.
x
if
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and

(2.17)

\displaystyle\left\lVert K_{t}u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\left\lVert K_{t}|u|\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

The next corollary follows from Lemma
2.5
.

Corollary 2.13
.

For
t>0
and

f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}(K_{t}f)(x)g(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)(K_{t}g)(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Proposition 2.14
.

(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a strongly continuous semigroup on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, i.e. for
t,s\geq 0
,
K_{t+s}u=K_{t}K_{s}u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\left\lVert K_{t}u-u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=0,\qquad u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Proof.

Let
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
u_{n}:=u\wedge n\vee(-n)
.
Of course,
\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Then from Lemma
2.10
, for
x\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle K_{t+s}u_{n}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t+s}(x,\mathrm{d}y)u_{n}(y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}(K_{t}K_{s})(x,\mathrm{d}y)u_{n}(y)=K_{t}K_{s}u_{n}(x).

From (
2.17
), we know that
K_{t+s}
,
K_{t}
and
K_{s}
are bounded, hence continuous, operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Therefore,
\left\lVert K_{t+s}u_{n}-K_{t+s}u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\to 0
and
\left\lVert K_{t}K_{s}u_{n}-K_{t}K_{s}u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. As a result
K_{t+s}u=K_{t}K_{s}u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Now we will prove the strong continuity.
Since the family
(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
forms a contraction semigroup of operators, then from Proposition 1.3 in Engel and Nagel
[
35
, Section 1]
it suffices to show a desired convergence for
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
, because
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
is a dense subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

We first show that for
u\in\mathcal{B}^{+}(\mathbb{R})\cap C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
and
x\neq 0
,

(2.18)

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}K_{t}u(x)=u(x).

Let
t\to 0^{+}
. From Lemma
1.5
,

\displaystyle K_{t,0}u(x)=\mathbf{1}_{D}(x)\int_{D}p_{t}^{D}(x,\mathrm{d}y)u(y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\mathbf{1}_{D^{c}}(x)u(x)e^{-\nu(x,D)t}\to u(x).

Furthermore, from Corollary
2.4
and Lemma
2.10
,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{t,n}u(x)\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}\mathbf{1}(x)\,{\rm d}r.

Then for
x>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}\mathbf{1}(x)\,{\rm d}r=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,p_{r}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)=\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}(\tau_{D}\leq t)\to 0.

Similarly, for
x<0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}\mathbf{1}(x)\,{\rm d}r=\int_{0}^{t}\nu(x,D)e^{-\nu(x,D)r}\,{\rm d}r\to 0.

Let
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
be arbitrary. We get

K_{t}u(x)=K_{t}u_{+}(x)-K_{t}u_{-}(x)\to u_{+}(x)-u_{-}(x)=u(x),

as
t\to 0^{+}
. Then, by (
2.17
),

\displaystyle\left\lVert K_{t}u-u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert K_{t}u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}-2\langle K_{t}u,u\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 2\big[\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}-\langle K_{t}u,u\rangle\big]=2\langle u-K_{t}u,u\rangle

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}\,\cap\,\supp{u}}\big[u(x)-K_{t}u(x)\big]u(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\to 0,

by the dominated convergence theorem.
∎

Lemma 2.15
.

For
t>0
,
x\neq 0
,
k>0
,
A\subset\mathbb{R}
,

(a)

K_{t,n}(kx,kA)=K_{tk^{-\alpha},n}(x,A)
,
n=0,1,2,\ldots
,

(b)

K_{t}(kx,kA)=K_{tk^{-\alpha}}(x,A)
.

Proof.

It suffices to show the equality (a). For
n=0
, from (
1.21
), it follows that for
x>0
,

\displaystyle K_{t,0}(kx,kA)

\displaystyle=\int_{kA\cap D}p_{t}^{D}(kx,y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\int_{kA\cap D}k^{-1}p_{tk^{-\alpha}}^{D}(x,y/k)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{A\cap D}p_{tk^{-\alpha}}^{D}(x,z)\,{\rm d}z=K_{tk^{-\alpha},0}(x,A).

Similarly, for
x<0
, from (
1.16
),

K_{t,0}(kx,kA)=\delta_{kx}(kA)e^{-\nu(kx,D)t}=\delta_{x}(A)e^{-\nu(x,D)tk^{-\alpha}}=K_{tk^{-\alpha},0}(x,A).

Assume that for some
n\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
we have
K_{t,n}(kx,kA)=K_{tk^{-\alpha},n}(x,A)
,
x\neq 0
,
t>0
,
k>0
.

Let
x>0
. From Lemma
2.3
, (
1.21
) and (
1.15
), we have the following equality

\displaystyle K_{t,n+1}(kx,kA)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,p^{D}_{r}(kx,y)\nu(y,z)K_{t-r,n}(z,kA)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,k^{-1}p^{D}_{rk^{-\alpha}}(x,y/k)k^{-\alpha-1}\nu(y/k,z/k)K_{t-r,n}(z,kA).

Using the substitution
s=rk^{-\alpha}
,
w=y/k
and
v=z/k
we get

\displaystyle K_{t,n+1}(kx,kA)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{tk^{-\alpha}}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}w\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}v\,p^{D}_{s}(x,w)\nu(w,v)K_{t-sk^{\alpha},n}(kv,kA),

so

\displaystyle K_{t,n+1}(kx,kA)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{tk^{-\alpha}}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}w\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}v\,p^{D}_{s}(x,w)\nu(w,v)K_{tk^{-\alpha}-s,n}(v,A)

\displaystyle=K_{tk^{-\alpha},n+1}(x,A).

Now, consider the case
x<0
. Again, from Lemma
2.3
, (
1.15
) and (
1.16
), we get

\displaystyle K_{t,n+1}(kx,kA)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,e^{-\nu(kx,D)r}\nu(kx,z)K_{t-r,n}(z,kA)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,e^{-\nu(x,D)rk^{-\alpha}}k^{-\alpha-1}\nu(x,z/k)K_{t-r,n}(z,kA).

Using the substitution
s=rk^{-\alpha}
and
w=z/k
we get

\displaystyle K_{t,n+1}(kx,kA)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{tk^{-\alpha}}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}w\,e^{-\nu(x,D)s}\nu(x,w)K_{t-sk^{\alpha},n}(kw,kA),

so

\displaystyle K_{t,n+1}(kx,kA)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{tk^{-\alpha}}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}w\,e^{-\nu(x,D)s}\nu(x,w)K_{tk^{-\alpha}-s,n}(w,A)=K_{tk^{-\alpha},n+1}(x,A),

which yields our claim.
∎

Proposition 2.16
.

For every
t>0
and
f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
, we have
K_{t}f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
.

Proof.

Let
f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
and
t>0
. The boundedness of
K_{t}f
is asserted in Lemma
2.10
. Therefore, it suffices to show that
K_{t}f
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}_{*}
.

Assume that
x>0
. Then
x\mapsto\widehat{P}_{t}f(x)=P_{t}^{D}f(x)
is a continuous function on
D
, which follows directly from Lemma
1.4
. From Corollary
2.4
it suffices to show a continuity of the function

\displaystyle D\ni x\mapsto F_{t}(x):=\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{s}\widehat{\nu}K_{t-s}f(x)\,{\rm d}s=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}\,{\rm d}z\,p_{s}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)K_{t-s}f(z).

From Lemma
2.10
, for
0\leq s\leq t
,
y\in D
and
z\in D^{c}
,

(2.19)

\displaystyle p_{s}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)K_{t-s}f(z)\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}p_{s}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z).

Moreover, from Ikeda–Watanabe formula (
1.30
) and (
1.31
),

\displaystyle G_{t}(x):=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}\,{\rm d}z\,p_{s}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)=1-p_{t}^{D}(x,D)=1-P_{t}^{D}\mathbf{1}(x).

By Lemma
1.4
, the function
D\ni x\mapsto G_{t}(x)
is continuous. From the Vitali’s theorem (see
[
61
, Theorem 16.6]
) it follows that the function
p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)
is uniformly integrable in
x
with respect to the measure
\ind_{[0,t]}(s)\ind_{D}(y)\ind_{D^{c}}(z)\,{\rm d}s\,\mathrm{d}y\,{\rm d}z
(see
[
61
, Definition 16.1]
). By (
2.19
), the functions
p_{s}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)K_{t-s}f(z)
are also locally in
x
uniformly integrable. Since the function
D\ni x\mapsto p_{s}^{D}(x,y)
is continuous, from the (direct proof of) Vitali’s theorem, the function
D\ni x\mapsto F_{t}(x)
is continuous.

Let
x<0
. Of course,
\overline{D}^{c}\ni x\mapsto\widehat{P}_{t}f(x)=f(x)e^{-\nu(x,D)t}
is a continuous function. We will verify the continuity of the function

\overline{D}^{c}\ni x\mapsto F_{t}(x):=\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{s}\widehat{\nu}K_{t-s}f(x)\,{\rm d}s=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,e^{-\nu(x,D)s}\nu(x,y)K_{t-s}f(y).

Again from Lemma
2.10
,
K_{t-s}f(y)\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}
and
the function

\overline{D}^{c}\ni x\mapsto\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\nu(x,D)e^{-\nu(x,D)s}\,{\rm d}s=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\big[1-e^{-\nu(x,D)t}\big]

is continuous. Hence, from Vitali’s theorem, the functions
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}e^{-\nu(x,D)s}\nu(x,y)
are locally uniformly in
x
integrable with respect to the measure
\ind_{[0,t]}(s)\ind_{D}(y)\,{\rm d}s\,\mathrm{d}y
and so are the functions
e^{-\nu(x,D)s}\nu(x,y)K_{t-s}f(y)
. By the (direct proof of) Vitali’s theorem, we get the continuity of the function
\overline{D}^{c}\ni x\mapsto F_{t}(x)
.
∎

By the above proof,
K_{t}f\in C_{b}(D)
for all
f\in\mathcal{B}_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
.

In passing, let us point out an alternative approach to concatenation by Bogdan and Kunze
[
18
]
, where the process is constructed by means of the semigroup, see also Bogdan and Kunze
[
17
]
and Kim et al.
[
48
,
49
]
.

2.3.
Excessive functions

For
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, we define function
h_{\beta}(x)=|x|^{\beta}
. We first observe that
h_{\alpha-1}
is
supermedian
for
\widehat{P}_{t}
.

Lemma 2.17
.

For
x\neq 0
and
t>0
,
\widehat{P}_{t}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\leq h_{\alpha-1}(x)
.

Proof.

Recall that
\alpha\in(0,2)
. Let
\gamma\in(0,1)
and
\delta\in(\gamma+\alpha/2,1+\alpha/2)
. Of course, the interval is nonempty. Let

g(x):=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,p_{t}^{D}(x,y)f(t)y^{-\delta},\quad x>0,

where
f(t):=\mathcal{C}^{-1}t^{(-\alpha/2-\gamma+\delta)/\alpha}
and

\mathcal{C}=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,p_{t}^{D}(1,y)t^{(-\alpha/2-\gamma+\delta)/\alpha}y^{-\delta}.

By the proof of
[
45
, Lemma 3.1]
,
\mathcal{C}<\infty
. From
[
45
, Lemma 3.4]
, we have
g(x)=x^{\alpha/2-\gamma}
, so by taking
\gamma=1-\alpha/2\in(0,1)
, we get
g(x)=h_{\alpha-1}(x)
,
x>0
.
Moreover, for
x>0
, we have

\begin{split}\widehat{P}_{t}h_{\alpha-1}(x)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}p_{t}^{D}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}s\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}z\,f(s)p_{s}^{D}(y,z)z^{-\delta}\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}s\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}z\,f(s)p_{t+s}^{D}(x,z)z^{-\delta}\\
&=\int_{t}^{\infty}{\rm d}u\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}z\,f(u-t)p_{u}^{D}(x,z)z^{-\delta}\\
&\leq h_{\alpha-1}(x),\end{split}

because
f
is increasing.

For
x<0
,

\widehat{P}_{t}h_{\alpha-1}(x)=e^{-\nu(x,D)t}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\leq h_{\alpha-1}(x).\qed

Lemma 2.18
.

For
x\neq 0
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\,{\rm d}r=h_{\alpha-1}(x).

Proof.

Let
x>0
. By (
1.32
),

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\,{\rm d}r=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}r\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}a\int_{-\infty}^{0}\mathrm{d}y\,p_{r}^{D}(x,a)\nu(a,y)h_{\alpha-1}(y)\\
&=\int_{-\infty}^{0}P_{D}(x,y)h_{\alpha-1}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y.\end{split}

From (
1.34
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\,{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(\pi\alpha/2)}{\pi}x^{\alpha/2}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{|y|^{\alpha/2-1}}{|x-y|}\,\mathrm{d}y=\frac{\sin(\pi\alpha/2)}{\pi}x^{\alpha/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{y^{\alpha/2-1}}{x+y}\,\mathrm{d}y.

By changing variables
y=xz
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\,{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(\pi\alpha/2)}{\pi}x^{\alpha-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{\alpha/2-1}}{1+z}{\rm d}z=\frac{\sin(\pi\alpha/2)}{\pi}x^{\alpha-1}\mathfrak{B}(\alpha/2,1-\alpha/2)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(\pi\alpha/2)}{\pi}x^{\alpha-1}\Gamma(\alpha/2)\Gamma(1-\alpha/2)=h_{\alpha-1}(x).

Let
x<0
. Then from (
1.17
),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\,{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\nu(x,D)r}\,{\rm d}r\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(x,y)y^{\alpha-1}\,\mathrm{d}y=\frac{1}{\nu(x,D)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(x,y)y^{\alpha-1}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\alpha|x|^{\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{y^{\alpha-1}}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y.

Using the substitution
y=|x|z
, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\,{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\alpha|x|^{\alpha-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{\alpha-1}}{(z+1)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}z=\alpha|x|^{\alpha-1}\mathfrak{B}(\alpha,1)=h_{\alpha-1}(x),

which is our claim.
∎

From Lemma
2.7
and Lemma
2.18
, it follows that
\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[h_{\alpha-1}(X_{R_{1}})\big]=h_{\alpha-1}(x)
.

The next result is an analogue of Lemma
2.10
, which asserts that
K_{t}\mathbf{1}\leq 1
on
\mathbb{R}_{*}
.

Proposition 2.19
.

For
\alpha\in(0,2)
, the function
h_{\alpha-1}(x)=|x|^{\alpha-1}
is supermedian for
K_{t}
, i.e.

K_{t}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\leq h_{\alpha-1}(x),\qquad t>0,~x\neq 0.

Proof.

We prove by induction that for
N=0,1,2,\ldots
and
t>0
,

(2.20)

S_{N}(x,t):=\sum_{n=0}^{N}K_{t,n}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\leq h_{\alpha-1}(x),\quad x\neq 0.

Let
N=0
. From Lemma
2.17
, it follows that

\begin{split}S_{0}(x,t)=\widehat{P}_{t}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\leq h_{\alpha-1}(x),\quad x\neq 0.\end{split}

Furthermore, using Lemma
2.18
, we obtain for
x\neq 0
,

(2.21)

\displaystyle\widehat{P}_{t}h_{\alpha-1}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{P}_{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\,{\rm d}r=\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{P}_{t+r}\widehat{\nu}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\,{\rm d}r=\int_{t}^{\infty}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\,{\rm d}r.

Assume that for some
N\in\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
and all
t>0
we have
S_{N}(x,t)\leq h_{\alpha-1}(x)
. We will show that
S_{N+1}(x,t)\leq h_{\alpha-1}(x)
. Using (
2.7
), (
2.21
) and Lemma
2.18
, we obtain

\displaystyle S_{N+1}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\widehat{P}_{t}h_{\alpha-1}(x)+\sum_{n=0}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}K_{t-r,n}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\,{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{\infty}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\,{\rm d}r+\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}S_{N}(x,t-r)\,{\rm d}r

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t}^{\infty}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\,{\rm d}r+\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\,{\rm d}r=h_{\alpha-1}(x).

Then from the fact that the sequence
(S_{N}(x,t))_{N\geq 0}
is non-decreasing and bounded, it follows that

K_{t}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\leq h_{\alpha-1}(x),\quad x>0.\qed

Corollary 2.20
.

If
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
\beta(\alpha-\beta-1)\geq 0
and
\lambda\in[0,\infty)
, then
h_{\beta}
is
\lambda
-excessive for
K_{t}
.

Proof.

The supermedian property in the case
\beta=0
and
\beta=\alpha-1
follows directly from Lemma
2.10
and Proposition
2.19
. Hence, it suffices to prove it for
\beta\neq 0
and
\beta\neq\alpha-1
.

Let
\gamma:=(\alpha-1)/\beta
. Of course
\gamma>1
and from Jensen’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle K_{t}h_{\beta}^{\gamma}(x)

\displaystyle=K_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})\int_{\mathbb{R}}h_{\beta}^{\gamma}(y)\frac{K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)}{K_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})}\geq K_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}}h_{\beta}(y)\frac{K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)}{K_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})}\Big]^{\gamma}

(2.22)

\displaystyle=\big[K_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})\big]^{1-\gamma}\big[K_{t}h_{\beta}(x)\big]^{\gamma}\geq\big[K_{t}h_{\beta}(x)\big]^{\gamma}.

Hence, from (
2.22
) and from Proposition
2.19
,

K_{t}h_{\beta}(x)\leq\big[K_{t}h_{\beta}^{\gamma}(x)\big]^{1/\gamma}=\big[K_{t}h_{\alpha-1}(x)\big]^{1/\gamma}\leq h_{\alpha-1}^{1/\gamma}(x)=h_{\beta}(x).

Let
x\neq 0
and
