\varphi\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
be such that
0\leq\varphi\leq h_{\beta}
and
\varphi(x)=h_{\beta}(x)
. By Lemma
2.2
and the first part of the proof, we have

h_{\beta}(x)=\varphi(x)=\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}\widehat{P}_{t}\varphi(x)\leq\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}\widehat{P}_{t}h_{\beta}(x)\leq\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}\widehat{P}_{t}h_{\beta}(x)\leq\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}K_{t}h_{\beta}(x)\leq h_{\beta}(x).

Hence
\lim\limits_{t\to 0^{+}}\widehat{P}_{t}h_{\beta}(x)=h_{\beta}(x)
. Furthermore,

\widehat{P}_{t}h_{\beta}(x)\leq K_{t}h_{\beta}(x)\leq h_{\beta}(x),

so
\lim\limits_{t\to 0^{+}}K_{t}h_{\beta}(x)=h_{\beta}(x)
.
∎

Note that the assumption
\alpha\in(0,2)
and
\beta(\alpha-\beta-1)\geq 0
in the previous corollary is equivalent to the following:
\beta\in[\alpha-1,0]
for
\alpha\in(0,1]
, and
\beta\in[0,\alpha-1]
for
\alpha\in[1,2)
.

3.
The lifetime of the process

In this section we study the lifetime of the process
X=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. We consider three cases. In the case
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we prove that the lifetime of the process
X
is infinite a.s. and
|X_{t}|\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
. For
\alpha\in(1,2)
, we show that the process
X
is absorbed by the origin in finite time. In the case
\alpha=1
, we prove that the lifetime of
X
is infinite and the limit of
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}X_{t}
does not exist. The precise statement of this fact is given in Section
3.4
. At the end of this section, we use the absorption result to prove the Feller property of the semigroup
K
for
\alpha>1
.

3.1.
The position of the first return
D

Let
W=X_{R_{2}}
. It is the first return position to
D=(0,\infty)
for the process
X=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
starting from
x>0
. By Lemma
2.9
,

\displaystyle R(x,w):=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}t\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}y\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\rm d}z\,p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)\frac{\nu(z,w)}{\nu(z,D)},\quad w\in D,

is the density function of
W
. By (
1.28
) and (
1.32
),

\displaystyle R(x,w)=\int_{-\infty}^{0}P_{D}(x,z)\frac{\nu(z,w)}{\nu(z,D)}\,{\rm d}z.

By changing variables
z=xs
, (
1.33
) and (
1.16
), we get

\displaystyle R(x,w)

\displaystyle=x\int_{-\infty}^{0}P_{D}(x,xs)\frac{\nu(xs,w)}{\nu(xs,D)}\,{\rm d}s=x^{1-1-\alpha-1+\alpha}\int_{-\infty}^{0}P_{D}(1,s)\frac{\nu(s,w/x)}{\nu(s,D)}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=x^{-1}R(1,w/x).

Therefore,
\mathbb{P}_{x}(W\in{\rm d}w)=\mathbb{P}_{1}(xW\in{\rm d}w)
.

Lemma 3.1
.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
,

\displaystyle\rho:=\mathbb{E}_{1}W^{(\alpha-1)/2}<1.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in(0,2)
. Using (
1.34
) and (
1.17
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}W^{(\alpha-1)/2}

\displaystyle=C\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}w\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\rm d}z\,|z|^{-\alpha/2}|1-z|^{-1}|z-w|^{-1-\alpha}|z|^{\alpha}w^{(\alpha-1)/2},

where
C=\pi^{-1}\alpha\sin(\pi\alpha/2)
. By Tonelli’s theorem and by changing variables
y=-z\in(0,\infty)
,
w=yv
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}W^{(\alpha-1)/2}

\displaystyle=C\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}y\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}v\,y^{1-\alpha/2-1-\alpha+\alpha+\alpha/2-1/2}(1+y)^{-1}(v+1)^{-1-\alpha}v^{(\alpha-1)/2}

\displaystyle=C\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{y}(1+y)}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{v^{(\alpha-1)/2}}{(v+1)^{1+\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\sin\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\cdot\pi\cdot B\big(\alpha/2+1/2,\alpha/2+1/2)

\displaystyle=\sin{\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}}\cdot\frac{(\Gamma(\alpha/2+1/2))^{2}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(\Gamma(\alpha/2+1/2))^{2}}{\Gamma(\alpha)}=:G(\alpha).

We will show that
G(\alpha)<G(1)=1
for
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
. Let
\psi(x):=\Gamma^{\prime}(x)/\Gamma(x)
be the digamma function. It is well known that
x\mapsto\psi(x)
is continuous and increasing for
x>0
(see, e.g., Andrews et al.
[
1
, Theorem 1.2.5]
).

In case
\alpha\in(0,1)
we have
\alpha/2+1/2>\alpha
and then
\psi(\alpha/2+1/2)>\psi(\alpha)
. Then, of course,
\Gamma^{\prime}(\alpha/2+1/2)\Gamma(\alpha)-\Gamma(\alpha/2+1/2)\Gamma^{\prime}(\alpha)>0
. Therefore,

G^{\prime}(\alpha)=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha/2+1/2)}{\Gamma^{2}(\alpha)}\big[\Gamma^{\prime}(\alpha/2+1/2)\Gamma(\alpha)-\Gamma(\alpha/2+1/2)\Gamma^{\prime}(\alpha)\big]>0,

and so
G(\alpha)<G(1)=1
.

In case
\alpha\in(1,2)
we have
\alpha/2+1/2<\alpha
and then
\psi(\alpha/2+1/2)<\psi(\alpha)
, or
\Gamma^{\prime}(\alpha/2+1/2)\Gamma(\alpha)-\Gamma(\alpha/2+1/2)\Gamma^{\prime}(\alpha)<0
. Therefore,
G^{\prime}(\alpha)<0
and again
G(\alpha)<G(1)=1
.
∎

Proposition 3.2
.

For
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
\mathbb{E}_{1}|\ln{W}|<\infty
and

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}\ln{W}

\displaystyle>0,\quad\mathrm{for}~\alpha\in(0,1),

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}\ln{W}

\displaystyle=0,\quad\mathrm{for}~\alpha=1,

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}\ln{W}

\displaystyle<0,\quad\mathrm{for}~\alpha\in(1,2).

Proof.

First, we prove that
\mathbb{E}_{1}|\ln{W}|<\infty
. From (
1.34
) and (
1.17
) we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}|\ln{W}|

\displaystyle=C\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}w\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\rm d}z\,|z|^{-\alpha/2}|1-z|^{-1}|z-w|^{-1-\alpha}|z|^{\alpha}|\ln{w}|,

where
C=\pi^{-1}\alpha\sin(\pi\alpha/2)
. By Tonelli’s theorem and by changing variables
y=-z\in(0,\infty)
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}|\ln{W}|=C\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}y\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}w\,\frac{y^{\alpha/2}|\ln{w}|}{(y+1)(w+y)^{\alpha+1}}.

By changing variables
w=yv
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}|\ln{W}|

\displaystyle=C\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}y\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}v\,\frac{|\ln{(yv)|}}{y^{\alpha/2}(y+1)(v+1)^{\alpha+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}y\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}v\,\frac{|\ln{y}|+|\ln{v}|}{y^{\alpha/2}(y+1)(v+1)^{\alpha+1}}

\displaystyle=C\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(v+1)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}v\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|\ln{y}|}{y^{\alpha/2}(y+1)}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+C\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|\ln{v}|}{(v+1)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}v\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{y^{-\alpha/2}}{y+1}\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

Thus,
\mathbb{E}_{1}|\ln{W}|<\infty
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
. Then, from Jensen’s inequality for concave functions and from Lemma
3.1
, we get

\tfrac{\alpha-1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{1}\ln{W}=\mathbb{E}_{1}\ln{W^{(\alpha-1)/2}}\leq\ln{\mathbb{E}_{1}{W^{(\alpha-1)/2}}}<\ln{1}=0.

Hence, for
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\mathbb{E}_{1}\ln{W}>0
, and for
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
\mathbb{E}_{1}\ln{W}<0
.

Now assume that
\alpha=1
. Then

\displaystyle C^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{1}(\ln{W})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}y\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}w\,\frac{y^{1/2}\ln{w}}{(y+1)(w+y)^{2}}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}y\int_{1}^{\infty}{\rm d}w\,\frac{y^{1/2}\ln{w}}{(y+1)(w+y)^{2}}

(3.4)

\displaystyle=:I+II.

Using substitution
w=1/v
and integrating by parts we get

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}y\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}w\,\frac{\sqrt{y}\ln{w}}{(y+1)(w+y)^{2}}=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{y}}{y+1}\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\ln{v}}{(1+yv)^{2}}\,{\rm d}v\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\ln(1+1/y)}{\sqrt{y}(y+1)}\,\mathrm{d}y=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\ln(y+1)}{\sqrt{y}(y+1)}\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\ln(y)}{\sqrt{y}(y+1)}\,\mathrm{d}y.

Moreover, by changing variables
y=1/v
,

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\ln(y)}{\sqrt{y}(y+1)}\,\mathrm{d}y=-\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\ln{v}}{\sqrt{v}(v+1)}\,{\rm d}v,

hence

(3.5)

\displaystyle I=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\ln(y+1)}{\sqrt{y}(y+1)}\,\mathrm{d}y.

By changing variables
w=yz
in the integral
II
, we obtain

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}y\int_{1/y}^{\infty}{\rm d}z\,\frac{\ln{y}+\ln{z}}{\sqrt{y}(y+1)(z+1)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\ln{y}}{\sqrt{y}(y+1)}\int_{1/y}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}z}{(z+1)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{y}(y+1)}\int_{1/y}^{\infty}\frac{\ln{z}}{(z+1)^{2}}\,{\rm d}z\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{y}\ln{y}}{(y+1)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{y}(y+1)}\Big(\ln(y+1)-\frac{y\ln{y}}{y+1}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}y

(3.6)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\ln(y+1)}{\sqrt{y}(y+1)}\,\mathrm{d}y.

From (
3.4
), (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) we get
\mathbb{E}_{1}(\ln{W})=0
.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

For
\alpha=1
, we have
\sigma^{2}:=\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[\ln^{2}{W}\big]=4\pi^{2}/3
.

Proof.

We observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[\ln^{2}{W}\big]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}R(1,w)\ln^{2}{w}\,{\rm d}w=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}w\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}z\,\frac{z^{1/2}\ln^{2}{w}}{(1+z)(z+w)^{2}}.

By the substitution
y=z^{1/2}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[\ln^{2}{W}\big]=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}w\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}y\frac{2y^{2}\ln^{2}{w}}{(y^{2}+1)(y^{2}+w)^{2}}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}w\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}y\frac{y^{2}\ln^{2}{w}}{(y^{2}+1)(y^{2}+w)^{2}}.

Using the Cauchy’s residue theorem, we obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{y^{2}}{(y^{2}+1)(y^{2}+w)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}y=\frac{\pi}{2\sqrt{w}(\sqrt{w}+1)^{2}},\qquad w>0.

Hence, also by the substitution
u=\sqrt{w}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[\ln^{2}{W}\big]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\ln^{2}{w}}{2\sqrt{w}(\sqrt{w}+1)^{2}}\,{\rm d}w=4\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\ln^{2}{u}}{(u+1)^{2}}\,{\rm d}u.

By the substitution
u=e^{z}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[\ln^{2}{W}\big]=4\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{z^{2}e^{z}}{(e^{z}+1)^{2}}\,{\rm d}z=8\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{2}e^{z}}{(e^{z}+1)^{2}}\,{\rm d}z.

Integrating by parts we obtain the equality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[\ln^{2}{W}\big]=16\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{z}{e^{z}+1}\,{\rm d}z.

From Bateman
[
5
, (1.12.5), p. 32]
it follows that

\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[\ln^{2}{W}\big]=8\zeta(2)=\frac{4}{3}\pi^{2}.\qed

3.2.
Consecutive return positions to
D
and their limit

For
i=1,2,\ldots
, we define random variables

(3.7)

\displaystyle W_{i}=\frac{X_{R_{2i}}}{X_{R_{2i-2}}}.

Recall that
R_{0}=0
and then
W_{1}=X_{R_{2}}/X_{0}
.
Note that

(3.8)

\displaystyle W_{i}=W_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{2i-2}}.

Moreover, for
j<i
we have

(3.9)

\displaystyle W_{i}=W_{i-j}\circ\theta_{R_{2j}}.

Indeed,

W_{i}=W_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{2i-2}}=W_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{2(i-j)-2}}\circ\theta_{R_{2j}}=W_{i-j}\circ\theta_{R_{2j}}.

Lemma 3.4
.

Under
\mathbb{P}_{x}
,
x>0
, every random variable
W_{i}
,
i=1,2,\ldots
, has the same distribution and the density function of
W_{i}
is given by
R(1,w)
. In particular, the distribution does not depend on
x
.

Proof.

For
i=1,2,\ldots
and a bounded function
f
, by the strong Markov property, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}[f(W_{i})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f\big(W_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{2i-2}}\big)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big[f\big(W_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{2i-2}}\big)\Big|\mathcal{F}_{R_{2i-2}}\Big]\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[f\big(W_{1}\big)\Big]\Big|_{y=X_{R_{2i-2}}}\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[f\Big(\frac{X_{R_{2}}}{X_{0}}\Big)\Big]\Big|_{y=X_{R_{2i-2}}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[f\Big(\frac{W}{y}\Big)\Big]\Big|_{y=X_{R_{2i-2}}}\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{E}_{1}[f(W)]\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{1}[f(W)],

which proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

The random variables
\{W_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are independent under
\mathbb{P}_{x}
,
x>0
.

Proof.

Let
f_{i}
,
i=1,2,\ldots
, be bounded functions. From Lemma
3.4
it suffices to show that for
x>0
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{n}
,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})f_{2}(W_{i_{2}})\ldots f_{n}(W_{i_{n}})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{1}(W_{1})]\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{2}(W_{1})]\ldots\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{n}(W_{1})].

We first show that for
i_{1}<i_{2}
,

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})f_{2}(W_{i_{2}})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(W_{1})]\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{2}(W_{1})].

Indeed, from the strong Markov property and Lemma
3.4
we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})f_{2}(W_{i_{2}})\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[\mathbb{E}\Big[f_{1}\big(W_{i_{1}}\big)f_{2}\big(W_{i_{2}-i_{1}}\circ\theta_{R_{2i_{1}}}\big)\Big|\mathcal{F}_{R_{2i_{1}}}\Big]\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[f_{1}\big(W_{i_{1}}\big)\mathbb{E}\Big[f_{2}\big(W_{i_{2}-i_{1}}\circ\theta_{R_{2i_{1}}}\big)\Big|\mathcal{F}_{R_{2i_{1}}}\Big]\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})\mathbb{E}_{y}\big[f_{2}(W_{i_{2}-i_{1}})\big]\Big|_{y=X_{R_{2i_{1}}}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})\mathbb{E}_{y}\big[f_{2}(W_{1})\big]\Big|_{y=X_{R_{2i_{1}}}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[f_{2}\Big(\frac{X_{R_{2}}}{y}\Big)\Big]\Big|_{y=X_{R_{2i_{1}}}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})\mathbb{E}_{1}[f_{2}(X_{R_{2}})]\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{1}[f_{2}(X_{R_{2}})]\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{1}(W_{1})]\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{2}(W_{1})].

Assume now that the equality (
3.10
) holds for
n\geq 2
. We will prove it for
n+1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})f_{2}(W_{i_{2}})\ldots f_{n+1}(W_{i_{n+1}})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})\mathbb{E}\big[f_{2}(W_{i_{2}-i_{1}}\circ\theta_{R_{2i_{1}}})\ldots f_{n+1}(W_{i_{n+1}-i_{1}}\circ\theta_{R_{2i_{1}}})\big|\mathcal{F}_{R_{2i_{1}}}\big]\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})\mathbb{E}_{y}\big[f_{2}(W_{i_{2}-i_{1}})\ldots f_{n+1}(W_{i_{n+1}-i_{1}})\big]\big|_{y=X_{R_{2i_{1}}}}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})\mathbb{E}_{y}[f_{2}(W_{1})]\ldots\mathbb{E}_{y}[f_{n+1}(W_{1})]\big|_{y=X_{R_{2i_{1}}}}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[f_{2}\Big(\frac{X_{R_{2}}}{y}\Big)\Big]\ldots\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[f_{n+1}\Big(\frac{X_{R_{2}}}{y}\Big)\Big]\Big|_{y=X_{R_{2i_{1}}}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})\mathbb{E}_{1}[f_{2}(X_{R_{2}})]\ldots\mathbb{E}_{1}[f_{n+1}(X_{R_{2}})]\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{1}(W_{i_{1}})]\mathbb{E}_{1}[f_{2}(X_{R_{2}})]\ldots\mathbb{E}_{1}[f_{n+1}(X_{R_{2}})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{1}(W_{1})]\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{2}(W_{1})]\ldots\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{n+1}(W_{1})],

which is our claim.
∎

From Lemma
3.4
and Lemma
3.5
it follows that
\{W_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are i.i.d.

Now we consider consecutive return positions
V_{n}
,
n=0,1,2,\ldots
, of the process
X
to
D
, starting from
x>0
. Thus,
V_{0}=X_{0}=x
and for
n\ \geq 1
,
V_{n}=X_{R_{2n}}
. From (
3.7
) it follows that for
n\geq 1
,
V_{n}=W_{n}V_{n-1}
. In other words, there exist i.i.d. random variables
W_{i}
,
i=1,2,\ldots
, with the density function
R(1,w)
such that for
n\geq 1
,
V_{n}=X_{0}\prod_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}
.

Proposition 3.6
.

The following statements hold
\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. for every
x>0
.

(1)

If
\alpha\in(0,1)
, then
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n}}=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}V_{n}=+\infty
.

(2)

If
\alpha\in(1,2)
, then
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n}}=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}V_{n}=0
.

(3)

If
\alpha=1
, then

\liminf_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n}}=0,\qquad\limsup_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n}}=+\infty.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
. By the Strong Law of Large Numbers and Proposition
3.2
, it follows that

\ln\prod_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln{W_{i}}\to+\infty~~\mathrm{a.s.,}

as
n\to\infty
, which implies the desired convergence.

Now let
\alpha\in(1,2)
. Again, by the Strong Law of Large Numbers and Proposition
3.2
it follows that

\ln\prod_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln{W_{i}}\to-\infty~~\mathrm{a.s.,}

as
n\to\infty
, which implies the desired convergence.

Now assume that
\alpha=1
. Let
S_{n}^{\prime}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln{W_{i}}
. From Proposition
3.2
we know that
\mathbb{E}_{1}\ln{W}=0
and from Lemma
3.3
,
\mathbb{E}_{1}|\ln{W}|^{2}=\sigma^{2}\in(0,\infty)
. Hence, from the Law of the Iterated Logarithm (see Hartman and Wintner
[
42
]
or Acosta
[
30
]
), it follows that

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{S_{n}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{2n\ln\ln{n}}}=\sigma>0,\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{S_{n}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{2n\ln\ln{n}}}=-\sigma<0.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln{W_{i}}=+\infty,\qquad\liminf_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ln{W_{i}}=-\infty,

and then
\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}V_{n}=+\infty
, and
\liminf\limits_{n\to\infty}V_{n}=0
.
∎

3.3.
The lifetime of the process
X

Let
T=R_{2}
be the random time of the first return to
D=(0,\infty)
of the process
X=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
starting from
x>0
. Then, by Lemma
2.9
,

\displaystyle S(x,t):=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}a\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\rm d}b\,p_{r}^{D}(x,a)\nu(a,b)\nu(b,D)e^{-\nu(b,D)(t-r)}

is the density function of
T
. By changing variables
a=xc
,
b=xe
,
r=x^{\alpha}s
and using (
1.21
), (
1.15
) and (
1.16
), we get

\displaystyle S(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{tx^{-\alpha}}{\rm d}s\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}c\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\rm d}e\,x^{\alpha+1+1}p_{x^{\alpha}s}^{D}(x,xc)\nu(xc,xe)\nu(xe,D)e^{-\nu(xe,D)(t-x^{\alpha}s)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{tx^{-\alpha}}{\rm d}s\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}c\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\rm d}e\,x^{\alpha+2}x^{-1}p_{s}^{D}(1,c)x^{-1-\alpha}\nu(c,e)x^{-\alpha}\nu(e,D)e^{-\nu(e,D)(tx^{-\alpha}-s)}

\displaystyle=x^{-\alpha}\int_{0}^{tx^{-\alpha}}{\rm d}s\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}c\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\rm d}e\,p_{s}^{D}(1,c)\nu(c,e)\nu(e,D)e^{-\nu(e,D)(tx^{-\alpha}-s)}

\displaystyle=x^{-\alpha}S(1,tx^{-\alpha}).

Therefore,
\mathbb{P}_{x}(T\in dt)=\mathbb{P}_{1}(x^{\alpha}T\in dt)
.

For
n=1,2,\ldots
, we define random variables

(3.12)

\displaystyle T_{n}=\frac{R_{2n}-R_{2n-2}}{X_{R_{2n-2}}^{\alpha}}.

Note that for
n\geq 1
,
R_{2n}-R_{2n-2}=R_{2}\circ\theta_{R_{2n-2}}
, hence

(3.13)

\displaystyle T_{n}=T_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{2n-2}}.

Moreover, for
k<n
,

(3.14)

\displaystyle T_{n}=T_{n-k}\circ\theta_{R_{2k}}.

Indeed,

T_{n}=T_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{2n-2}}=T_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{2(n-k)-2}}\circ\theta_{R_{2k}}=T_{n-k}\circ\theta_{R_{2k}}.

Lemma 3.7
.

Under
\mathbb{P}_{x}
,
x>0
, every random variable
T_{n}
,
n=1,2,\ldots
, has the same distribution and the density function of
T_{n}
is given by
S(1,t)
. In particular, the distribution does not depend on
x
.

Proof.

For a bounded or non-negative function
f
, by the strong Markov property we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}[f(T_{n})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}[\mathbb{E}[f(T_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{2n-2}})|\mathcal{F}_{R_{2n-2}}]]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{E}_{y}[f(T_{1})]\big|_{y=X_{R_{2n-2}}}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[f\Big(\frac{R_{2}}{X_{0}^{\alpha}}\Big)\Big]\Big|_{y=X_{R_{2n-2}}}\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[f\Big(\frac{T}{y^{\alpha}}\Big)\Big]\Big|_{y=X_{R_{2n-2}}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{E}_{1}[f(T)]\big]=\mathbb{E}_{1}[f(T)],

which proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 3.8
.

The random variables
\{T_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are independent under
\mathbb{P}_{x}
,
x>0
.

Proof.

Let
f_{i}
,
i=1,2,\ldots
, be bounded or non-negative functions. From Lemma
3.7
it suffices to show that for
x>0
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
n_{1}<n_{2}<\ldots<n_{k}
,

(3.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(T_{n_{1}})f_{2}(T_{n_{2}})\ldots f_{k}(T_{n_{k}})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{1}(T_{1})]\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{2}(T_{1})]\ldots\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{k}(T_{1})].

First, we will show that for
n_{1}<n_{2}
,

(3.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(T_{n_{1}})f_{2}(T_{n_{2}})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{1}(T_{1})]\mathbb{E}_{x}[f_{2}(T_{1})].

Indeed, from the strong Markov property and Lemma
3.7
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(T_{n_{1}})f_{2}(T_{n_{2}})\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[f_{1}\big(T_{n_{1}}\big)\mathbb{E}\Big[f_{2}\big(T_{n_{2}-n_{1}}\circ\theta_{R_{2n_{1}}}\big)\Big|\mathcal{F}_{R_{2n_{1}}}\Big]\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[f_{1}\big(T_{n_{1}}\big)\mathbb{E}_{y}\big[f_{2}\big(T_{n_{2}-n_{1}}\big)\big]\big|_{y=X_{R_{2n_{1}}}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[f_{1}\big(T_{n_{1}}\big)\mathbb{E}_{y}\big[f_{2}\big(T_{1}\big)\big]\big|_{y=X_{R_{2n_{1}}}}\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[f_{1}\big(T_{n_{1}}\big)\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[f_{2}\Big(\frac{T}{y^{\alpha}}\Big)\Big]\Big|_{y=X_{R_{2n_{1}}}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[f_{1}\big(T_{n_{1}}\big)\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[f_{2}\big(T\big)\big]\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}\big(T_{n_{1}}\big)\big]\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[f_{2}\big(T\big)\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}\big(T_{1}\big)\big]\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[f_{2}\Big(\frac{R_{2}}{X_{0}^{\alpha}}\Big)\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(T_{1})\big]\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{2}(T_{1})\big].

Assume now that the equality (
3.15
) holds for some
k\geq 2
and all
x>0
. We will show it for
k+1
. We have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(T_{n_{1}})f_{2}(T_{n_{2}})\ldots f_{k+1}(T_{n_{k+1}})\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(T_{n_{1}})\mathbb{E}\big[f_{2}(T_{n_{2}-n_{1}}\circ\theta_{R_{2n_{1}}})\ldots f_{k+1}(T_{n_{k+1}-n_{1}}\circ\theta_{R_{2n_{1}}})\big|\mathcal{F}_{R_{2n_{1}}}\big]\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(T_{n_{1}})\mathbb{E}_{y}\big[f_{2}(T_{n_{2}-n_{1}})\ldots f_{k+1}(T_{n_{k+1}-n_{1}})\big]\big|_{y=X_{R_{2n_{1}}}}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(T_{n_{1}})\mathbb{E}_{y}[f_{2}(T_{1})]\ldots\mathbb{E}_{y}[f_{k+1}(T_{1})]\big|_{y=X_{R_{2n_{1}}}}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(T_{n_{1}})\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[f_{2}\Big(\frac{T}{y^{\alpha}}\Big)\Big]\ldots\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[f_{k+1}\Big(\frac{T}{y^{\alpha}}\Big)\Big]\big|_{y=X_{R_{2n_{1}}}}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(T_{n_{1}})\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[f_{2}(T)\big]\ldots\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[f_{k+1}(T)\big]\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(T_{n_{1}})\big]\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[f_{2}(T)\big]\ldots\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[f_{k+1}(T)\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{1}(T_{1})\big]\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{2}(T_{1})\big]\ldots\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f_{k+1}(T_{1})\big],

which is the desired conclusion.
∎

From Lemma
3.7
and Lemma
3.8
it follows that the family
\{T_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is i.i.d.

Now we consider the increments of times
S_{n}
,
n=0,1,2,\ldots
, of consecutive returns of the process
X
to
D
starting from
x>0
. Thus,
S_{0}:=0
and for
n\geq 1
,
S_{n}:=R_{2n}-R_{2n-2}
. From (
3.12
) it follows that for
n\geq 1
,
S_{n}=V_{n-1}^{\alpha}T_{n}
. In other words, there exist i.i.d. random variables
T_{n}
with the density function
S(1,t)
and i.i.d. random variables
W_{i}
with the density function
R(1,w)
such that for
n\geq 1
,
S_{n}=X_{0}^{\alpha}\big(\prod_{0<k<n}W_{k}^{\alpha}\big)T_{n}
.

Lemma 3.9
.

For
n\geq 1
,
x>0
, and bounded or non-negative functions
f,g
,

\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f(V_{n-1})g(T_{n})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f(V_{n-1})]\mathbb{E}_{x}[g(T_{n})\big].

Proof.

Note that
V_{n-1}=X_{R_{2n-2}}
and
T_{n}=T_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{2n-2}}
. Then from the strong Markov property and Lemma
3.7
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f(V_{n-1})g(T_{n})\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f(X_{R_{2n-2}})\mathbb{E}\big[g(T_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{2n-2}})\big|\mathcal{F}_{R_{2n-2}}\big]\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f(X_{R_{2n-2}})\mathbb{E}_{y}\big[g(T_{1})\big]\big|_{y=X_{R_{2n-2}}}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[f(X_{R_{2n-2}})\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[g\Big(\frac{R_{2}}{y^{\alpha}}\Big)\Big]\Big|_{y=X_{R_{2n-2}}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f(V_{n-1})\big]\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[g(R_{2})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f(V_{n-1})\big]\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[g\Big(\frac{R_{2}}{x^{\alpha}}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f(V_{n-1})\big]\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[g(T_{1})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[f(V_{n-1})\big]\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[g(T_{n})\big],

which completes the proof.
∎

Recall that
R_{\infty}=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}R_{2n}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}S_{n}=X_{0}^{\alpha}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\big(\prod_{0<k<n}W_{k}^{\alpha}\big)T_{n}
is the lifetime of the process
X
.

Proposition 3.10
.

The following statements hold
\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. for every
x\neq 0
.

(1)

If
\alpha\in(0,1]
, then
R_{\infty}=\infty
.

(2)

If
\alpha\in(1,2)
, then
R_{\infty}<\infty
.

Proof.

Let
x>0
. Assume that
\alpha\in(0,1)
. We have
\ln{S_{n}}=\alpha\ln{V_{n-1}}+\ln{T_{n}}
. By Proposition
3.6
,
V_{n-1}\to\infty
a.e. as
n\to\infty
. Moreover, for any
n=1,2,\ldots
,
\mathbb{P}_{x}(T_{n}>1)=c>0
. Hence,
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}(T_{n}>1)=\infty
. Therefore, by Lemma
3.8
and the Borel–Cantelli lemma we have
\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}\{T_{n}>1\}\big)=1
, which means that with probability one there exists a subsequence
(n_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
T_{n_{k}}>1
and then
\ln{T_{n_{k}}}>0
. Therefore,
\ln{S_{n_{k}}}\to\infty
a.e., as
k\to\infty
. As a result,
R_{\infty}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}S_{n}=\infty

\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s.

Assume that
\alpha\in(1,2)
. From the subadditivity of the function
r^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}
for
r>0
, the Tonelli’s theorem, Lemma
3.9
and Lemma
3.7
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}S_{n}\Big]^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}S_{n}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}V_{n-1}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}T_{n}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}\Big]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[V_{n-1}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}T_{n}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[V_{n-1}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\Big]\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[T_{n}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[T_{1}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}\Big]\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[V_{n-1}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{1}\Big[T^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}\Big]\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[V_{n-1}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\Big].

Moreover, from Lemma
3.5
and Lemma
3.1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[V_{n-1}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[X_{0}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}W_{i}\Big]^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}=x^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[W_{1}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\big]

\displaystyle=x^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[W^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\big]=x^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\rho^{n-1}=x^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\frac{1}{1-\rho}<\infty.

It suffices to show that
\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[T^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}\big]<\infty
.
For
T=R_{2}
, we have
T=R_{1}+R_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{1}}
.
Using this observation, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}T^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{1}[R_{1}+R_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{1}}]^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}\leq\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[R_{1}\big]^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}+\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[R_{1}\circ\theta_{R_{1}}\big]^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{1}^{Y}\tau_{D}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}+\mathbb{E}_{1}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{y}\big(R_{1}\big)^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}\Big|_{y=X_{R_{1}}}\Big],

where
\tau_{D}
is the first exit time from
D
of the process
Y
. Recall that
R_{1}<\infty
a.s.

By Bañuelos and Bogdan
[
3
, Exercise 3.2 and Theorem 4.1]
,
\mathbb{E}_{1}^{Y}\tau_{D}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}<\infty
, because
\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}<\frac{1}{2}
, for
\alpha\in(1,2)
. Alternatively, the estimates of
\mathbb{E}_{1}^{Y}\tau_{D}^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}
can be obtained from the estimates of survival probability in Bogdan et al.
[
15
]
and the equality

\mathbb{E}_{x}^{Y}\tau_{D}^{p}=p\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{p-1}\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}(\tau_{D}>t)\,{\rm d}t,\quad p>0,~x>0.

Furthermore, since
R_{1}
for the starting point
y<0
has the exponential distribution with mean
1/\nu(y,D)
, then from (
1.17
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{y}\big(R_{1}\big)^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}\nu(y,D)e^{-\nu(y,D)t}\,{\rm d}t=\big[\nu(y,D)\big]^{\frac{1-\alpha}{2\alpha}}\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}e^{-s}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\approx|y|^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}e^{-s}\,{\rm d}s=\Gamma\big(\tfrac{3\alpha-1}{2\alpha}\big)|y|^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\approx|y|^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{y}\big(R_{1}\big)^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}}\Big|_{y=X_{R_{1}}}\Big]

\displaystyle\approx\mathbb{E}_{1}\big[|X_{R_{1}}|^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\big]=\mathbb{E}_{1}^{Y}\big[|Y_{\tau_{D}}|^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}\big],

and from (
1.34
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}^{Y}|Y_{\tau_{D}}|^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{0}|y|^{\frac{\alpha-1}{2}}P_{D}(1,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\approx\int_{-\infty}^{0}|y|^{-1/2}|1-y|^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}y=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\sqrt{y}(1+y)}<\infty.

Let
\alpha=1
. From Proposition
3.6
,
\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}V_{n}=+\infty

\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s. Thus, there exists subsequence
(V_{n_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
V_{n_{k}}\to+\infty

\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s., as
k\to\infty
. By the same argument as in the case of
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\ln{S_{n_{k}}}=\ln{V_{n_{k}-1}}+\ln{T_{n_{k}}}\to+\infty
in probability. Therefore, there exists a subsequence
(\ln S_{n_{k_{l}}})_{l\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\ln S_{n_{k_{l}}}\to+\infty

\mathbb{P}_{x}
-a.s., as
l\to\infty
, which implies that
R_{\infty}=\infty
.

Now let
x<0
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(R_{\infty}<\infty\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{E}\big(\ind_{(0,\infty)}(R_{\infty})~\big|~\mathcal{F}_{R_{1}}\big)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{E}\big(\ind_{(0,\infty)}(R_{\infty}\circ\theta_{R_{1}}+R_{1})~\big|~\mathcal{F}_{R_{1}}\big)\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathbb{E}_{X_{R_{1}}}\big(\ind_{(0,\infty)}(R_{\infty}+s)\big)\Big|_{s=R_{1}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[\mathbb{P}_{X_{R_{1}}}\big(R_{\infty}+s<\infty\big)\Big|_{s=R_{1}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{P}_{X_{R_{1}}}\big(R_{\infty}<\infty\big)\big],

since
R_{1}<\infty
a.s.
Note that for
\alpha\in(0,1]
, from the first part of the proof, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(R_{\infty}<\infty\big)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{P}_{X_{R_{1}}}\big(R_{\infty}<\infty\big)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}0=0,

and for
\alpha\in(1,2)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(R_{\infty}<\infty\big)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\mathbb{P}_{X_{R_{1}}}\big(R_{\infty}<\infty\big)\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}1=1,

which completes the proof.
∎

3.4.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

Let
\alpha=1
. The case
x>0
follows directly from Proposition
3.6
and Proposition
3.10
. In case
x<0
, we have

\liminf_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n+1}}=\big(\liminf_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n}}\big)\circ\theta_{R_{1}}

and then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\liminf_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n+1}}=0\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big(\mathbb{E}\big(\ind_{\{0\}}\big(\liminf_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n+1}}\big)~\big|~\mathcal{F}_{R_{1}}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big(\mathbb{E}\big(\ind_{\{0\}}\big(\liminf_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n}}\big)\circ\theta_{R_{1}}\big)~\big|~\mathcal{F}_{R_{1}}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big(\mathbb{E}_{X_{R_{1}}}\big(\ind_{\{0\}}\big(\liminf_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n}}\big)\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big(\mathbb{P}_{X_{R_{1}}}\big(\liminf_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n}}=0\big)\big)=1.

Similarly, we prove that
\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n+1}}=\infty\big)=1
.

Now assume that
\alpha\neq 1
. Note that
Y_{t}:=h_{\alpha-1}(X_{t})\geq 0
is a supermartingale with right-continuous trajectories. Indeed, for
s<t
, from Markov property and Proposition
2.19
we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[Y_{t}~\big|~\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[h_{\alpha-1}(X_{t})~\big|~\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[h_{\alpha-1}(X_{t-s}\circ\theta_{s})~\big|~\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[h_{\alpha-1}(X_{t-s})\circ\theta_{s}~\big|~\mathcal{F}_{s}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{X_{s}}\big[h_{\alpha-1}(X_{t-s})\big]=K_{t-s}h_{\alpha-1}(X_{s})\leq h_{\alpha-1}(X_{s})=Y_{s}.

Hence, it is clear that
Z_{t}:=-h_{\alpha-1}(X_{t})
is a submartingale.

Following
[
57
, Chapter II.2]
, we consider a function
f:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathbb{T}\subseteq[0,\infty)\cap\mathbb{Q}
is a countable set, and define the
number of downcrossings
of the interval
[a,b]
by the function
f
as follows. Let
F:=\{t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{m}\}\subset\mathbb{T}
. For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
a<b
, we define inductively,

\displaystyle s_{1}

\displaystyle=\inf\{t_{i}:~t_{i}\in F,~f(t_{i})>b\},

\displaystyle s_{2}

\displaystyle=\inf\{t_{i}>s_{1}:~t_{i}\in F,~f(t_{i})<a\},

\displaystyle s_{2n+1}

\displaystyle=\inf\{t_{i}>s_{2n}:~t_{i}\in F,~f(t_{i})>b\},

\displaystyle s_{2n+2}

\displaystyle=\inf\{t_{i}>s_{2n+1}:~t_{i}\in F,~f(t_{i})<a\},

while
\inf(\emptyset):=t_{m}
.
We set

\displaystyle D_{F}(f,[a,b]):=\sup\{n:~s_{2n}<t_{m}\}.

The
number of downcrossings
of the interval
[a,b]
by the function
f:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
we define as the number

\displaystyle D_{\mathbb{T}}(f,[a,b]):=\sup\{D_{F}(f,[a,b]):~F\subseteq\mathbb{T},~F~\mathrm{finite}\}.

From the Doob’s downcrossing lemma
[
57
, Proposition 2.1, p. 61]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[D_{\mathbb{T}}(Z,[a,b])\big]\leq\sup_{t\in\mathbb{T}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[(Z_{t}-b)^{+}\big]}{b-a}=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{T}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[(h_{\alpha-1}(X_{t})+b)^{-}\big]}{b-a}\leq\frac{|b|}{b-a}<\infty.

Hence,
\mathbb{P}_{x}\big(D_{\mathbb{T}}(Z,[a,b])=\infty)=0
. Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{T}}:=\bigcap_{a,b\in\mathbb{Q},~a<b}\big\{\omega:~D_{\mathbb{T}}(Z(\omega),[a,b])<\infty\big\}.

Then
\mathbb{P}_{x}(\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{T}})=1
, because
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{T}}
is an intersection of countable many sets of the measure one.

Let
\xi
denote the lifetime of the process
X
, i.e.
\xi=R_{\infty}
. Moreover, let
\mathbb{T}=[0,\infty)\cap\mathbb{Q}
.
If
\omega\in\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{T}}
, then
D_{\mathbb{T}}(Z(\omega),[a,b])<\infty
for any rational numbers
a<b
.
We claim that the limit
Z(\omega):=\lim\limits_{t\to\xi}Z_{t}(\omega)
exists (but it may be infinite).
Indeed, assume that this limit does not exist. Then we can find rational numbers
\alpha<\beta
such that

\displaystyle\liminf_{t\to\xi}Z_{t}(\omega)<\alpha<\beta<\limsup_{t\to\xi}Z_{t}(\omega).

From this fact we conclude that there exists an increasing sequence
(r_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{T}
such that
Z_{r_{2n}}(\omega)\leq\alpha<\beta\leq Z_{r_{2n-1}}(\omega)
. By taking
\mathbb{I}=\{r_{1},r_{2},r_{3},\ldots\}
, we obtain that

D_{\mathbb{T}}(Z(\omega),[\alpha,\beta])\geq D_{\mathbb{I}}(Z(\omega),[\alpha,\beta])=\infty,

which is a contradiction. Hence, the limit
Z:=-\lim\limits_{t\nearrow\xi}h_{\alpha-1}(X_{t})
exists with probability one. Therefore,
\lim\limits_{t\nearrow\xi}|X_{t}|
exists with probability one.

Assume that
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Then from Proposition
3.10
, the lifetime of the process
X
is infinite a.e. Moreover, from Proposition
3.6
for
x>0
we have
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n}}=\infty
a.e., hence
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}h_{\alpha-1}(X_{R_{2n}})=0
a.e. From the uniqueness of the limit we get that
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}h_{\alpha-1}(X_{t})=0
a.e., hence
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}|X_{t}|=\infty
a.e. For
x<0
, by the analogous computations as in the case
\alpha=1
, we get
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n+1}}=\infty
a.e., hence in the same way as above we get
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}|X_{t}|=\infty
a.e.

Now assume that
\alpha\in(1,2)
. Then from Proposition
3.10
for
x>0
, the lifetime of the process
X
is finite a.e. Moreover, from Proposition
3.6
,
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}X_{R_{2n}}=0
a.e., hence
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}h_{\alpha-1}(X_{R_{2n}})=0
a.e. From the uniqueness of the limit we get that
\lim\limits_{t\nearrow\xi}h_{\alpha-1}(X_{t})=0
a.e., hence
\lim\limits_{t\nearrow\xi}X_{t}=0
a.e. For
x<0
, we argue in the same way.

The proof is complete. In passing we note that the process
X
crosses zero an infinite number of times before the lifetime because
0<R_{n}<\infty
for every
n
by Remark
2.8
. ∎

3.5.
Feller property

Here we prove that for
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
forms a Feller semigroup on the space
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
. For this purpose, we will use the fact that the process
X
has finite lifetime for such
\alpha
.

Corollary 3.11
.

For
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
x\neq 0
, and
t>0
,

\lim_{x\to 0}K_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})=\lim_{x\to 0}\mathbb{P}_{x}(R_{\infty}>t)=0.

Proof.

From Lemma
2.15
it follows that
K_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})=K_{tx^{-\alpha}}(1,\mathbb{R})
for all
x>0
. By Proposition
3.10
,

K_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{P}_{1}(R_{\infty}>tx^{-\alpha})\to 0,

as
x\to 0^{+}
.

Similarly, for all
x<0
, from Lemma
2.15
it follows that
K_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})=K_{t|x|^{-\alpha}}(-1,\mathbb{R})
, and again by Proposition
3.10
,

K_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{P}_{-1}(R_{\infty}>t|x|^{-\alpha})\to 0,

as
x\to 0^{-}
.
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

For
f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
and
x\neq 0
,
K_{t}f(x)\to f(x)
, as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Proof.

From Corollary
2.4
and Lemma
2.10
we have

\displaystyle|K_{t}f(x)-f(x)|

\displaystyle=\left|\widehat{P}_{t}f(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{s}\widehat{\nu}K_{t-s}f(x)\,{\rm d}s-f(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\big|\widehat{P}_{t}f(x)-f(x)\big|+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{s}\widehat{\nu}\mathbf{1}(x)\,{\rm d}s.

Moreover, from Ikeda–Watanabe formula (
1.30
), for
x>0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{s}\widehat{\nu}\mathbf{1}(x)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,p_{s}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)=\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}(\tau_{D}<t)\to 0,

as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Similarly, for
x<0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{s}\widehat{\nu}\mathbf{1}(x)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\nu(x,D)e^{-\nu(x,D)s}\,{\rm d}s=1-e^{-\nu(x,D)t}\to 0,

as
t\to 0^{+}
. Hence, from Lemma
2.2
we obtain a desired convergence.
∎

Proposition 3.13
.

For
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a Feller semigroup on
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
.

Proof.

From Lemma
2.10
it follows that for any
f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
,
0\leq f\leq 1
we have
0\leq K_{t}f\leq 1
,
t\geq 0
. Hence
(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, is a semigroup of nonnegative contraction operators on
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
.

We claim that
K_{t}C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})\subset C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
. Let
t>0
and
f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
. Then from Proposition
2.16
,
K_{t}f\in C(\mathbb{R}_{*})
. Moreover, from Corollary
3.11
,

\lim_{x\to 0}\big|K_{t}f(x)\big|\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\lim_{x\to 0}K_{t}\mathbf{1}(x)=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\lim_{x\to 0}K_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})=0.

From Corollary
2.4
and Lemma
2.10
, for
x>0
,

\displaystyle\big|K_{t}f(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leq P_{t}^{D}|f|(x)+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,p_{s}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z).

From (
1.30
) we get

(3.17)

\big|K_{t}f(x)\big|\leq P_{t}^{D}|f|(x)+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}(\tau_{D}<t).

It follows from (
1.21
) that

\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}(\tau_{D}<t)=1-\int_{D}p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y=1-\int_{D}p_{x^{-\alpha}t}^{D}(1,y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\mathbb{P}_{1}^{Y}(\tau_{D}<x^{-\alpha}t)\to 0,
as
x\to\infty.

From (
3.17
) and Lemma
1.5
it follows that
\lim\limits_{x\to+\infty}K_{t}f(x)=0
. Similarly, from Corollary
2.4
and Lemma
2.10
, for
x<0
,

\displaystyle\big|K_{t}f(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leq|f(x)|e^{-\nu(x,D)t}+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\nu(x,D)e^{-\nu(x,D)s}\,{\rm d}s

(3.18)

\displaystyle=|f(x)|e^{-\nu(x,D)t}+\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\big[1-e^{-\nu(x,D)t}\big].

From (
3.18
) and (
1.17
) it follows that
\lim\limits_{x\to-\infty}K_{t}f(x)=0
. Therefore, we obtained that
K_{t}f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
.

Hence, with Lemma
3.12
, we finish the proof.
∎

4.
Short-time behavior

In this section, we are interested in infinitesimal behavior of the semigroup at
t=0
.
Recall that the classical
infinitesimal generator
(see, e.g.,
[
52
]
) for a Feller semigroup
(T_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on
C_{0}(\mathscr{X})
is

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}f:=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{T_{t}f-f}{t}\quad\mathrm{in}~C_{0}(\mathscr{X}).

The domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L})
of
\mathcal{L}
consists of all the functions
f\in C_{0}(\mathscr{X})
for which the limit (
4.1
) exists. Dealing with generators is somehow difficult because for nonlocal operators, test functions as a rule are not in their domain; see Bäumer, Luks, Meerschaert
[
2
, Theorem 2.3]
.

The main goal of this section is to derive the
pointwise formula

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{K_{t}h_{\beta}(x)-h_{\beta}(x)}{t},\quad x\neq 0,

for the functions
h_{\beta}
defined in Section
2
.
The precise statement is given in Proposition
4.15
after a number of auxiliary results.

4.1.
Estimation of integrals

Lemma 4.1
.

For
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
x>0
,
y<0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1/2}s^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|}{s^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{\alpha/2}

\displaystyle\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{s^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{s}(x,y)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\approx\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(|x-y|^{-1}\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

Proof.

Let
A:=\int_{0}^{1/2}s^{-1}\big(1\wedge\frac{|x|}{s^{1/\alpha}}\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{s^{1/\alpha}}\big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{s}(x,y)\,{\rm d}s
and consider three cases.

Case 1.
Let
x^{\alpha}\geq 1/2
. Then of course
|x-y|^{\alpha}\geq 1/2
and then from (
1.9
),

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{1/2}s^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{s^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}\frac{s}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\Big[\int_{0}^{|y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2}{\rm d}s+|y|^{-\alpha/2}\int_{|y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2}^{1/2}s^{1/2}\,{\rm d}s\Big]

\displaystyle=|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\Big[\big(|y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2\big)+\tfrac{2}{3}|y|^{-\alpha/2}\Big(\Big(\frac{1}{2}\Big)^{3/2}-\big(|y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2\big)^{3/2}\Big)\Big].

For
|y|^{\alpha}>1/4
,

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\approx|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\approx|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big(1\vee|y|^{-\alpha/2}\big)

\displaystyle\approx\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(|x-y|^{-1}\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big),

and for
|y|^{\alpha}\leq 1/4
,

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\approx|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big[|y|^{\alpha}+|y|^{-\alpha/2}\big]=|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}|y|^{-\alpha/2}\big[1+|y|^{3\alpha/2}\big]

\displaystyle\approx|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}|y|^{-\alpha/2}\approx|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big(1\vee|y|^{-\alpha/2}\big)

\displaystyle\approx\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(|x-y|^{-1}\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

Case 2.
Let
x^{\alpha}<1/2
and
|x-y|^{\alpha}>1
. Then
|y|^{\alpha}=\big(|x-y|-|x|\big)^{\alpha}>\big(1-2^{-1/\alpha}\big)^{\alpha}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{x^{\alpha}}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}s+\int_{x^{\alpha}}^{1/2}\frac{x^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{s}}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}s=|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\Big[x^{\alpha}+2x^{\alpha/2}\big(2^{-1/2}-x^{\alpha/2}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}x^{\alpha/2}\big[\sqrt{2}-x^{\alpha/2}\big].

Note that
2^{-1/2}=\sqrt{2}-2^{-1/2}\leq\sqrt{2}-x^{\alpha/2}\leq\sqrt{2}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle A\approx|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}x^{\alpha/2}\approx\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(|x-y|^{-1}\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

Case 3.
Let
x^{\alpha}<1/2
and
|x-y|^{\alpha}\leq 1
. Then
|y|^{\alpha}=\big(|x-y|-|x|\big)^{\alpha}\leq 1
. Assume that
|x|<|y|
. Then

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{x^{\alpha}}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}s+\int_{x^{\alpha}}^{|y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2}\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{s}}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{|y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2}^{|x-y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2}|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}s+\int_{|x-y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2}^{1/2}|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}s^{-1/\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}|x|^{\alpha}+2|x|^{\alpha/2}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big(\big(|y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2\big)^{1/2}-|x|^{\alpha/2}\big)

\displaystyle+|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big[\big(|x-y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2\big)-\big(|y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2\big)\big]

(4.2)

\displaystyle+\alpha|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}\big[\big(|x-y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2\big)^{-1/\alpha}-2^{1/\alpha}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|x|^{\alpha}}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}+\frac{2|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{\alpha/2}}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}+\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}+\alpha|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}\big(|x-y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2\big)^{-1/\alpha}.

Note that
|x|^{\alpha}=|x|^{\alpha/2}|x|^{\alpha/2}<|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{\alpha/2}
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle|x-y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2\geq|x-y|^{\alpha}\wedge\tfrac{1}{2}|x-y|^{\alpha}=\tfrac{1}{2}|x-y|^{\alpha}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{\alpha/2}}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}+\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}=\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\left[1+\frac{|y|^{\alpha}}{|x-y|^{\alpha}}\right]\lesssim\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}

\displaystyle=\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(|x-y|^{-1}\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

For the estimate from below, we consider two cases. For
|x|<|y|<(8^{1/\alpha}-1)|x|
, from (
4.2
), we have that

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\gtrsim|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}|x|^{\alpha}=\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\cdot\left[\frac{|x|\,|y|}{(|x|+|y|)^{2}}\right]^{\alpha/2}\geq\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\cdot\left[\frac{|x|}{|x|+|y|}\right]^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\cdot\left[\frac{|x|}{|x|+(8^{1/\alpha}-1)|x|}\right]^{\alpha}\approx\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}

\displaystyle=\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(|x-y|^{-1}\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

For
|y|\geq(8^{1/\alpha}-1)|x|
, from (
4.2
), we have that

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\gtrsim 2|x|^{\alpha/2}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big(\big(|y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2\big)^{1/2}-|x|^{\alpha/2}\big)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\left(\frac{|y|}{|x|+|y|}\right)^{\alpha/2}\left[\left(\frac{|y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2}{|x-y|^{\alpha}}\right)^{1/2}-\left(\frac{|x|}{|x-y|}\right)^{\alpha/2}\right]

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\left(\frac{1}{(8^{1/\alpha}-1)^{-1}+1}\right)^{\alpha/2}\left[\left(\frac{|y|\wedge 2^{-1/\alpha}}{\big((8^{1/\alpha}-1)^{-1}+1\big)|y|}\right)^{\alpha/2}-\left(\frac{1}{8^{1/\alpha}}\right)^{\alpha/2}\right]

\displaystyle\approx\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\left[\big(8^{1/\alpha}-1)^{-1}+1\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge 2^{-1/\alpha}|y|^{-1}\big)^{\alpha/2}-8^{-1/2}\right].

Recall that
|y|\leq 1
and then

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\left[\big((8^{1/\alpha}-1)^{-1}+1\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge 2^{-1/\alpha}\big)^{\alpha/2}-8^{-1/2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\left[\frac{(8^{1/\alpha}-1)^{\alpha/2}}{4}-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\approx\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(|x-y|^{-1}\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

Now assume that
|x|\geq|y|
. Then, similarly,

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{|y|^{\alpha}}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}s+\int_{|y|^{\alpha}}^{x^{\alpha}}s^{1/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{x^{\alpha}}^{|x-y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2}|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}s+\int_{|x-y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2}^{1/2}s^{-1/\alpha-1}|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}|y|^{\alpha}+\tfrac{2}{3}|y|^{-\alpha/2}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big[|x|^{3\alpha/2}-|y|^{3\alpha/2}\big]

\displaystyle+|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big[\big(|x-y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2\big)-|x|^{\alpha}\big]

\displaystyle+\alpha|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}\big[\big(|x-y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2\big)^{-1/\alpha}-2^{1/\alpha}\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{|y|^{\alpha}}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}+\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}+\frac{\alpha|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{\big(|x-y|^{\alpha}\wedge 1/2\big)^{1/\alpha}}

(4.3)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{|y|^{\alpha}}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}+\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}.

Note that from the assumption, we have that

\frac{|y|^{\alpha}}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}=\frac{|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\cdot\left[\frac{|y|}{|x-y|}\right]^{\alpha}|y|^{\alpha/2}\leq\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}.

Hence,

\displaystyle A\lesssim\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}=\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(|x-y|^{-1}\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

For the estimate from below, we consider two cases. For
|x|\geq|y|\geq 2^{2/3-2/\alpha}|x|
, from (
4.3
),

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{|y|^{\alpha}}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}=\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\left[\frac{|y|^{3/2}|x|^{-1/2}}{|x-y|}\right]^{\alpha}\geq\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\left[\frac{|y|^{3/2}|x|^{-1/2}}{2|x|}\right]^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\left(\frac{|y|}{2^{2/3}|x|}\right)^{3\alpha/2}\geq\frac{1}{8}\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}

\displaystyle\approx\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(|x-y|^{-1}\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

Similarly, for
|y|<2^{2/3-2/\alpha}|x|
, from (
4.3
),

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\gtrsim|y|^{-\alpha/2}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big[|x|^{3\alpha/2}-|y|^{3\alpha/2}\big]\geq|y|^{-\alpha/2}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}|x|^{3\alpha/2}\big[1-2^{\alpha-3}\big]

\displaystyle\approx\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\left(\frac{|x|}{|x-y|}\right)^{\alpha}\geq\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}|y|^{-\alpha/2}}{|x-y|}\left(\frac{|x|}{2|x|}\right)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\approx\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(|x-y|^{-1}\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big),

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

For
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
t>0
,
x>0
, and
y<0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(1\wedge\frac{z^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)p_{t}(x,z)\nu(z,y)\,{\rm d}z\approx t^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{t^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{t}(x,y).

Proof.

Let
I_{t}(x,y):=\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(1\wedge\frac{z^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\big)p_{t}(x,z)\nu(z,y)\,{\rm d}z
. By substitution
z=t^{1/\alpha}w
, from (
1.8
), we get

(4.4)

\displaystyle I_{t}(t^{1/\alpha}x,t^{1/\alpha}y)=t^{-1/\alpha-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(1\wedge w^{\alpha/2}\big)p_{1}(x,w)\nu(w,y)\,{\rm d}w=t^{-1/\alpha-1}I_{1}(x,y),

hence it suffices to find an estimation of
I_{1}(x,y)
.

First, assume that
x<1/2
. From (
1.9
) we then have

\displaystyle I_{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(1\wedge w^{\alpha/2}\big)\big(1\wedge|x-w|^{-\alpha-1}\big)|w-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}w

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{1}w^{\alpha/2}|w-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}w+\int_{1}^{\infty}|x-w|^{-\alpha-1}|w-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}w=:A(y)+B(x,y).

Using the substitution
w=|y|u
we obtain that

(4.5)

\displaystyle A(y)=|y|^{-\alpha/2}\int_{0}^{1/|y|}\frac{u^{\alpha/2}}{(u+1)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}u.

Furthermore, for
|y|\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{u^{\alpha/2}}{(u+1)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}u\leq\int_{0}^{1/|y|}\frac{u^{\alpha/2}}{(u+1)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}u\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{\alpha/2}}{(u+1)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}u<\infty,

hence
\int_{0}^{1/|y|}\frac{u^{\alpha/2}}{(u+1)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}u\approx 1
. Moreover, for
|y|>1
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1/|y|}\frac{u^{\alpha/2}}{(u+1)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}u\approx\int_{0}^{1/|y|}u^{\alpha/2}\,{\rm d}u\approx|y|^{-\alpha/2-1}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1/|y|}\frac{u^{\alpha/2}}{(u+1)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}u\approx 1\wedge|y|^{-\alpha/2-1}.

From (
4.5
),

(4.6)

\displaystyle A(y)\approx|y|^{-\alpha-1}\wedge|y|^{-\alpha/2}.

For the integral
B(x,y)
, we proceed as follows,

\displaystyle B(x,y)\lesssim\int_{1}^{\infty}|w-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}w\leq\int_{1}^{\infty}w^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}w\approx 1,

and

\displaystyle B(x,y)\leq|y|^{-\alpha-1}\int_{1}^{\infty}|w-1/2|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}w\approx|y|^{-\alpha-1}.

Hence,

(4.7)

\displaystyle B(x,y)\lesssim 1\wedge|y|^{-\alpha-1}.

Furthermore, note that from (
4.6
) and (
4.7
),

\displaystyle I_{1}(x,y)\approx A(y)+B(x,y)\lesssim\big(|y|^{-\alpha-1}\wedge|y|^{-\alpha/2}\big)+\big(1\wedge|y|^{-\alpha-1}\big)\lesssim|y|^{-\alpha-1}\wedge|y|^{-\alpha/2}.

Indeed, for
|y|\leq 1
it is obvious, because
|y|^{-\alpha/2}\geq 1
. On the other hand, for
|y|>1
,
1\wedge|y|^{-\alpha-1}=|y|^{-\alpha-1}=|y|^{-\alpha-1}\wedge|y|^{-\alpha/2}
.
Further, it is obvious that

\displaystyle I_{1}(x,y)\gtrsim A(y)\approx|y|^{-\alpha-1}\wedge|y|^{-\alpha/2},

hence

(4.8)

\displaystyle I_{1}(x,y)\approx|y|^{-\alpha-1}\wedge|y|^{-\alpha/2}.

Moreover, for
|y|<1
we have
|x-y|\lesssim 1
, hence from (
4.8
), we get

I_{1}(x,y)\approx|y|^{-\alpha/2}\approx\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

Similarly, for
|y|\geq 1
, we have
|x-y|=x+|y|\geq x+1>x+2x=3x
and
|x-y|\geq|y|=|x-y|-|x|>|x-y|-\tfrac{1}{3}|x-y|=\tfrac{2}{3}|x-y|
and from (
4.8
),

\displaystyle I_{1}(x,y)\approx|y|^{-\alpha-1}\approx|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}=\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

To sum up, we obtain so far that for
x\in(0,1/2)
,

(4.9)

\displaystyle I_{1}(x,y)\approx\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

Now, assume that
x\geq 1/2
. From (
1.9
) we have

\displaystyle I_{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(1\wedge w^{\alpha/2}\big)\big(1\wedge|x-w|^{-\alpha-1}\big)|w-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}w

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{1/4}w^{\alpha/2}|x-w|^{-\alpha-1}|w-y|^{-\alpha-1}{\rm d}w

\displaystyle+\int\limits_{|x-w|<1/4}|w-y|^{-\alpha-1}{\rm d}w

\displaystyle+\int\limits_{\,\,\begin{subarray}{c}|x-w|\geq 1/4\\[3.0pt]
w\geq 1/4\end{subarray}}|x-w|^{-\alpha-1}|w-y|^{-\alpha-1}{\rm d}w=:C(x,y)+D(x,y)+E(x,y).

From (
4.6
),

\displaystyle C(x,y)

\displaystyle\approx|x|^{-\alpha-1}\int_{0}^{1/4}w^{\alpha/2}|w-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}w\approx|x|^{-\alpha-1}A(4y)

\displaystyle\approx|x|^{-\alpha-1}\big(|4y|^{-\alpha-1}\wedge|4y|^{-\alpha/2}\big)\approx|x|^{-\alpha-1}\big(|y|^{-\alpha-1}\wedge|y|^{-\alpha/2}\big).

Furthermore,

\displaystyle D(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{|x-w|<1/4}|w-y|^{-\alpha-1}{\rm d}w=\alpha^{-1}(x+|y|)^{-\alpha}\Big[\Big(1-\frac{1/4}{x+|y|}\Big)^{-\alpha}-\Big(1+\frac{1/4}{x+|y|}\Big)^{-\alpha}\Big]

\displaystyle\approx\alpha^{-1}(x+|y|)^{-\alpha}\frac{1/4}{x+|y|}\approx|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}.

From the fact that
a\vee b\approx a+b
,
a,b\geq 0
, we also get that

\displaystyle E(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\,\,\begin{subarray}{c}|x-w|\geq 1/4\\[3.0pt]
w\geq 1/4\end{subarray}}\big(|x-w|\wedge|w-y|\big)^{-\alpha-1}\big(|x-w|\vee|w-y|\big)^{-\alpha-1}{\rm d}w

\displaystyle\approx\int\limits_{\,\,\begin{subarray}{c}|x-w|\geq 1/4\\[3.0pt]
w\geq 1/4\end{subarray}}\big(|x-w|\wedge|w-y|\big)^{-\alpha-1}\big(|x-w|+|w-y|\big)^{-\alpha-1}{\rm d}w

\displaystyle\leq|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\int\limits_{\,\,\begin{subarray}{c}|x-w|\geq 1/4\\[3.0pt]
w\geq 1/4\end{subarray}}\big(|x-w|\wedge|w-y|\big)^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}w

\displaystyle\approx|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}.

Hence, in case
x\geq 1/2
we obtain that

(4.10)

\displaystyle I_{1}(x,y)\approx|x|^{-\alpha-1}\big(|y|^{-\alpha-1}\wedge|y|^{-\alpha/2}\big)+|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}.

Let
|y|\geq 1
. It is obvious that
|x|\cdot|y|\approx\big(|x|\wedge|y|\big)\big(|x|+|y|\big)=\big(|x|\wedge|y|\big)|x-y|
and then from (
4.10
),

\displaystyle I_{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle\approx\big(|x|\cdot|y|\big)^{-\alpha-1}+|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\approx|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big[1+\big(|x|\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha-1}\big]

\displaystyle\approx|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}=\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

Now assume that
|y|<1
. Then
|x-y|=x+|y|\geq\tfrac{1}{2}|y|+|y|=\tfrac{3}{2}|y|
and
|x|=|x-y|-|y|\geq|x-y|-\tfrac{2}{3}|x-y|=\tfrac{1}{3}|x-y|
. Therefore, from (
4.10
),

\displaystyle I_{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle\approx|x|^{-\alpha-1}|y|^{-\alpha/2}+|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\approx|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big(1+|y|^{-\alpha/2}\big)\approx|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}|y|^{-\alpha/2}

\displaystyle=|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\approx\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

Hence, for
x\geq 1/2
,

(4.11)

\displaystyle I_{1}(x,y)\approx\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

From (
4.9
), (
4.11
) and (
1.9
) it follows that

\displaystyle I_{1}(x,y)\approx\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{1}(x,y),\qquad x>0,~y<0,

and then from (
4.4
) and (
1.8
), for
t>0
,

\displaystyle I_{t}(x,y)

\displaystyle=t^{-1/\alpha-1}I_{1}\big(t^{-1/\alpha}x,t^{-1/\alpha}y\big)

\displaystyle\approx t^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{t^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}t^{-1/\alpha}p_{1}\big(t^{-1/\alpha}x,t^{-1/\alpha}y\big)

\displaystyle=t^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{t^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{t}(x,y),

which completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 4.3
.

For
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
t>0
,
x>0
and
y<0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(t,x,y):=\int_{D}p_{t}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)\,{\rm d}z\approx t^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|}{t^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{\alpha/2}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{t^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{t}(x,y).

Proof.

It follows directly from (
1.25
), (
1.26
) and from Lemma
4.2
.
∎

Corollary 4.4
.

For
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
t>0
and
x>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{D}{\rm d}z\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,p_{t}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)\approx t^{-1}\left[\left(\frac{|x|}{t^{1/\alpha}}\right)^{-\alpha}\wedge\left(\frac{|x|}{t^{1/\alpha}}\right)^{\alpha/2}\right].

Proof.

From the Tonelli’s theorem and from Corollary
4.3
,

\displaystyle I(t,x)

\displaystyle:=\int_{D}{\rm d}z\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,p_{t}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)

(4.12)

\displaystyle\approx t^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|}{t^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{\alpha/2}\int_{D^{c}}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{t^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Hence, it suffices to find an estimation of
A(t,x):=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{t^{1/\alpha}}\big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y
. Note that by substitution
y=t^{1/\alpha}w
,

(4.13)

\displaystyle A(t,t^{1/\alpha}x)=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\big(1\wedge|w|\big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{1}(x,w)\,{\rm d}w=A(1,x).

Moreover, from (
1.9
),

\displaystyle A(1,x)

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|x+y|^{-\alpha-1}\big)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}|y|^{-\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|x+y|^{-\alpha-1}\big)\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{1}^{\infty}\big(1\wedge|x+y|^{-\alpha-1}\big)\,\mathrm{d}y.

For
|x|\leq 1
,

\displaystyle A(1,x)

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{1}|y|^{-\alpha/2}\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{1}^{\infty}|x+y|^{-\alpha-1}\,\mathrm{d}y\approx 1,

and for
|x|>1
,

\displaystyle A(1,x)

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{1}|y|^{-\alpha/2}|x+y|^{-\alpha-1}\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{1}^{\infty}|x+y|^{-\alpha-1}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=|x|^{-3\alpha/2}\int_{0}^{1/x}w^{-\alpha/2}(1+w)^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}w+|x|^{-\alpha}\int_{1/x}^{\infty}\frac{{\rm d}w}{(w+1)^{\alpha+1}}

\displaystyle\approx|x|^{-3\alpha/2}\int_{0}^{1/x}w^{-\alpha/2}\,{\rm d}w+|x|^{-\alpha}

\displaystyle\approx|x|^{-\alpha-1}+|x|^{-\alpha}\approx|x|^{-\alpha}.

Hence,

(4.14)

\displaystyle A(1,x)\approx 1\wedge|x|^{-\alpha},

and from (
4.13
),

\displaystyle A(t,x)=A\big(1,t^{-1/\alpha}x\big)\approx 1\wedge t|x|^{-\alpha}.

Combining this result with (
4.12
) we get

\displaystyle I(t,x)\approx t^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha}}{t}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(1\wedge\frac{t}{|x|^{\alpha}}\Big)=t^{-1}\big(t|x|^{-\alpha}\wedge t^{-1/2}|x|^{\alpha/2}\big),

which is our claim.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

For
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
t>0
,
x>0
,
y<0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}s^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{s}}\Big)

\displaystyle\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{s^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{s}(x,y)e^{-\nu(y,D)(t-s)}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\approx\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)p_{t}(x,y).

Proof.

Let
I_{t}(x,y):=\int_{0}^{t}s^{-1}\big(1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{s}}\big)\big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{s^{1/\alpha}}\big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{s}(x,y)e^{-\nu(y,D)(t-s)}\,{\rm d}s
. By substitution
s=tu
and from (
1.8
) and (
1.16
), we get

(4.15)

\displaystyle I_{t}(t^{1/\alpha}x,t^{1/\alpha}y)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}s^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{\sqrt{t}|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{s}}\Big)\Big(1\wedge\frac{t^{1/\alpha}|y|}{s^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{s}(t^{1/\alpha}x,t^{1/\alpha}y)e^{-\nu(t^{1/\alpha}y,D)(t-s)}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=t^{-1/\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}u^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{u}}\Big)\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{u^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{u}(x,y)e^{-\nu(y,D)(1-u)}\,{\rm d}u

(4.16)

\displaystyle=t^{-1/\alpha}I_{1}(x,y).

Hence it suffices to find an estimation of
I_{1}(x,y)
.

Note that

(4.17)

\displaystyle I_{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1/2}\ldots{\rm d}u+\int_{1/2}^{1}\ldots{\rm d}u:=A(x,y)+B(x,y).

For
u\in[1/2,1)
, we have

\displaystyle u^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{u}}\Big)\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{u^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{u}(x,y)\approx\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{1}(x,y).

Furthermore, note that
1-e^{-\eta/2}\approx 1\wedge\eta
,
\eta\geq 0
. Indeed, for
\eta\geq 1
,
1-e^{-\eta/2}\approx 1=1\wedge\eta
. For
0\leq\eta<1
, there exists a constant
c\in(0,\eta)
such that
1-e^{-\eta/2}=\tfrac{\eta}{2}\big(1-\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-c}\eta\big)
. Then,
1-e^{-\eta/2}\approx\tfrac{\eta}{2}\approx 1\wedge\eta
, which follows from the inequality
1\geq 1-\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-c}\eta\geq 3/4
. From this observation, from (
1.17
), it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{1/2}^{1}e^{-\nu(y,D)(1-u)}\,{\rm d}u=\frac{1}{\nu(y,D)}\Big[1-e^{-\nu(y,D)/2}\Big]\approx\frac{1}{\nu(y,D)}\big(1\wedge\nu(y,D)\big)\approx\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{\alpha}.

Therefore,

(4.18)

\displaystyle B(x,y)\approx\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{\alpha/2}p_{1}(x,y).

For
u\in(0,1/2)
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(4.19)

\displaystyle e^{-\nu(y,D)(1-u)}\leq e^{-C|y|^{-\alpha}}\lesssim\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{\alpha+1}.

From Lemma
4.1
and (
4.19
),

\displaystyle A(x,y)\lesssim\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(|x-y|^{-1}\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big)\big(1\wedge|y|\big).

Note that for
|x-y|<1
obviously we have
|y|<1
and

\big(|x-y|^{-1}\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big)\big(1\wedge|y|\big)=\frac{|y|}{|x-y|}<1=\big(1\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

Similarly, for
|x-y|\geq 1
,

\big(|x-y|^{-1}\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big)\big(1\wedge|y|\big)=|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)\leq|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}=\big(1\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big).

Hence,

(4.20)

\displaystyle A(x,y)\lesssim\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big)\approx\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{\alpha/2}p_{1}(x,y).

From (
4.17
), (
4.18
) and (
4.20
) we get

(4.21)

\displaystyle I_{1}(x,y)\approx\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{\alpha/2}p_{1}(x,y).

and then from (
4.15
) and (
4.21
) and (
1.8
), for
t>0
,

\displaystyle I_{t}(x,y)

\displaystyle=t^{-1/\alpha}I_{1}\big(t^{-1/\alpha}x,t^{-1/\alpha}y\big)\approx\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)p_{t}(x,y),

which completes the proof.
∎

From Lemma
4.2
and Lemma
4.5
and (
1.27
) the following conclusion follows immediately.

Corollary 4.6
.

For
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
t>0
,
x>0
, and
y<0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(t,x,y):=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,p_{s}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)e^{-\nu(y,D)(t-s)}\approx\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)p_{t}(x,y).

Proof.

From (
1.25
) and (
1.26
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{s}}\Big)\Big(1\wedge\frac{|z|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{s}}\Big)p_{s}(x,z)\nu(z,y)e^{-\nu(y,D)(t-s)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\,\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{s}}\Big)e^{-\nu(y,D)(t-s)}\int_{D}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|z|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{s}}\Big)p_{s}(x,z)\nu(z,y)\,{\rm d}z.

From Lemma
4.2
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(t,x,y)\approx\int_{0}^{t}s^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{s}}\Big)\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{s^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{s}(x,y)e^{-\nu(y,D)(t-s)}\,{\rm d}s,

and further, from Lemma
4.5
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(t,x,y)\approx\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big)p_{t}(x,y),

which is our claim.
∎

Corollary 4.7
.

For
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
t>0
, and
x>0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(t,x):=\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}z\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,p_{s}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)e^{-\nu(y,D)(t-s)}\approx\left(\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\right)^{-2}\wedge\frac{|x|^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}.

Proof.

By substituting
s=tu
,
y=t^{1/\alpha}a
, and
z=t^{1/\alpha}b
,we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(t,t^{1/\alpha}x)=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}u\int_{D}{\rm d}b\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}a\,t^{1+2/\alpha}p_{tu}^{D}(t^{1/\alpha}x,t^{1/\alpha}b)\nu(t^{1/\alpha}b,t^{1/\alpha}a)e^{-t\nu(t^{1/\alpha}a,D)(1-u)}.

From (
1.20
) and (
1.16
),

(4.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(t,t^{1/\alpha}x)=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}u\int_{D}{\rm d}b\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}a\,p_{u}^{D}(x,b)\nu(b,a)e^{-\nu(a,D)(1-u)}=\mathcal{L}(1,x).

Hence, it suffices to find an estimation of
\mathcal{L}(1,x)
.

Note that from the Tonelli’s theorem and from Corollary
4.6
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(1,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\mathcal{K}(1,x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\approx\big(1\wedge|x|^{\alpha/2}\big)\int_{-\infty}^{0}\big(1\wedge|y|^{\alpha/2}\big)\big(1\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Moreover,

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle:=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\big(1\wedge|y|^{\alpha/2}\big)\big(1\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\approx\int_{-1}^{0}|y|^{\alpha/2}\big(1\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big)\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{-\infty}^{-1}\big(1\wedge|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\big)\,\mathrm{d}y.

For
|x|\leq 1
,

\displaystyle A\approx\int_{-1}^{0}|y|^{\alpha/2}\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{-\infty}^{-1}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,\mathrm{d}y\approx 1=|x|^{-\alpha}\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha},

and for
|x|>1
,

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\approx\int_{-1}^{0}|y|^{\alpha/2}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{-\infty}^{-1}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\int_{-\infty}^{0}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,\mathrm{d}y\approx|x|^{-\alpha}

\displaystyle=|x|^{-\alpha}\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha},

and

\displaystyle A\gtrsim\int_{-\infty}^{-1}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,\mathrm{d}y=\frac{1}{\alpha}(1+x)^{-\alpha}\geq\frac{2^{-\alpha}}{\alpha}x^{-\alpha}\approx|x|^{-\alpha}\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha}.

Hence,
A\approx|x|^{-\alpha}\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha}
.

Therefore, we obtain that

(4.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(1,x)\approx\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha/2}|x|^{-\alpha}\big(1\wedge|x|\big)^{\alpha}=|x|^{-\alpha}\wedge|x|^{\alpha/2}.

Hence, from (
4.22
) and (
4.23
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(t,x)=\mathcal{L}(1,t^{-1/\alpha}x)\approx t|x|^{-\alpha}\wedge t^{-1/2}|x|^{\alpha/2},

which is the desired conclusion.
∎

4.2.
Convergence of integrals

Lemma 4.8
.

For
x,y>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}=\nu(x,y).

Proof.

By the Hunt formula (
1.19
) and the Ikeda–Watanabe formula (
1.30
),

(4.24)

\displaystyle\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{t}(x,y)}{t}-\frac{1}{t}\mathbb{E}_{x}^{Y}\big[\tau_{D}<t;~p_{t-\tau_{D}}(Y_{\tau_{D}},y)\big].

From Pòlya
[
56
]
(see also Cygan et al.
[
29
]
),
\lim\limits_{t\to 0^{+}}p_{t}(x,y)/t=\nu(x,y)
. Moreover, it is obvious that
Y_{\tau_{D}}<0,
hence
|Y_{\tau_{D}}-y|>y
and from (
1.9
) we get

(4.25)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\mathbb{E}_{x}^{Y}\left[\tau_{D}<t;~p_{t-\tau_{D}}(Y_{\tau_{D}},y)\right]\lesssim\frac{1}{t}\mathbb{E}_{x}^{Y}\left[\tau_{D}<t;\frac{t-\tau_{D}}{|Y_{\tau_{D}}-y|^{\alpha+1}}\right]\leq|y|^{-1-\alpha}\,\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}\left(\tau_{D}<t\right)\to 0,

as
t\to 0^{+}
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

The function

(0,\infty)\times D\times\overline{D}^{c}\ni(t,x,y)\mapsto\mathcal{J}(t,x,y)=\int_{D}p_{t}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)\,{\rm d}z

is continuous.

Proof.

Let
g(t,x,y,z):=p_{t}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
and define
T=[\varepsilon,\infty)
,
K=[\varepsilon,\infty)
,
L=(-\infty,-\varepsilon]
.
We will show that for all
(t,x,y)\in T\times K\times L
and for any sequence
(t_{n},x_{n},y_{n})\subset T\times K\times L
such that
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}(t_{n},x_{n},y_{n})=(t,x,y)
, we have
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{J}(t_{n},x_{n},y_{n})=\mathcal{J}(t,x,y)
.

Note that
T\times K\times L\ni(t,x,y)\mapsto p_{t}^{D}(x,y)
is continuous. Therefore, the function
(t,x,y)\mapsto g(t,x,y,z)
,
z\in D
, is continuous on
T\times K\times L
, hence
g(t_{n},x_{n},y_{n},z)\to g(t,x,y,z)
as
n\to\infty
.
Furthermore, from (
1.27
) it follows that for
(t,x,y)\in T\times K\times L
and
z\in D
we have

\displaystyle|g(t,x,y,z)|

\displaystyle\lesssim t^{-1/\alpha}\nu(z,y)\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}|z+\varepsilon|^{-1-\alpha}.

Moreover,
\int_{D}|z+\varepsilon|^{-1-\alpha}\,{\rm d}z<\infty
, hence from the dominated convergence theorem we then obtain continuity of the function
\mathcal{J}
on
T\times K\times L
.

Since
\varepsilon>0
was chosen arbitrarily, we conclude that the function
\mathcal{J}
is continuous on
(0,\infty)\times D\times\overline{D}^{c}
.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

For
x>0
and
y<0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(t,x,y)=\int_{D}p_{t}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)\,{\rm d}z\to\nu(x,y),

as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and define
K=[\varepsilon,\infty)
,
L=(-\infty,-\varepsilon]
. Let
U,V\subset\mathbb{R}
,
U\neq V
, be the open sets such that
x\in K\subset U\subset V\subset D
. Let
\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\}
be a partition of unity for the sets
U^{c}
and
V
, i.e. for
i=1,2
, the functions
\alpha_{i}:D\to[0,1]
satisfy the following conditions:
\alpha_{i}\in C^{\infty}(D)
,
\supp{\alpha_{1}}\subseteq V
,
\supp{\alpha_{2}}\subseteq U^{c}\cap D
,
\alpha_{1}=1
on
U
,
\alpha_{2}=1
on
V^{c}\cap D
and
\alpha_{1}(x)+\alpha_{2}(x)=1
for
x\in D
.

For
z\in D
and
y\in L
, let
\varphi_{1}(z,y):=\alpha_{1}(z)\nu(z,y)
,
\varphi_{2}(z,y):=\alpha_{2}(z)\nu(z,y)
. Then
\supp{\varphi_{1}}\subseteq V
,
\supp{\varphi_{2}}\subseteq U^{c}\cap D
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(t,x,y)=\int_{D}p_{t}^{D}(x,z)\varphi_{1}(z,y)\,{\rm d}z+\int_{D}p_{t}^{D}(x,z)\varphi_{2}(z,y)\,{\rm d}z=:A(t,x,y)+B(t,x,y).

From the construction it follows that
\varphi_{1}(\cdot,y)\in C_{0}(D)
, hence from Lemma
1.5
,

\displaystyle A(t,x,y)=\int_{D}p_{t}^{D}(x,z)\varphi_{1}(z,y)\,{\rm d}z\to\varphi_{1}(x,y)=\nu(x,y),

as
t\to 0^{+}
.

We will show that
B(t,x,y)\to 0
, as
t\to 0^{+}
. From (
1.27
) we have

\begin{split}B(t,x,y)&=\int_{\supp{\varphi_{2}(\cdot,y)}}p_{t}^{D}(x,z)\varphi_{2}(z,y)\,{\rm d}z\\
&\leq\int_{U^{c}\cap D}p_{t}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)\,{\rm d}z\\
&\lesssim t\int_{U^{c}\cap D}|x-z|^{-\alpha-1}|z-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}z.\end{split}

Let
\rho:=\dist(K,U^{c}\cap D)>0
and
\eta:=\dist(L,U^{c}\cap D)
. Then,

\displaystyle B(t,x,y)\lesssim t\rho^{-\alpha-1}\eta^{-\alpha-1}|U^{c}\cap D|\to 0,

as
t\to 0^{+}
.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

For
x>0
,
y<0
, and
\mu\in\{0,1\}
, we have

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,p_{s}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)e^{-\mu\nu(y,D)(t-s)}=\nu(x,y).

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
K:=[\varepsilon,\infty)
,
L:=(-\infty,-\varepsilon]
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
t\leq 1
. Let
x\in K
and
y\in L
. From Lemma
4.9
and Lemma
4.10
, by putting
\mathcal{J}(0,x,y):=\nu(x,y)
, we obtain a continuous function
t\mapsto\mathcal{J}(t,x,y)
on the interval
[0,1]
. Hence, by the Mean Value Theorem it follows that there exists
c=c(t)\in[0,t]
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,p_{s}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)e^{-\mu\nu(y,D)(t-s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{J}(s,x,y)e^{-\mu\nu(y,D)(t-s)}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}(c(t),x,y)e^{-\mu\nu(y,D)(t-c(t))}.

Therefore, from Lemma
4.10
,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,p_{s}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)e^{-\mu\nu(y,D)(t-s)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{J}(c(t),x,y)e^{-\mu\nu(y,D)(t-c(t))}

\displaystyle=\nu(x,y),

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

For
x>0
, we have

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\mathbb{P}_{x}^{Y}(\tau_{D}<t)=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,p_{s}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,D^{c})=\nu(x,D^{c}).

Proof.

The first equality follows from (
1.30
). We will prove the latter equality.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
t\leq 1
. Note that from the Tonelli’s theorem,

\displaystyle A(t,x):=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,p_{s}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,D^{c})=\int_{D^{c}}\left[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{J}(s,x,y)\,{\rm d}s\right]\mathrm{d}y.

By a similar argument as in the proof of Lemma
4.11
, we conclude that there exists
c=c(t)\in[0,t]
, such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{J}(s,x,y)\,{\rm d}s=\mathcal{J}(c(t),x,y).

From Corollary
4.3
and (
1.9
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(c(t),x,y)

\displaystyle\lesssim(c(t))^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|y|}{(c(t))^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{c(t)}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq(c(t))^{-1}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}p_{c(t)}(x,y)

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\int_{D^{c}}\big(1\wedge|y|\big)^{-\alpha/2}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\big(1\wedge y\big)^{-\alpha/2}(x+y)^{-\alpha-1}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{y^{\alpha/2}(x+y)^{\alpha+1}}+\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{(x+y)^{\alpha+1}}<\infty.

Hence, we use the dominated convergence theorem to obtain that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}A(t,x)=\int_{D^{c}}\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{J}(c(t),x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\nu(x,D^{c}),

where the latter convergence follows from Lemma
4.10
.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,2)
. Assume that
f:\mathbb{R}_{*}\to[0,\infty)
is a function such that for some
x\neq 0
we have
\widehat{\nu}f(x)<\infty
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}K_{t,1}f(x)=\widehat{\nu}f(x).

Proof.

Assume that
x>0
. Note that from the Tonelli’s theorem,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}K_{t,1}f(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}z\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,p_{s}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)e^{-\nu(y,D)(t-s)}f(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{c}}\Big[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,p_{s}^{D}(x,z)\nu(z,y)e^{-\nu(y,D)(t-s)}\Big]f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{c}}t^{-1}\mathcal{K}(t,x,y)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

From Corollary
4.6
and (
1.9
),
t^{-1}\mathcal{K}(t,x,y)\lesssim t^{-1}p_{t}(x,y)\lesssim\nu(x,y)
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\int_{D^{c}}\nu(x,y)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\widehat{\nu}f(x)<\infty.

Therefore, we can use the dominated convergence theorem to obtain that from Lemma
4.11
with
\mu=1
we get

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}K_{t,1}f(x)=\int_{D^{c}}\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}t^{-1}\mathcal{K}(t,x,y)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\int_{D^{c}}\nu(x,y)f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\widehat{\nu}f(x).

The case
x<0
we prove in the same way, but we propose it for the convenience of the reader. Assume that
x<0
. From the Tonelli’s theorem,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}K_{t,1}f(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,e^{-\nu(x,D)r}\nu(x,y)p_{t-r}^{D}(y,z)f(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\left[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,p_{t-r}^{D}(z,y)\nu(y,x)e^{-\nu(x,D)r}\right]f(z)\,{\rm d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\left[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,p_{s}^{D}(z,y)\nu(y,x)e^{-\nu(x,D)(t-s)}\right]f(z)\,{\rm d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{D}t^{-1}\mathcal{K}(t,z,x)f(z)\,{\rm d}z.

From Corollary
4.6
and (
1.9
),
t^{-1}\mathcal{K}(t,z,x)\lesssim t^{-1}p_{t}(z,x)\lesssim\nu(z,x)
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\int_{D}\nu(z,x)f(z)\,{\rm d}z=\widehat{\nu}f(x)<\infty.

Therefore, we can use the dominated convergence theorem to obtain that from Lemma
4.11
with
\mu=1
,

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}K_{t,1}f(x)=\int_{D}\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}t^{-1}\mathcal{K}(t,z,x)f(z)\,{\rm d}z=\int_{D}\nu(z,x)f(z)\,{\rm d}z=\widehat{\nu}f(x),

which is our conclusion.
∎

Corollary 4.14
.

For
\alpha\in(0,2)
and
\beta\in(-1,\alpha)
, we have

(4.26)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}K_{t,1}h_{\beta}(x)=\widehat{\nu}h_{\beta}(x),\qquad x\neq 0,

Proof.

Note that for
x>0
we have

\widehat{\nu}h_{\beta}(x)\approx\int_{D^{c}}\frac{|y|^{\beta}}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y=x^{\beta-\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{w^{\beta}}{(1+w)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}w=x^{\beta-\alpha}\mathfrak{B}(\beta+1,\alpha-\beta)<\infty.

Similarly, for
x<0
,
\widehat{\nu}h_{\beta}(x)<\infty
. Therefore, we use Lemma
4.13
to get (
4.26
).
∎

4.3.
The main theorem

Now we introduce the main result of this section.

Proposition 4.15
.

Assume that
x\neq 0
,
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
\beta(\alpha-\beta-1)\geq 0
and let

\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta,x):=\begin{cases}\alpha^{-1}-\mathfrak{B}(\beta+1,\alpha-\beta)-\gamma(\alpha,\beta),&x>0,\\
\alpha^{-1}-\mathfrak{B}(\beta+1,\alpha-\beta),&x<0,\end{cases}

where

\gamma(\alpha,\beta):=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(t^{\beta}-1)(1-t^{\alpha-\beta-1})}{(1-t)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}t\leq 0.

Then
\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta,x)\geq 0
and

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{h_{\beta}(x)-K_{t}h_{\beta}(x)}{t}=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta,x)h_{\beta}(x)|x|^{-\alpha}.

Moreover,
\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta,x)=0
if and only if
\beta=0
or
\beta=\alpha-1
.

To prove this theorem, we first have to find the analogous result for the
\alpha
-stable killed Lévy process
Y
. Below, we prove it for a wider class of functions.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,2)
. If
f\in C^{2}(D)
satisfies

\int_{D}\frac{|f(y)|}{(1+y)^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty,

then for
x>0
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{f(x)-P^{D}_{t}f(x)}{t}=\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{D}(f(x)-f(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y+f(x)\nu(x,D^{c}).

Proof.

Let
t>0
. Note that

(4.27)

\displaystyle\frac{f(x)-P^{D}_{t}f(x)}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\int_{D}(f(x)-f(y))p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y+f(x)\frac{1-p_{t}^{D}(x,D)}{t}.

From (
1.30
) we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1-p_{t}^{D}(x,D)}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\mathbb{P}^{Y}_{x}(\tau_{D}<t)=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,p_{s}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,D^{c}).

Hence, from Lemma
4.12
,

(4.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1-p_{t}^{D}(x,D)}{t}=\nu(x,D^{c}).

Combining (
4.27
) and (
4.28
) we obtain the following equality

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{f(x)-P^{D}_{t}f(x)}{t}=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{D}(f(x)-f(y))\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}y+f(x)\nu(x,D^{c}),

with the assumption that the latter limit exists—we will show it in the further part of the proof. We will find this limit in a few steps.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,x/2]
be an arbitrary constant. Then we write that

\displaystyle\int_{D}(f(x)-f(y))\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}(f(x)-f(y))\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|\geq\varepsilon\}}(f(x)-f(y))\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}y

(4.29)

\displaystyle=:I_{t}+II_{t}.

Note that from (
1.27
) it follows that

\displaystyle|f(x)-f(y)|\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}\lesssim|f(x)-f(y)|\cdot|x-y|^{-\alpha-1},

and

\displaystyle\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|\geq\varepsilon\}}|f(x)-f(y)|\cdot|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,\mathrm{d}y

(4.30)

\displaystyle\leq|f(x)|\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|\geq\varepsilon\}}|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|\geq\varepsilon\}}\frac{|f(y)|}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y.

Moreover, for
|x-y|\geq\varepsilon
there exists a constant
\widehat{C}=\widehat{C}(x,\varepsilon)>0
, such that
\widehat{C}(y+1)\leq|x-y|
.
To prove it we consider two cases. Let
y\leq x-\varepsilon
. Then for
C_{1}:=\frac{\varepsilon}{x+1}
we have
C_{1}<\frac{\varepsilon}{x+1-\varepsilon}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{y+1}\leq\frac{x-y}{y+1}
, which gives us the inequality
C_{1}(y+1)<|x-y|
. In case
y\geq x+\varepsilon
we set
C_{2}:=\frac{\varepsilon}{1+3x/2}
and then we observe that
C_{2}<\frac{\varepsilon}{1+x+\varepsilon}=\frac{1-\frac{x}{x+\varepsilon}}{1+\frac{1}{x+\varepsilon}}\leq\frac{1-\frac{x}{y}}{1+\frac{1}{y}}=\frac{y-x}{y+1}
. This gives us
C_{2}(y+1)<|x-y|
. Thus, the claim follows by taking
\widehat{C}:=C_{1}\wedge C_{2}
.

Furthermore, note that from the above observations we get that
