\displaystyle\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|\geq\varepsilon\}}\frac{|f(y)|}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\widehat{C}^{-1-\alpha}\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|\geq\varepsilon\}}\frac{|f(y)|}{(1+y)^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

Hence, the integral of the left-hand side of (
4.30
) is finite. Therefore, from the dominated convergence theorem and by Lemma
4.8
,

(4.31)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}II_{t}=\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|\geq\varepsilon\}}(f(x)-f(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

For now, we consider the integral
I_{t}
. From the Hunt formula (
1.19
) we get

\displaystyle I_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}(f(x)-f(y))\frac{p_{t}(x,y)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}y-\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}(f(x)-f(y))\mathcal{H}(t,x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

(4.32)

\displaystyle=:A_{t}-B_{t},

where
\mathcal{H}(t,x,y):=t^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{x}^{Y}\big[\tau_{D}<t;~p_{t-\tau_{D}}(Y_{\tau_{D}},y)\big]
.
From (
4.25
) it follows that

\displaystyle|f(x)-f(y)|\mathcal{H}(t,x,y)

\displaystyle\lesssim|f(x)-f(y)|\cdot|y|^{-\alpha-1}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{|y|^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y

(4.33)

\displaystyle\leq|f(x)|\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{|y|^{\alpha+1}}+\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}\frac{|f(y)|}{|y|^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y.

Note that for
|x-y|<\varepsilon
there exists a constant
\widetilde{C}=\widetilde{C}(x)>0
such that
1+y\leq\widetilde{C}y
. Indeed, let
\widetilde{C}:=\frac{2}{x}+4
. Then
\frac{3x}{2}<\frac{\widetilde{C}x}{2}-1
. Recall that
\varepsilon\leq\frac{x}{2}
. Then, from the assumption, it is obvious that
\frac{x}{2}<y<\frac{3x}{2}
and then
\widetilde{C}^{-1}(1+y)<\widetilde{C}^{-1}\big(1+\frac{3x}{2}\big)<\frac{x}{2}<y
, which is our claim.

Furthermore, from the above result we obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}\frac{|f(y)|}{|y|^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y\approx\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}\frac{|f(y)|}{(1+y)^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

Hence, the integral of the left-hand side of (
4.33
) is finite. Hence, from the dominated convergence theorem and from (
4.25
),

(4.34)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}B_{t}=\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}(f(x)-f(y))\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{H}(t,x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y=0.

Now we consider the integral
A_{t}
. Let
t>0
be small enough that
t^{1/\alpha}<\varepsilon
. Note that by the symmetry of
p_{t}
,

\displaystyle\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}(y-x)p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{x}^{x+\varepsilon}(y-x)p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{x-\varepsilon}^{x}(y-x)p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{x}^{x+\varepsilon}(y-x)p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{x}^{x+\varepsilon}(x-w)p_{t}(x,2x-w)\,{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\int_{x}^{x+\varepsilon}(y-x)p_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y-\int_{x}^{x+\varepsilon}(w-x)p_{t}(x,w)\,{\rm d}w=0.

Hence,

\displaystyle|A_{t}|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}(f(x)-f(y))\frac{p_{t}(x,y)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}y\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}\big(f(y)-f(x)-(y-x)f^{\prime}(x)\big)\frac{p_{t}(x,y)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}y\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}\big|f(y)-f(x)-(y-x)f^{\prime}(x)\big|\frac{p_{t}(x,y)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}y.

From the Taylor’s theorem,

\displaystyle f(y)-f(x)-(y-x)f^{\prime}(x)=\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(c)}{2}(y-x)^{2},

where
c
lies strictly between
x
and
y
. From the fact that
\varepsilon\leq x/2
, it follows that
x-\varepsilon\geq x/2
. Hence, for
|x-y|<\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle\big|f(y)-f(x)-(y-x)f^{\prime}(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{c\in(x-\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon)}|f^{\prime\prime}(c)||y-x|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{c\in(x/2,3x/2)}|f^{\prime\prime}(c)||y-x|^{2}=:C(x)|y-x|^{2}.

Hence,

\displaystyle|A_{t}|\leq C(x)\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}|y-x|^{2}\frac{p_{t}(x,y)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}y.

From (
1.9
), there exists a constant
C_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle|A_{t}|

\displaystyle\leq C(x)C_{0}\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}|y-x|^{2}\frac{1}{t}\Big(t^{-1/\alpha}\wedge\frac{t}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=C(x)C_{0}t^{-1/\alpha-1}\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<t^{1/\alpha}\}}|y-x|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}y+C(x)C_{0}\int_{D\cap\{t^{1/\alpha}\leq|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}|y-x|^{1-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{2}{3}C(x)C_{0}t^{-1/\alpha-1}t^{3/\alpha}+C(x)C_{0}\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<\varepsilon\}}|y-x|^{1-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=C(x)C_{0}\big[\tfrac{2}{3}t^{2/\alpha-1}+\tfrac{2}{2-\alpha}\varepsilon^{2-\alpha}\big].

Using the above observations, we conclude that

(4.35)

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}|A_{t}|\leq\tfrac{2}{2-\alpha}C(x)C_{0}\,\varepsilon^{2-\alpha}=:\widehat{C}(x,\alpha)\varepsilon^{2-\alpha}.

Combining (
4.32
), (
4.34
) and (
4.35
) we get

(4.36)

\displaystyle\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}I_{t}\geq\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}A_{t}-\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}B_{t}=\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}A_{t}\geq-\widehat{C}(x,\alpha)\varepsilon^{2-\alpha},

and

(4.37)

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}I_{t}\leq\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}A_{t}-\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}B_{t}=\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}A_{t}\leq\widehat{C}(x,\alpha)\varepsilon^{2-\alpha}.

Hence, from (
4.29
), (
4.31
) and (
4.36
),

\displaystyle\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{D}(f(x)-f(y))\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}y

(4.38)

\displaystyle\geq-\widehat{C}(x,\alpha)\varepsilon^{2-\alpha}+\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|\geq\varepsilon\}}(f(x)-f(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

We will show that there exists the limit

\displaystyle\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{D}(f(x)-f(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|\geq\varepsilon\}}(f(x)-f(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

The existence follows from the dominated convergence theorem. Indeed, by the same argument as in the previous part of the proof we know that

\displaystyle\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|\geq\varepsilon\}}(f(x)-f(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\ind_{\{|x-y|\geq\varepsilon\}}\big(f(x)-f(y)-\ind_{B(x,x/2)}(y)(y-x)f^{\prime}(x)\big)\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Furthermore, from the Taylor’s theorem we get

\displaystyle\ind_{\{|x-y|\geq\varepsilon\}}\big|f(x)-f(y)-\ind_{B(x,x/2)}(y)(y-x)f^{\prime}(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\ind_{B(x,x/2)^{c}}(y)\big(|f(x)|+|f(y)|\big)+\ind_{B(x,x/2)}(y)\sup_{y\in B(x,x/2)}|f^{\prime\prime}(y)||y-x|^{2}

\displaystyle=:\ind_{B(x,x/2)^{c}}(y)\big(|f(x)|+|f(y)|\big)+\ind_{B(x,x/2)}(y)C(x)|y-x|^{2},

and then

\displaystyle\int_{D\cap B(x,x/2)^{c}}\frac{|f(x)|+|f(y)|}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y+C(x)\int_{B(x,x/2)}|y-x|^{1-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}y<\infty.

Note that the finiteness of the above expression follows from the previous part of the proof. Then from the dominated convergence theorem we obtain that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|\geq\varepsilon\}}(f(x)-f(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\big(f(x)-f(y)-\ind_{B(x,x/2)}(y)(y-x)f^{\prime}(x)\big)\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y,

and in particular the above limit exists.
Hence, by taking
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
in (
4.38
), we get

\displaystyle\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{D}(f(x)-f(y))\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}y\geq\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{D}(f(x)-f(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Similarly, from (
4.29
), (
4.31
) and (
4.37
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{D}(f(x)-f(y))\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{D}(f(x)-f(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{D}(f(x)-f(y))\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}y=\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{D}(f(x)-f(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y,

which ends the proof.
∎

We note in passing that if the pointwise formula in Lemma
4.16
gives a
C_{0}(D)
function, then
f
is in the domain of the Dirichlet fractional Laplacian on
D
. This is true more generally, see
[
2
, Theorem 2.3]
.

In what follows, however, we focus on the functions
h_{\beta}
.

Corollary 4.17
.

For
x>0
,
\alpha\in(0,2)
, and
\beta\in(-1,\alpha)
, we have

(4.39)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{h_{\beta}(x)-P^{D}_{t}h_{\beta}(x)}{t}=\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{D}(h_{\beta}(x)-h_{\beta}(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y+h_{\beta}(x)\nu(x,D^{c}).

Lemma 4.18
.

For
\alpha\in(0,2)
and
\beta(\alpha-\beta-1)\geq 0
,

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}K_{t,n}h_{\beta}(x)=0,\qquad x\neq 0.

Proof.

First, assume that
x>0
. Then from Corollary
2.4
it follows that

(4.40)

\displaystyle K_{t}=\widehat{P}_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{t-r}\,{\rm d}r+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{r}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{s-r}\widehat{\nu}K_{t-s}\,{\rm d}s\,{\rm d}r,

and from Corollary
2.20
we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}K_{t,n}h_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{r}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}\widehat{P}_{s-r}\widehat{\nu}K_{t-s}h_{\beta}(x)\,{\rm d}s\,{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\int_{r}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}v\,p_{r}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)e^{-\nu(z,D)(s-r)}\nu(z,v)K_{t-s}h_{\beta}(v)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\int_{r}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}{\rm d}v\,p_{r}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)e^{-\nu(z,D)(s-r)}\nu(z,v)h_{\beta}(v).

Direct calculations show that for
z<0
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(z,v)h_{\beta}(v)\,{\rm d}v\approx\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{v^{\beta}}{|z-v|^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}v=|z|^{\beta-\alpha}\mathfrak{B}(\beta+1,\alpha-\beta)\approx|z|^{\beta-\alpha}.

Therefore, from (
1.17
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}K_{t,n}h_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\int_{r}^{t}{\rm d}s\,p_{r}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)e^{-\nu(z,D)(s-r)}|z|^{\beta-\alpha}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,p_{r}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)|z|^{\beta}\big[1-e^{-\nu(z,D)(t-r)}\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,p_{r}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)|z|^{\beta}\big(1\wedge t|z|^{-\alpha}\big).

Using the substitution
r=tu
we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}K_{t,n}h_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}u\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,p_{tu}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)|z|^{\beta}\big(1\wedge t|z|^{-\alpha}\big),

and by (
1.27
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}K_{t,n}h_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}u\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,\Big(1\wedge\frac{y^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{tu}}\Big)\Big((tu)^{-1/\alpha}\wedge\frac{tu}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\Big)\nu(y,z)|z|^{\beta}\big(1\wedge t|z|^{-\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}u\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<(tu)^{1/\alpha}\}}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,(tu)^{-1/\alpha}\frac{|z|^{\beta}}{|y-z|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|z|^{-\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}u\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|\geq(tu)^{1/\alpha}\}}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,\Big(1\wedge\frac{y^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{tu}}\Big)\frac{tu}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\frac{|z|^{\beta}}{|y-z|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|z|^{-\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle=:I_{t}+II_{t}.

If
t
is sufficiently small compared with
x
in
I_{t}
, then
|z-x|\lesssim|z-y|
and

\displaystyle I_{t}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}u\int_{D\cap\{|x-y|<(tu)^{1/\alpha}\}}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,(tu)^{-1/\alpha}\frac{|z|^{\beta}}{|x-z|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|z|^{-\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}u\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,\frac{|z|^{\beta}}{|x-z|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|z|^{-\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{c}}\frac{|z|^{\beta}}{|x-z|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|z|^{-\alpha}\big)\,{\rm d}z,

and the latter integral converges to
0
as
t\to 0^{+}
from the dominated convergence theorem. Indeed,
\big(1\wedge t|z|^{-\alpha}\big)\leq 1
and

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{|z|^{\beta}}{|x-z|^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}z

\displaystyle=x^{\beta-\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{w^{\beta}}{(w+1)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}w=x^{\beta-\alpha}\,\mathfrak{B}(\beta+1,\alpha-\beta)<\infty.

It remains to show that
II_{t}\to 0
as
t\to 0^{+}
. We will show that in a few steps.

The integrand in
II_{t}
is non-negative, so from the Tonelli’s theorem, we can change the order of integrals. Hence, for now, we consider only integral over
u
. We have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\ind_{\{|x-y|\geq(tu)^{1/\alpha}\}}\Big(1\wedge\frac{y^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{tu}}\Big)u\,{\rm d}u=\int_{0}^{1\wedge(|x-y|^{\alpha}/t)}\Big(1\wedge\frac{y^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{tu}}\Big)u\,{\rm d}u.

Here we consider two cases. If
y\geq x/2
then
y\geq|x-y|
and hence
\frac{y^{\alpha}}{t}\geq 1\wedge\frac{|x-y|^{\alpha}}{t}\geq u
. This means that
\frac{y^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{tu}}\geq 1
, so

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1\wedge(|x-y|^{\alpha}/t)}\Big(1\wedge\frac{y^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{tu}}\Big)u\,{\rm d}u=\int_{0}^{1\wedge(|x-y|^{\alpha}/t)}u\,{\rm d}u=\frac{1}{2}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x-y|^{\alpha}}{t}\Big)^{2}.

If
y\in(0,x/2)
then similarly we have that
\frac{y^{\alpha}}{t}<\frac{|x-y|^{\alpha}}{t}
. Hence, for sufficiently small
t
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1\wedge(|x-y|^{\alpha}/t)}\Big(1\wedge\frac{y^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{tu}}\Big)u\,{\rm d}u

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1\wedge(y^{\alpha}/t)}u\,{\rm d}u+\int_{1\wedge(y^{\alpha}/t)}^{1\wedge(|x-y|^{\alpha}/t)}\frac{y^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\sqrt{u}\,{\rm d}u

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big(1\wedge\frac{y^{\alpha}}{t}\Big)^{2}+\frac{2}{3}\frac{y^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\Big[\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x-y|^{\alpha}}{t}\Big)^{3/2}-\Big(1\wedge\frac{y^{\alpha}}{t}\Big)^{3/2}\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(1\wedge\frac{y^{\alpha}}{t}\Big)^{2}+\Big(\frac{y^{\alpha}}{t}\Big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{y^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}.

Now we consider again the integral
II_{t}
. Using previous observations, we get

\displaystyle II_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}u\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,\ind_{\{|x-y|\geq(tu)^{1/\alpha}\}}\Big(1\wedge\frac{y^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{tu}}\Big)\frac{tu}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\frac{|z|^{\beta}}{|y-z|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|z|^{-\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{x/2}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,\frac{y^{\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{t}}\frac{t}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\frac{|z|^{\beta}}{|y-z|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|z|^{-\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle+\int_{x/2}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x-y|^{\alpha}}{t}\Big)^{2}\frac{t}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\frac{|z|^{\beta}}{|y-z|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|z|^{-\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle=:A_{t}+B_{t}.

For
y\in(0,x/2)
, it is obvious that
|x-y|\approx x
and then

\displaystyle A_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{t}\int_{0}^{x/2}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,\frac{y^{\alpha/2}}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\frac{|z|^{\beta}}{|y-z|^{\alpha+1}}

\displaystyle\approx x^{-\alpha-1}\sqrt{t}\int_{0}^{x/2}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,y^{\alpha/2}\frac{|z|^{\beta}}{|y-z|^{\alpha+1}}

\displaystyle\approx x^{-\alpha-1}\sqrt{t}\int_{0}^{x/2}y^{\beta-\alpha/2}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim x^{\beta-3\alpha/2}\sqrt{t}\to 0,

as
t\to 0^{+}
.

Since, for
y>x/2
and
z<0
, we have
|y-z|>|x/2-z|=\tfrac{1}{2}|x-2z|
, then by substitution
w=2z
we get

\displaystyle B_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{x/2}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,\Big(1\wedge\frac{|x-y|^{\alpha}}{t}\Big)^{2}\frac{t}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\frac{|z|^{\beta}}{|y-z|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|z|^{-\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{|x-y|\leq t^{1/\alpha}}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}w\,\frac{|x-y|^{\alpha-1}}{t}\frac{|w|^{\beta}}{|x-w|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|w|^{-\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle+\int_{\{y\geq x/2\}\cap\{|x-y|>t^{1/\alpha}\}}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}w\frac{t}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\frac{|w|^{\beta}}{|x-w|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|w|^{-\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle=:C_{t}+D_{t}.

Note that from Tonelli’s theorem we get

\displaystyle C_{t}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{D^{c}}\frac{|w|^{\beta}}{|x-w|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|w|^{-\alpha}\big)\,{\rm d}w\to 0,

as
t\to 0^{+}
, which follows in the same way as in the case of
I_{t}
. For the integral
D_{t}
, again from the Tonelli’s theorem, we have

\displaystyle D_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{\{y\geq x/2\}\cap\{|x-y|>t^{1/\alpha}\}}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}w\,\frac{t}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\frac{|w|^{\beta}}{|x-w|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|w|^{-\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{D^{c}}\frac{t|w|^{\beta}}{|x-w|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|w|^{-\alpha}\big)\int_{\{|x-y|>t^{1/\alpha}\}}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{c}}\frac{t|w|^{\beta}}{|x-w|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|w|^{-\alpha}\big)\int_{\{|u|>t^{1/\alpha}\}}\frac{{\rm d}u}{|u|^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}w

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\alpha}\int_{D^{c}}\frac{|w|^{\beta}}{|x-w|^{\alpha+1}}\big(1\wedge t|w|^{-\alpha}\big)\,{\rm d}w\to 0,

as
t\to 0^{+}
. The latter convergence follows from the dominated convergence theorem.

Now let
x<0
. From Lemma
2.3
we have the following equality:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}K_{t,n}h_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{P}_{r}\widehat{\nu}K_{t-r,n}h_{\beta}(x)\,{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\,{\rm d}r\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,e^{-\nu(x,D)r}\nu(x,z)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{t-r,n}h_{\beta}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\,{\rm d}r\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,e^{-\nu(x,D)tr}\nu(x,z)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{t(1-r),n}h_{\beta}(z)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\,{\rm d}r\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,\nu(x,z)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{t(1-r),n}h_{\beta}(z).

From the first part of the proof, we know that
\lim\limits_{t\to 0^{+}}\tfrac{1}{t}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}K_{t,n}h_{\beta}(z)=0
, where
z>0
. Hence, of course,
\lim\limits_{t\to 0^{+}}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}K_{t,n}h_{\beta}(z)=0
. Moreover, from Corollary
4.14
it follows that
\lim\limits_{t\to 0^{+}}K_{t,1}h_{\beta}(z)=0
. Combining this results, for
z>0
, we get
\lim\limits_{t\to 0^{+}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{t,n}h_{\beta}(z)=0
. From Corollary
2.20
it follows that

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{t(1-r),n}h_{\beta}(z)\leq K_{t(1-r)}h_{\beta}(z)\leq h_{\beta}(z)
and we observe that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\,{\rm d}r\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,\nu(x,z)h_{\beta}(z)\approx\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{\beta}}{|x-z|^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}z=x^{\beta-\alpha}\mathfrak{B}(\beta+1,\alpha-\beta)<\infty.

Therefore, by the dominated convergence theorem,

\int_{0}^{1}\,{\rm d}r\int_{D}{\rm d}z\,\nu(x,z)\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{t(1-r),n}h_{\beta}(z)\to 0,

as
t\to 0^{+}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.15
.

Let
\mathcal{G}(t,x):=(h_{\beta}(x)-K_{t}h_{\beta}(x))/t
and assume that
x>0
. Then from Corollary
4.17
,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{G}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{h_{\beta}(x)-\widehat{P}_{t}h_{\beta}(x)}{t}-\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{t,n}h_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{D}(h_{\beta}(x)-h_{\beta}(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y+h_{\beta}(x)\nu(x,D^{c})-\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{t,n}h_{\beta}(x).

From Corollary
4.14
and from Lemma
4.18
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}K_{t,n}h_{\beta}(x)=\int_{D^{c}}h_{\beta}(y)\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Therefore,

(4.41)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{G}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{D}(h_{\beta}(x)-h_{\beta}(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{D^{c}}(h_{\beta}(x)-h_{\beta}(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(h_{\beta}(x)-h_{\beta}(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Now we will calculate the exact values of the above integrals. Using the substitution
y=|x|z
we obtain,

\displaystyle\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{D}(h_{\beta}(x)-h_{\beta}(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}|x|^{\beta-\alpha}\,\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1-z^{\beta}}{|1-z|^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}z.

From Section 5 of
[
11
]
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1-z^{\beta}}{|1-z|^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}z=-\gamma(\alpha,\beta)\geq 0.

Hence,

(4.42)

\displaystyle\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{D}(h_{\beta}(x)-h_{\beta}(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y=-\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}|x|^{\beta-\alpha}\gamma(\alpha,\beta).

Moreover, from (
1.18
),

\displaystyle\int_{D^{c}}(h_{\beta}(x)-h_{\beta}(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=h_{\beta}(x)\nu(x,D^{c})-\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{|y|^{\beta}}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y

(4.43)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}|x|^{\beta-\alpha}\big[\alpha^{-1}-\mathfrak{B}(\beta+1,\alpha-\beta)\big].

From (
4.41
), (
4.42
) and (
4.43
) we get

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{G}(t,x)=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}|x|^{\beta-\alpha}\big[\alpha^{-1}-\mathfrak{B}(\beta+1,\alpha-\beta)-\gamma(\alpha,\beta)\big].

Now consider the case
x<0
. Then,

\mathcal{G}(t,x)=h_{\beta}(x)\frac{1-e^{-\nu(x,D)t}}{t}-\frac{1}{t}K_{t,1}h_{\beta}(x)-\frac{1}{t}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}K_{t,n}h_{\beta}(x).

From Corollary
4.14
and Lemma
4.18
we obtain that

(4.44)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{G}(t,x)=\int_{D}(h_{\beta}(x)-h_{\beta}(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Moreover, from (
1.17
),

\displaystyle\int_{D}(h_{\beta}(x)-h_{\beta}(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=h_{\beta}(x)\nu(x,D)-\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{y^{\beta}}{|x-y|^{\alpha+1}}\,\mathrm{d}y

(4.45)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}|x|^{\beta-\alpha}\big[\alpha^{-1}-\mathfrak{B}(\beta+1,\alpha-\beta)\big].

Hence,

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{G}(t,x)=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}|x|^{\beta-\alpha}\big[\alpha^{-1}-\mathfrak{B}(\beta+1,\alpha-\beta)\big].

Now, it remains to show that
\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta,x)>0
for
\beta(\alpha-\beta-1)>0
. From
[
11
, (5.3)]
it follows that
\gamma(\alpha,\beta)<0
. Hence, it suffices to show that for
\beta(\alpha-\beta-1)>0
,
\mathcal{M}(\alpha,\beta):=\alpha^{-1}-\mathfrak{B}(\beta+1,\alpha-\beta)>0
.
From the properties of the Beta function we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\alpha,\beta)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha+1)}\big[\Gamma(\alpha)-\Gamma(\beta+1)\Gamma(\alpha-\beta)\big].

We will consider two cases.

Assume that
1<\alpha<2
and
0<\beta<\alpha-1
and define a function
(0,\alpha-1)\ni\beta\mapsto F(\beta):=\Gamma(\alpha)-\Gamma(\beta+1)\Gamma(\alpha-\beta)
. Then

F^{\prime}(\beta)=\Gamma(\beta+1)\Gamma(\alpha-\beta)\big[\psi(\alpha-\beta)-\psi(\beta+1)\big],

where
\psi
denotes the digamma function. From the fact that the digamma function is increasing on
(0,\infty)
, we obtain that for
\beta\in(0,(\alpha-1)/2)
,
F^{\prime}(\beta)>0
and for
\beta\in((\alpha-1)/2,\alpha-1)
,
F^{\prime}(\beta)<0
. Hence, for
\beta=(\alpha-1)/2
the function
F
has the global maximum on the interval
(0,\alpha-1)
.
Moreover, for
\beta\in(0,\alpha-1)
,
F(\beta)>F(0)=F(\alpha-1)=0
. Thus,
\mathcal{M}(\alpha,\beta)>0
.

Now, assume that
0<\alpha<1
and
\alpha-1<\beta<0
. Using the same notation as in the previous case we get that for
\beta\in(\alpha-1,(\alpha-1)/2)
,
F^{\prime}(\beta)>0
and for
\beta\in((\alpha-1)/2,0)
,
F^{\prime}(\beta)<0
. And then we conclude that again for
\beta=(\alpha-1)/2
the function
F
has the global maximum on the interval
(\alpha-1,0)
and moreover,
F(\beta)>0
for
\beta\in(\alpha-1,0)
. Hence,
\mathcal{M}(\alpha,\beta)>0
.
∎

Corollary 4.19
.

If
f\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
and
|f(x)|\lesssim\left(1+|x|^{\alpha-1}\right)
on
\mathbb{R}_{*}
, then

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{f(x)-K_{t}f(x)}{t}=\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(f(x)-f(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad x>0,

and

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{f(x)-K_{t}f(x)}{t}=\int_{D}(f(x)-f(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad x<0.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
4.18
and the proof of Proposition
4.15
, the result holds for
C^{2}
functions
f
majorized by the functions
h_{0}
or
h_{\alpha-1}
, in fact, by their sum.
∎

5.
Function spaces and the Dirichlet form

In this section, we prove the Hardy inequality for
K
in case
\alpha\neq 1
and investigate the Dirichlet form
\mathcal{E}
corresponding to the process
X
. We also prove various characterizations of the domain of the form
\mathcal{E}
.

5.1.
Dirichlet form

From Section
2.2
we know that
(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is symmetric and strongly continuous contraction semigroup on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Following
[
22
, p. 3]
or
[
38
, p. 23]
, for
t>0
,

\mathcal{E}^{(t)}(u,v):=\frac{1}{t}\langle u-K_{t}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},\qquad u,v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

is a symmetric form on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. From general theory (see, e.g.,
[
22
]
,
[
38
]
) it turns out that for
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\mathcal{E}^{(t)}(u,u)
is non-negative. Moreover, if
t>0
decreases, then
\mathcal{E}^{(t)}(u,u)
is increasing (it follows from the spectral representation of
(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
). Therefore, the limit
\lim\limits_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{E}^{(t)}(u,u)=\sup\limits_{t>0}\mathcal{E}^{(t)}(u,u)
exists, and we may then set

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:=\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}):~\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{E}^{(t)}(u,u)<\infty\},

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v):=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{E}^{(t)}(u,v),\qquad u,v\in\mathcal{F}.

Then
\mathcal{E}
becomes a closed symmetric form on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
corresponding to the semigroup
(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
(see
[
22
, Chapter 1, p. 3]
). Moreover, from Lemma
2.10
it follows that for each
t\geq 0
the operator
K_{t}
is
Markovian
, i.e. for
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
0\leq u\leq 1
we have
0\leq K_{t}u\leq 1
. Hence, from
[
38
, Theorem 1.4.1]
, the form
\mathcal{E}
is
Markovian
(for the definition of Markovian property of symmetric forms see, e.g.,
[
38
, p. 4]
). Therefore, the form
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
corresponding to the semigroup
(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is in fact a
Dirichlet form
.

In what follows we will use the following abbreviation:
\mathcal{E}[u]:=\mathcal{E}(u,u)
,
u\in\mathcal{F}
. We want to extend this definition to the whole space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, by setting
\mathcal{E}[v]=\infty
if
v\notin\mathcal{F}
. This convention will be useful in the formulation of the Hardy inequality for the form
\mathcal{E}
.

From the general properties of the Dirichlet forms we can prove the following (see proof of the Theorem 1.4.2 in
[
38
]
): if
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
,
w\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
satisfy

|w(x)-w(y)|\leq|u_{1}(x)-u_{1}(y)|+|u_{2}(x)-u_{2}(y)|

and

|w(x)|\leq|u_{1}(x)|+|u_{2}(x)|

almost everywhere, then
w\in\mathcal{F}
and

(5.2)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathcal{E}[w]}\leq\sqrt{\mathcal{E}[u_{1}]}+\sqrt{\mathcal{E}[u_{2}]}.

By taking
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
and
w:=u_{1}+u_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, from (
5.2
), we obtain the following triangle inequality for
\mathcal{E}^{1/2}
:

(5.3)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathcal{E}[u_{1}+u_{2}]}\leq\sqrt{\mathcal{E}[u_{1}]}+\sqrt{\mathcal{E}[u_{2}]},\qquad u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathcal{F}.

Hence, from the definition of the form
\mathcal{E}
and from the property (
5.3
), it follows that in fact
\sqrt{\mathcal{E}[\cdot]}
is a seminorm on
\mathcal{F}
. From (
5.3
) it follows also that
\mathcal{F}
is in fact a linear subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

On the space
\mathcal{F}
we define an inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}
and a norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}
by the following expressions:

\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}:=\langle u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\mathcal{E}(u,v),\qquad u,v\in\mathcal{F},

(5.4)

\displaystyle\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}:=\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\mathcal{E}[u],\qquad u\in\mathcal{F}.

The fact that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}
is an inner product is an elementary exercise, and we will not present its details. Moreover, let us observe that the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}
is norm induced by the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}
.
Then from the fact that
\mathcal{E}
is a Dirichlet form,
(\mathcal{F},\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}})
becomes a Hilbert space.

5.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

We will use the method of Bogdan, Dyda, Kim
[
12
]
.

Let
h_{\beta}(x)=|x|^{\beta}
for
\beta(\alpha-\beta-1)>0
and
v:=u/h_{\beta}
, with the convention that for
h_{\beta}(x)=0
or
\infty
, we have
v(x)=0
.
Of course
vh_{\beta}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, because
|vh_{\beta}|\leq|u|
. Moreover, from Corollary
2.20
we know that
vK_{t}h_{\beta}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Therefore, for
t>0
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(t)}[vh_{\beta}]=\frac{1}{t}\langle v(h_{\beta}-K_{t}h_{\beta}),vh_{\beta}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\frac{1}{t}\langle vK_{t}h_{\beta}-K_{t}(vh_{\beta}),vh_{\beta}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=:A_{t}+B_{t}.

Note that

B_{t}=\frac{1}{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\,v(x)h_{\beta}(x)h_{\beta}(y)(v(x)-v(y))

Moreover, using Lemma
2.5
, we obtain

\displaystyle B_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\langle vK_{t}h_{\beta},vh_{\beta}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}-\frac{1}{t}\langle K_{t}(vh_{\beta}),vh_{\beta}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\,v^{2}(x)h_{\beta}(x)h_{\beta}(y)-\frac{1}{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\,v(x)h_{\beta}(x)v(y)h_{\beta}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\,v^{2}(y)h_{\beta}(y)h_{\beta}(x)-\frac{1}{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\,v(x)h_{\beta}(x)v(y)h_{\beta}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\,v(y)h_{\beta}(y)h_{\beta}(x)(v(y)-v(x)).

From the above equalities, we get

\displaystyle B_{t}=\frac{1}{2t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\,h_{\beta}(x)h_{\beta}(y)(v(x)-v(y))^{2}\geq 0,

hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(t)}[vh_{\beta}]\geq A_{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}v^{2}(x)h_{\beta}(x)\frac{h_{\beta}(x)-K_{t}h_{\beta}(x)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}x.

From Corollary
2.20
, the integrand is non-negative and then from Fatou’s lemma and Proposition
4.15
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}[vh_{\beta}]

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}}v^{2}(x)h_{\beta}(x)\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{h_{\beta}(x)-K_{t}h_{\beta}(x)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}}v^{2}(x)h_{\beta}^{2}(x)\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta,x)|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Thus,

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}[u]

\displaystyle\geq(\mathcal{C}_{\alpha,\beta}+\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta})\int_{D}u^{2}(x)|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{C}_{\alpha,\beta}\int_{D^{c}}u^{2}(x)|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x,

where

\mathcal{C}_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\big[\alpha^{-1}-\mathfrak{B}(\beta+1,\alpha-\beta)\big],\qquad\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}=-\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\gamma(\alpha,\beta).

The fact that
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha,\beta}>0
and
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}>0
follows also from Proposition
4.15
.

Now, we will show that the constants
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha,\beta}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
have the biggest values for
\beta=(\alpha-1)/2
. Recall that

\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}=-\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(t^{\beta}-1)(1-t^{\alpha-\beta-1})}{(1-t)^{\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}t,

and define the function

u(\beta,t)=\frac{(t^{\beta}-1)(1-t^{\alpha-\beta-1})}{(1-t)^{\alpha+1}},

where
t\in(0,1)
and
\beta(\alpha-\beta-1)>0
. We will calculate the derivative

\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta}\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}=-\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta}\int_{0}^{1}u(\beta,t)\,{\rm d}t.

We want to use
[
61
, Theorem 11.5]
to change the order of derivative and integral.
Obviously, the function
(0,1)\ni t\mapsto u(\beta,t)
is integrable for each considered
\beta
and the function
\beta\mapsto u(\beta,t)
is differentiable for each
t\in(0,1)
. Moreover, note that for
t\in(0,1)
,

(5.6)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta}u(\beta,t)\right|=\frac{|\ln{t}|}{(1-t)^{\alpha+1}}\big|t^{\beta}-t^{\alpha-\beta-1}\big|.

We will show that, for
t\in(0,1)
and
\beta(\alpha-\beta-1)>0
,

(5.7)

\displaystyle\big|t^{\beta}-t^{\alpha-\beta-1}\big|\leq\big|1-t^{\alpha-1}\big|.

We consider two cases. First, assume that
1<\alpha<2
and
0<\beta<\alpha-1
. Then for
\beta\in(0,\tfrac{\alpha-1}{2})
,
t^{\alpha-1}\leq t^{\alpha-1-2\beta}
and

\big|1-t^{\alpha-1}\big|=1-t^{\alpha-1}\geq 1-t^{\alpha-1-2\beta}\geq t^{\beta}\big(1-t^{\alpha-1-2\beta}\big)=t^{\beta}-t^{\alpha-\beta-1}=\big|t^{\beta}-t^{\alpha-\beta-1}\big|.

Similarly, for
\beta\in[\tfrac{\alpha-1}{2},\alpha-1)
,
t^{\alpha-1}\leq t^{2\beta-\alpha+1}
and

\big|1-t^{\alpha-1}\big|=1-t^{\alpha-1}\geq 1-t^{2\beta-\alpha+1}\geq t^{\alpha-\beta-1}\big(1-t^{2\beta-\alpha+1}\big)=t^{\alpha-\beta-1}-t^{\beta}=\big|t^{\beta}-t^{\alpha-\beta-1}\big|.

Now assume that
0<\alpha<1
and
\alpha-1<\beta<0
. Note that (
5.7
) is equivalent to the inequality

(5.8)

\displaystyle\big|t^{-\beta}-t^{(2-\alpha)+\beta-1}\big|\leq\big|1-t^{(2-\alpha)-1}\big|.

Let
\gamma:=2-\alpha\in(1,2)
and
\delta:=-\beta\in(0,\gamma-1)
and from the first case we get the following inequality

\big|t^{\delta}-t^{\gamma-\delta-1}\big|\leq\big|1-t^{\gamma-1}\big|,

which is equivalent to the inequality (
5.8
). Thus, we have proved (
5.7
) in the second case.

From (
5.6
) and (
5.7
) we have

(5.9)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta}u(\beta,t)\right|\leq\frac{|\ln{t}|}{(1-t)^{\alpha+1}}|1-t^{\alpha-1}|.

Moreover,

(5.10)

\displaystyle I:=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{|\ln{t}|}{(1-t)^{\alpha+1}}|1-t^{\alpha-1}|\,{\rm d}t<\infty.

We will show that again in two cases.

Assume that
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Note that for
t\in(1/2,1)
we have
|\ln{t}|\approx 1-t
and
t^{\alpha-1}-1\leq t^{-1}-1=(1-t)/t
. Then

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1/2}\frac{|\ln{t}|}{(1-t)^{\alpha+1}}(t^{\alpha-1}-1)\,{\rm d}t+\int_{1/2}^{1}\frac{|\ln{t}|}{(1-t)^{\alpha+1}}(t^{\alpha-1}-1)\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{1/2}|\ln{t}|\,t^{\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}t+\int_{1/2}^{1}\frac{{\rm d}t}{t(1-t)^{\alpha-1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{1/2}|\ln{t}|\,t^{\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}t+\int_{1/2}^{1}\frac{{\rm d}t}{(1-t)^{\alpha-1}}<\infty.

Now let
\alpha\in(1,2)
. Then for
t\in(0,1)
,
1-t^{\alpha-1}\leq 1-t
. Hence,

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1/2}\frac{|\ln{t}|}{(1-t)^{\alpha+1}}(1-t^{\alpha-1})\,{\rm d}t+\int_{1/2}^{1}\frac{|\ln{t}|}{(1-t)^{\alpha+1}}(1-t^{\alpha-1})\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{1/2}|\ln{t}|\,{\rm d}t+\int_{1/2}^{1}\frac{|\ln{t}|}{(1-t)^{\alpha}}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{1/2}|\ln{t}|\,{\rm d}t+\int_{1/2}^{1}\frac{{\rm d}t}{(1-t)^{\alpha-1}}<\infty.

Thus we have proved (
5.10
).

Therefore, from
[
61
, Theorem 11.5]
we obtain that

\frac{\partial}{\partial\beta}\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\ln{t}}{(1-t)^{\alpha+1}}\big[t^{\alpha-\beta-1}-t^{\beta}\big]\,{\rm d}t.

Note that for
\beta<(\alpha-1)/2
,
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\beta}\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}>0
and for
\beta>(\alpha-1)/2
,
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\beta}\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}<0
. Hence,
\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,\beta}
has the biggest value for
\beta=(\alpha-1)/2
. Moreover, from the proof of Proposition
4.15
, it follows that the constant
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha,\beta}
has the biggest value also for
\beta=(\alpha-1)/2
.

Thus, by taking the maximum over
\beta\in(0,\alpha-1)
in (
5.5
) we get the desired inequality:

\mathcal{E}[u]\geq(\mathcal{C}_{\alpha,(\alpha-1)/2}+\mathcal{D}_{\alpha,(\alpha-1)/2})\int_{D}u^{2}(x)|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{C}_{\alpha,(\alpha-1)/2}\int_{D^{c}}u^{2}(x)|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x.

The proof is complete. ∎

In case
\alpha=1
the above proof is also valid, but we obtain only trivial inequality (with constant
0
), which cannot be improved because of
[
33
, (F2) with
D=\mathbb{R}^{*}
]
.

From Theorem
1.1
we have obvious corollary.

Corollary 5.1
.

For
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}(x)|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim\mathcal{E}[u].

5.3.
The characterization of a domain of the Dirichlet form

Recall that
D=(0,\infty)
and the form
\mathcal{E}_{D}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with its natural domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D})
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{D}(u,v):=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\setminus D^{c}\times D^{c}}(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y,\qquad u,v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D}):=\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}):~\mathcal{E}_{D}(u,u)<\infty\}.

Similarly as in the case of
\mathcal{E},
we denote
\mathcal{E}_{D}[u]:=\mathcal{E}_{D}(u,u)
. Note that the set
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\setminus D^{c}\times D^{c}
in the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{D}
is equal to the sum
(D\times D)\cup(D\times D^{c})\cup(D^{c}\times D)
.

Let
u,v\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D})
. From the inequality
(a-b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
,
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we get

\mathcal{E}_{D}[u+v]=\mathcal{E}_{D}(u+v,u+v)\leq 2\left(\mathcal{E}_{D}[u]+\mathcal{E}_{D}[v]\right)<\infty,

which yields that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D})
is a linear subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}).

We want to define a norm on the space
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D})
. For this purpose, we prove the following lemma.

Lemma 5.2
.

A function
p:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D})\to[0,\infty)
defined by
p(u):=\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{D}[u]}
is a seminorm.

Proof.

The absolute homogeneity follows immediately from the definition of the form
\mathcal{E}_{D}
. We will prove the triangle inequality for
p
, i.e. the inequality
p(u+v)\leq p(u)+p(v)
,
u,v\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D})
. We will use the analogous methods as in the proof of the Minkowski inequality (see Stein and Shakarchi
[
68
, Theorem 1.2]
).

We may assume that
p(u+v)>0
, because if
p(u+v)=0
, then the triangle inequality for
p
is obvious.
From the inequality
|x+y|\leq|x|+|y|
,
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, and from the Hölder inequality, we get

\displaystyle 2p^{2}(u+v)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\setminus D^{c}\times D^{c}}(u(x)+v(x)-u(y)-v(y))^{2}\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\setminus D^{c}\times D^{c}}|u(x)-u(y)||u(x)+v(x)-u(y)-v(y)|\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle+\iint_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\setminus D^{c}\times D^{c}}|v(x)-v(y)||u(x)+v(x)-u(y)-v(y)|\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2p(u)p(u+v)+2p(v)p(u+v).

Dividing both sides of the above inequality by
2p(u+v)
we get a desired triangle inequality for
p
.
∎

In what follows, we will also consider the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}
and the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}
corresponding to the form
\mathcal{E}_{D}
defined by the following expressions:

\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}:=\langle u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\mathcal{E}_{D}(u,v),\qquad u,v\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D}),

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}^{2}:=\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{D}[u],\qquad u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D}).

In this section we propose the connection between the Dirichlet form
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
corresponding to the semigroup
(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and the form
(\mathcal{E}_{D},\mathcal{F}^{*})
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{*}:=\Big\{u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D}):~\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}(x)|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty\Big\}.

The first connection is established in the following proposition, which describes the explicit form of the Dirichlet form
\mathcal{E}
on the smooth functions with compact support.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
u,v\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
. Then
u,v\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D})\cap\mathcal{F}
and

(5.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)=\mathcal{E}_{D}(u,v).

Proof.

We will show that
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D})
. Let
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
and observe that it is obvious that there exist
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(D)
and
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}\big(\overline{D}^{c}\big)
such that
u=f+g
. From the inequality
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
,
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, it suffices to show that
f,g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D})
.

It is obvious that
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Note that from Tonelli’s theorem,

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{D}[f]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{D\times D}(f(x)-f(y))^{2}\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{D}f^{2}(x)\nu(x,D^{c})\,\mathrm{d}x.

From (
1.18
) it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{D}f^{2}(x)\nu(x,D^{c})\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\int_{\supp(f)}|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty,

hence it suffices to show the finiteness of the first integral in (
5.12
). From the fact that
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(D)
and by the symmetry it is obvious that

\displaystyle\iint_{D\times D}(f(x)-f(y))^{2}\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{(\supp{f}\times D)\cup(D\times\supp{f})}(f(x)-f(y))^{2}\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{\supp{f}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(f(x)-f(y))^{2}\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle\approx\int_{\supp{f}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D\cap\{|y-x|<1\}}\mathrm{d}y\,(f(x)-f(y))^{2}\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle+\int_{\supp{f}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D\cap\{|y-x|\geq 1\}}\mathrm{d}y\,(f(x)-f(y))^{2}\nu(x,y)=:A+B.

In case of the integral
A
we note that from Lagrange mean value theorem
|f(x)-f(y)|=|f^{\prime}(c)||x-y|
, where
c
lies between
x
and
y
. Moreover, for
|y-x|<1
,
|f^{\prime}(c)|\leq M
for some constant
M>0
. Then,

\displaystyle A\lesssim\int_{\supp{f}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\{|y-x|<1\}}\mathrm{d}y\,|x-y|^{2}\nu(x,y)\approx\int_{\supp{f}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\{|w|<1\}}|w|^{1-\alpha}\,{\rm d}w<\infty,

and

\displaystyle B\lesssim 2\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{2}_{\infty}\int_{\supp{f}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\{|y-x|\geq 1\}}\mathrm{d}y\,|x-y|^{-\alpha-1}\lesssim\int_{\supp{f}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\{|w|\geq 1\}}\mathrm{d}y\,|w|^{-\alpha-1}<\infty.

Thus we have proved that
\mathcal{E}_{D}[f]<\infty
.

Similarly,

\mathcal{E}_{D}[g]=\int_{D^{c}}g^{2}(x)\nu(x,D)\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\int_{\supp(g)}|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

Hence,
f,g\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D})
.

Let
u,v\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
and recall that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big[u(x)-K_{t}u(x)\big]v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\int_{D}\big[u(x)-K_{t}u(x)\big]v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\int_{D^{c}}\big[u(x)-K_{t}u(x)\big]v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

(5.13)

\displaystyle=:I+II,

provided the limits exist.
Therefore, without loss of generality, in what follows, we may assume that
t\in(0,1)
.

Let
x>0
. From Corollary
2.4
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\big[u(x)-K_{t}u(x)\big]

(5.14)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\big[u(x)-P_{t}^{D}u(x)\big]-\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,p_{r}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)K_{t-r}u(z).

First we show that

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{D}(u,v)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\int_{D}\big[u(x)-P_{t}^{D}u(x)\big]v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

(5.15)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\nu(x,y)+\int_{D}u(x)v(x)\nu(x,D^{c})\,\mathrm{d}x.

The exact form of the form
\mathcal{C}^{D}
of the killed stable process is commonly known (see, e.g., Fukushima et al.
[
38
, Theorem 4.5.2, p. 185]
), but we propose its proof for completion of the argument.

Note that
u(x)=u(x)p_{t}^{D}(x,D)+u(x)\big(1-p_{t}^{D}(x,D)\big)
. Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\int_{D}\big[u(x)-P_{t}^{D}u(x)\big]v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}+\int_{D}u(x)v(x)\frac{1-p_{t}^{D}(x,D)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=:A_{t}+B_{t}.

From (
1.27
) it follows that
p_{t}^{D}(x,y)/t\lesssim\nu(x,y)
. Moreover,
from the inequality
ab\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(a^{2}+b^{2})
,
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,|u(x)-u(y)||v(x)|\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\infty}t^{-1}\int_{D\cap\,\supp(v)}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(|u(x)|+|u(y)|)p_{t}^{D}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\infty}t^{-1}\left[\int_{D\cap\,\supp(v)}p_{t}^{D}(x,D)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{D\cap\,\supp(v)}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D\cap\supp(u)}p_{t}^{D}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\infty}t^{-1}\big[|\supp(v)|+t^{-1/\alpha}|\supp(v)|\cdot|\supp(u)|\big]<\infty.

From the Fubini’s theorem and from the symmetry of
p^{D}
,

\displaystyle A_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}=-\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(y)\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}.

Hence,

\displaystyle A_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}.

Note that from the inequalities
p_{t}^{D}(x,y)/t\lesssim\nu(x,y)
and
ab\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(a^{2}+b^{2})
,
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, and from the fact that

\displaystyle\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,|u(x)-u(y)||v(x)-v(y)|\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x,y)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(v(x)-v(y))^{2}\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{E}_{D}[u]+\mathcal{E}_{D}[v]<\infty,

it follows that we can use the dominated convergence theorem to obtain that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}A_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{p_{t}^{D}(x,y)}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\nu(x,y).

The latter convergence follows from Lemma
4.8
.

From (
1.30
), (
1.31
) and from Corollary
4.4
it follows that

(5.16)

\displaystyle\frac{1-p_{t}^{D}(x,D)}{t}=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}s\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,p_{s}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)\lesssim|x|^{-\alpha},

and

\displaystyle\int_{D}|u(x)v(x)||x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\infty}\int_{D\,\cap\,\supp(u)}|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

Therefore, again from the dominated convergence theorem, (
5.16
) and Lemma
4.12
it follows that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}B_{t}=\int_{D}u(x)v(x)\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1-p_{t}^{D}(x,D)}{t}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{D}u(x)v(x)\nu(x,D^{c})\,\mathrm{d}x.

Thus, we have proved (
5.15
).

Further, from the fact that
|K_{t}u(z)|\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}
,
t>0
,
z\neq 0
, and from Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle C(t,x):\!

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,p_{r}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)K_{t-r}u(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}r\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\Big[\int_{D}p_{tr}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)\,\mathrm{d}y\Big]K_{t(1-r)}u(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}r\int_{D^{c}}\mathcal{J}(tr,x,z)K_{t(1-r)}u(z)\,{\rm d}z.

From Corollary
4.3
and from (
1.9
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(tr,x,z)|K_{t(1-r)}u(z)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}(tr)^{-1}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|z|}{(tr)^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}\frac{tr}{|x-z|^{\alpha+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|z|}{r^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}|x-z|^{-\alpha-1},

and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}r\int_{D^{c}}\Big(1\wedge\frac{|z|}{r^{1/\alpha}}\Big)^{-\alpha/2}|x-z|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}z

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}r\int_{-\infty}^{-r^{1/\alpha}}|x-z|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}z+\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}r\int_{-r^{1/\alpha}}^{0}\sqrt{r}|z|^{-\alpha/2}|x-z|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}z

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\infty}^{0}|x-z|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}z+\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{r}{\rm d}r\int_{-\infty}^{0}|z|^{-\alpha/2}|x-z|^{-\alpha-1}\,{\rm d}z<\infty.

Therefore, from the dominated convergence theorem, Lemma
4.10
and Lemma
3.12
it follows that

(5.17)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}C(t,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}r\int_{D^{c}}\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{J}(tr,x,z)K_{t(1-r)}u(z)\,{\rm d}z=\int_{D^{c}}\nu(x,z)u(z)\,{\rm d}z.

From Corollary
4.4
we have

|C(t,x)|\leq\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D^{c}}{\rm d}z\,p_{r}^{D}(x,y)\nu(y,z)|K_{t-r}u(z)|\lesssim\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}|x|^{-\alpha},

and

\int_{D}|v(x)||x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\infty}\int_{D\,\cap\,\supp{(v)}}|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

Hence, from the dominated convergence theorem and from (
5.17
),

(5.18)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{D}v(x)C(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{D}v(x)\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}C(t,x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,u(y)v(x)\nu(x,y).

Combining (
5.14
), (
5.15
) and (
5.18
) we get

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\nu(x,y)

(5.19)

\displaystyle+\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x,y).

Now assume that
x<0
. From Corollary
2.4
we have

(5.20)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}\big[u(x)-K_{t}u(x)\big]

\displaystyle=u(x)\frac{1-e^{-\nu(x,D)t}}{t}-\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,e^{-\nu(x,D)r}\nu(x,y)K_{t-r}u(y).

Moreover, from the inequality
1-e^{-x}\leq x
,
x\geq 0
, and from the fact that

\int_{D^{c}}|u(x)v(x)|\nu(x,D)\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\infty}\int_{D^{c}\,\cap\,\supp{(u)}}|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty,

we can use the dominated convergence theorem to obtain that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{D^{c}}u(x)v(x)\frac{1-e^{-\nu(x,D)t}}{t}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{c}}u(x)v(x)\nu(x,D)\,\mathrm{d}x

(5.21)

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{c}}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,u(x)v(x)\nu(x,y).

Furthermore,

e^{-\nu(x,D)tr}\int_{D}\nu(x,y)|K_{t(1-r)}u(y)|\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\infty}\nu(x,D),

and

\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}r\,|v(x)|\nu(x,D)\lesssim\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\infty}\int_{D^{c}\,\cap\,\supp{(v)}}|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

Then, from the dominated convergence theorem and from Lemma
3.12
,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{D^{c}}v(x)\left[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,e^{-\nu(x,D)r}\nu(x,y)K_{t-r}u(y)\right]\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}r\,v(x)e^{-\nu(x,D)tr}\int_{D}\nu(x,y)K_{t(1-r)}u(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{0}^{1}{\rm d}r\,v(x)\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{D}\nu(x,y)K_{t(1-r)}u(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

(5.22)

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,u(y)v(x)\nu(x,y).

Combining (
5.20
), (
5.21
) and (
5.22
) we get

(5.23)

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{c}}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x,y).

From (
5.13
), (
5.19
) and (
5.23
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,v)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle+\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x,y)

(5.24)

\displaystyle+\int_{D^{c}}\,\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x,y).

Note that from the inequality
ab\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(a^{2}+b^{2})
,
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,|u(x)-u(y)||v(x)|\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x,y)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}v^{2}(x)\nu(x,D^{c})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{D}[u]+\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\int_{D\,\cap\,\supp(v)}|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

Thus, from the Fubini’s theorem and the symmetry of
\nu
we get

\displaystyle\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\,(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle=-\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(y)\nu(x,y).

Hence,

\displaystyle\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x,y)

(5.25)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x,y)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(y)\nu(x,y).

Similarly, we prove that

\displaystyle\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x,y)

(5.26)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(x)\nu(x,y)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))v(y)\nu(x,y).

Combining (
5.24
), (
5.25
) and (
5.26
) we get (
5.11
), which completes the proof.
∎

The above result gives us explicit form of the Dirichlet form
\mathcal{E}
only for the smooth functions with compact support. To extend this result for a larger set of functions, we will study equivalent definitions of the domain
\mathcal{F}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

For
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\mathcal{E}_{D}[u]\leq\mathcal{E}[u]
.

Proof.

From Lemma
2.10
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(t)}[u]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big[u(x)K_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})-K_{t}u(x)+u(x)\big(1-K_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})\big)\big]u(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\,(u(x)-u(y))u(x)+\frac{1}{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}(x)\big(1-K_{t}(x,\mathbb{R})\big)\,\mathrm{d}x

(5.27)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\,(u(x)-u(y))u(x).

Moreover,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}(x)K_{t}\mathbf{1}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty
and then from Lemma
2.5
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\,(u(x)-u(y))u(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}(x)K_{t}\mathbf{1}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(x)K_{t}u(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}u^{2}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(x)K_{t}u(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\,(u(y)-u(x))u(y).

Hence,

(5.28)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\,(u(x)-u(y))u(x)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\,(u(x)-u(y))^{2}.

Recall that
K_{t,1}(x,D)=0
for
x>0
and
K_{t,1}(x,D^{c})=0
for
x<0
. Combining (
5.27
) and (
5.28
) we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(t)}[u]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{t}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\,(u(x)-u(y))^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2t}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,p_{t}^{D}(x,y)(u(x)-u(y))^{2}+\frac{1}{2t}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D^{c}}K_{t,1}(x,\mathrm{d}y)(u(x)-u(y))^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2t}\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}K_{t,1}(x,\mathrm{d}y)(u(x)-u(y))^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2t}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,p_{t}^{D}(x,y)(u(x)-u(y))^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\,\Big[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}{\rm d}a\,p_{r}^{D}(x,a)\nu(a,y)e^{-\nu(y,D)(t-r)}\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\Big[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}{\rm d}r\int_{D}{\rm d}b\,e^{-\nu(x,D)r}\nu(x,b)p_{t-r}^{D}(b,y)\Big].

From the Fatou’s lemma, Lemma
4.8
and Lemma
4.11
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}[u]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x,y)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x,y)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{D^{c}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(y,x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{D}[u],

which is our claim.
∎

For
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
A\subset\mathbb{R}
, we set

\displaystyle u_{A}(x):=u(x)\ind_{A}(x)=\begin{cases}u(x),&x\in A,\\
0,&x\notin A.\end{cases}

Of course
u_{A}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
u=u_{D}+u_{\overline{D}^{c}}
a.e.
With such definition, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
. Then each of the functions
u_{D}
and
u_{\overline{D}^{c}}
belongs to
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D})
.

Proof.

From (
1.18
) we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{D}[u_{D}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{D}\int_{D}(u(x)-u(y))^{2}\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{D}u^{2}(x)\nu(x,D^{c})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{D}[u]+\int_{D}u^{2}(x)|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

Then similarly,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{D}[u_{\overline{D}^{c}}]

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{c}}u^{2}(x)\nu(x,D)\,\mathrm{d}x\approx\int_{D^{c}}u^{2}(x)|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x<\infty.\qed

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Assume that there exists a sequence
(u_{n})\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
such that
\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, then
\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Conversely, if there exists
(u_{n})\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
such that
\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, then
\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Hence,

\overline{C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})}^{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}}=\overline{C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})}^{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}}.

Proof.

Assume that there exists
(u_{n})\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
such that
\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}\to 0
. Then
(u_{n})
is a Cauchy sequence with respect to the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}
, i.e.
\left\lVert u_{n}-u_{m}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}=\left\lVert u_{n}-u_{m}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\mathcal{E}[u_{n}-u_{m}]\to 0
as
n,m\to\infty
. From Proposition
5.3
we get that
\left\lVert u_{n}-u_{m}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\mathcal{E}_{D}[u_{n}-u_{m}]\to 0
as
n,m\to\infty
. Thus
\left\lVert u_{n}-u_{m}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}\to 0
,
n,m\to\infty
. From the fact that
\mathcal{E}_{D}
is closed and symmetric form (for the proof of the closedness see Voight
[
69
, Lemma 2.19]
), it follows that there exists
\widehat{u}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D})
such that
\left\lVert\widehat{u}-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
(see
[
38
, p. 4]
). We have obtained so far that in particular
\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\to 0
and
\left\lVert\widehat{u}-u_{n}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\to 0
,
n\to\infty
. Hence, from the uniqueness of the limit in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
it follows that
\widehat{u}=u
a.e. Therefore,
\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}=\left\lVert\widehat{u}-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

We know that
\mathcal{E}
is a Dirichlet form, hence it is closed. Therefore, the second implication follows in the same way.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

If
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D})
and
u=0
a.e. on
D
then there exists a sequence
(u_{n})\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
such that
\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

Note that

(5.29)

\displaystyle\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{c}}|u(x)|^{2}\big(1+\nu(x,D)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\overline{D}^{c}}\Big[u(x)\sqrt{1+\nu(x,D)}\Big]^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Let
f(x):=u(x)\sqrt{1+\nu(x,D)}
for
x\in\overline{D}^{c}
. Then from (
5.29
) it follows that
f\in L^{2}(\overline{D}^{c})
. From Lemma 3.1 in
[
67
, p. 222]
it follows that there exists a sequence
(f_{n})\subset C_{c}^{\infty}\big(\overline{D}^{c}\big)
such that
\left\lVert f-f_{n}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\overline{D}^{c})}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\lVert f-f_{n}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\overline{D}^{c})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{c}}|f(x)-f_{n}(x)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{D^{c}}\Big[u(x)\sqrt{1+\nu(x,D)}-f_{n}(x)\Big]^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

(5.30)

\displaystyle=\int_{D^{c}}\Big[u(x)-u_{n}(x)\Big]^{2}\big(1+\nu(x,D)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\to 0,

where
u_{n}(x):=\frac{f_{n}(x)}{\sqrt{1+\nu(x,D)}}
for
x\in\overline{D}^{c}
. It is an easy exercise to show that
u_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{D}^{c})
. We extend
u_{n}
to
\mathbb{R}_{*}
by letting
u_{n}=0
on
D
and from (
5.29
) and (
5.30
) we get

\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}^{2}=\int_{D^{c}}\big[u(x)-u_{n}(x)\big]^{2}\big(1+\nu(x,D)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\to 0,

as
n\to\infty
.
∎

Proposition 5.8
.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
, we have the following equalities between the domains:

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}^{*}=\overline{C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})}^{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}}=\overline{C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})}^{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}}.

Proof.

Note that the inclusion
\overline{C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})}^{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}}\subset\mathcal{F}
is obvious by the definition. Therefore, from Lemma
5.6
it suffices to show the following inclusions:
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{F}^{*}\subset\overline{C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})}^{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}}
.

Assume that
u\in\mathcal{F}
, i.e.
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathcal{E}[u]<\infty
. Then from Lemma
5.4
,
\mathcal{E}_{D}[u]<\infty
. Moreover, from Corollary
5.1
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}u^{2}(x)|x|^{-\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}x\lesssim\mathcal{E}[u]<\infty
. Hence,
u\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
.

Assume that
u\in\mathcal{F}^{*}
. From Lemma
5.5
it follows that
u_{D}
and
u_{\overline{D}^{c}}
belongs to
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{E}_{D})
. Note that

\mathcal{E}_{D}[u_{D}]=\frac{1}{2}\iint_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}}(u_{D}(x)-u_{D}(y))^{2}\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y,

and by Fiscella et al.
[
37
, Theorem 6]
it follows that there exists a sequence
(f_{n})\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
such that
\left\lVert u_{D}-f_{n}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\mathcal{E}_{D}^{1/2}[u_{D}-f_{n}]\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Therefore,
\left\lVert u_{D}-f_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
Moreover, from Lemma
5.7
for
u_{\overline{D}^{c}}
there exists a sequence
(g_{n})\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
such that
\left\lVert u_{\overline{D}^{c}}-g_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
u_{n}:=f_{n}+g_{n}
and note that
u_{n}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}=\left\lVert(u_{D}-f_{n})+(u_{\overline{D}^{c}}-g_{n})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}\leq\left\lVert u_{D}-f_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}+\left\lVert u_{\overline{D}^{c}}-g_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}\to 0,

as
n\to\infty
. Thus
u\in\overline{C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})}^{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}}
. ∎

For an analogous result to the one given in Proposition
5.8
, see
[
16
, Proposition 6.3]
.

In terms of Dirichlet forms, we may then say that the form
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
is in fact regular. Indeed, by the general theory of Dirichlet forms (see
[
38
, p. 6]
) it follows that the symmetric form
\mathcal{E}
is called
regular
if there exists a subset
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{F}\cap C_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
such that
\mathcal{C}
is dense in
\mathcal{F}
with norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}
and dense in
C_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
with uniform norm. The set
\mathcal{C}
is then called a
core
of the form
\mathcal{E}
. From Proposition
5.8
it follows immediately that
\mathcal{C}=C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
is a core for
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
. According to this, we get the following corollary.

Corollary 5.9
.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
, the form
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
is regular.

The next proposition describes the explicit form of the form
\mathcal{E}
on the larger class of functions than in the Proposition
5.3
.

Proposition 5.10
.

For
\alpha\in(0,2)
and
u\in\overline{C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})}^{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}}
,

\mathcal{E}[u]=\mathcal{E}_{D}[u].

Proof.

Let
u\in\overline{C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})}^{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}}
. Then there exists a sequence
(u_{n})\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
such that
\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}\to 0
,
n\to\infty
. From (
5.3
), (
5.4
) and from Proposition
5.3
it follows that

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathcal{E}[u]}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mathcal{E}[(u-u_{n})+u_{n}]}\leq\sqrt{\mathcal{E}[u-u_{n}]}+\sqrt{\mathcal{E}[u_{n}]}\leq\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{F}}+\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{D}[u_{n}]}.

By taking
n\to\infty
, we get

(5.31)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\mathcal{E}[u]}\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{D}[u_{n}]}.

We will show that
\mathcal{E}_{D}[u_{n}]\to\mathcal{E}_{D}[u]
,
n\to\infty
. Indeed, from Lemma
5.6
it follows that
\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
and then from Lemma
5.2
, by the inverse triangle inequality for
\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{D}[\cdot]}
, we have

(5.32)

\displaystyle\big|\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{D}[u]}-\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{D}[u_{n}]}\big|\leq\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{D}[u-u_{n}]}\leq\left\lVert u-u_{n}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{E}_{D}}\to 0,

as
n\to\infty
. Thus, from (
5.31
) and (
5.32
), we get the inequality
\mathcal{E}[u]\leq\mathcal{E}_{D}[u]
, which, together with Lemma
5.4
, ends the proof.
∎

6.
Boundary problems

This section is devoted to finding the direct solutions of two nonlocal boundary problems. For this purpose, we use the
Dynkin characteristic operator
, which is defined in Section
6.1
.
The first problem involves the
\lambda
-potentials
(or the
resolvents
), see. Corollary
6.2
below. It is a well-known formula for the Feller generators (see, e.g., Dynkin
[
34
, Theorem 1.1, p. 24]
) and we prove that it holds in our case also for the Dynkin characteristic operator.
The second problem is central for the paper—we prove that under certain assumptions on the function
f
, the solution of the Neumann boundary problem

\displaystyle\ \begin{cases}\hfill(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}u&=~f,\quad\mathrm{in}~D,\\
\hfill\mathcal{N}_{\alpha/2}u&=~f,\quad\,\mathrm{in}~\overline{D}^{c}.\end{cases}

is given by the Green operator
G
of
K
.

6.1.
Dynkin characteristic operator

The
Dynkin characteristic
operator for the process
X=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is defined by the following expression

(6.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}f(x):=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{x}f(X_{\tau_{B(x,r)}})-f(x)}{\mathbb{E}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}},\quad x\neq 0.

Here
x\in\mathbb{R}_{*}
and a function
f
are such that the limit exists.

Assume that
x>0
and a function
f
are such that the limit (
6.1
) exists. Then, from the construction of the process
X
, from Lemma
2.7
and from the Ikeda–Watanabe formula (
1.30
) it follows that
\mathbb{E}_{x}f(X_{\tau_{B(x,r)}})=\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}f(Y_{\tau_{B(x,r)}})
and
\mathbb{E}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}=\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}f(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}f(Y_{\tau_{B(x,r)}})-f(x)}{\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}},\qquad x>0.

Moreover, from Kwaśnicki
[
50
, Lemma 3.3]
it follows that

(6.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}f(x)

\displaystyle=-(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}f(x),\qquad x>0,

i.e. the operator
\mathcal{D}
, for
x>0
, is the fractional Laplacian on
\mathbb{R}
.

Now, assume that
x<0
and
f
is such that
\widehat{\nu}|f|(x)<\infty.

Then, from the construction of the process
X
, it is obvious that

\mathbb{E}_{x}f(X_{\tau_{B(x,r)}})=\int_{D}f(y)k(x,\mathrm{d}y)=\int_{D}f(y)\frac{\nu(x,y)}{\nu(x,D)}\,\mathrm{d}y,

and

\mathbb{E}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}=\frac{1}{\nu(x,D)},

which follows from the fact that for
x<0
,
\tau_{B(x,r)}
is the random variable from the exponential distribution with mean
1/\nu(x,D)
. Hence,

(6.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}f(x)=-\mathcal{N}_{\alpha/2}f(x)=-\int_{D}(f(x)-f(y))\nu(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\qquad x<0,

i.e. the operator
\mathcal{D}
, for
x<0
, is the
nonlocal normal derivative
(see, e.g., Dipierro et al.
[
32
]
). Further, note that from (
6.3
), we also have the following equality:

(6.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}f(x)=\widehat{\nu}f(x)-f(x)\nu(x,D).

6.2.
The
\lambda
-potentials

Here assume that
f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
and define the
\lambda
-potential
,
\lambda>0
, of the semigroup
K=(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by

U_{\lambda}f(x):=\mathbb{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}f(X_{t})\,{\rm d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}K_{t}f(x)\,{\rm d}t,\qquad x\neq 0.

Then of course
U_{\lambda}|f|<\infty
for all
\lambda>0
. Indeed, for
x\neq 0
, from Lemma
2.10
,

\displaystyle U_{\lambda}|f|(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}K_{t}|f|(x)\,{\rm d}t\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}K_{t}\mathbf{1}(x)\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}\,{\rm d}t=\lambda^{-1}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}<\infty.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
\lambda>0
and
f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
. Then,
u=U_{\lambda}f
is the solution of the equation
(\mathcal{D}-\lambda\mathrm{I})u=-f
in
\mathbb{R}_{*}
.

Proof.

For
\lambda>0
and
f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
,
u=U_{\lambda}f
is well-defined.
From
[
34
, Theorem 5.1]
it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\big(e^{-\lambda\tau_{B(x,r)}}u(X_{\tau_{B(x,r)}})\big)=u(x)-\mathbb{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(x,r)}}e^{-\lambda t}f(X_{t})\,{\rm d}t.

Recall that we may use cited theorem, because from Chapter 3.3 in Dynkin
[
34
]
it follows that each right-continuous process is strongly measurable.

Hence, for
x\neq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}u(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[(1-e^{-\lambda\tau_{B(x,r)}})u(X_{\tau_{B(x,r)}})\big]}{\mathbb{E}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}}-\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(x,r)}}e^{-\lambda t}f(X_{t})\,{\rm d}t

(6.5)

\displaystyle=:I-II.

Assume that
x>0
. Since
\tau_{B(x,r)}\leq\zeta^{(1)}
, we have
X_{t}=Y_{t}
for
t<\tau_{B(x,r)}
. Therefore, from Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(x,r)}}e^{-\lambda t}f(X_{t})\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(x,r)}}e^{-\lambda t}f(Y_{t})\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}f(Y_{t})\ind_{[t,\infty)}(\tau_{B(x,r)})\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\big[e^{-\lambda t}f(Y_{t})\ind_{[t,\infty)}(\tau_{B(x,r)})\big]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\big[e^{-\lambda t}f(x+Y_{t})\ind_{[t,\infty)}(\tau_{B(0,r)})\big]\,{\rm d}t.

From the scaling property of
(Y_{t},\tau_{B(x,r)})
with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{Y}
(see (
1.11
)) and again from the Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(x,r)}}e^{-\lambda t}f(Y_{t})\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\big[e^{-\lambda t}f(x+rY_{r^{-\alpha}t})\ind_{[t,\infty)}(r^{\alpha}\tau_{B(0,1)})\big]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\int_{0}^{r^{\alpha}\tau_{B(0,1)}}e^{-\lambda t}f(x+rY_{r^{-\alpha}t})\,{\rm d}t.

Using the substitution
s=r^{-\alpha}t
we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(x,r)}}e^{-\lambda t}f(Y_{t})\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=r^{\alpha}\,\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(0,1)}}e^{-\lambda sr^{\alpha}}f(x+rY_{s})\,{\rm d}s.

Moreover, from the shift invariance of the process
Y
,
\mathbb{E}_{x}^{Y}\tau_{B(x,r)}=\mathbb{E}_{0}^{Y}\tau_{B(0,r)}=r^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}_{0}^{Y}\tau_{B(0,1)}
. Hence, from the dominated convergence theorem,

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}}\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(x,r)}}e^{-\lambda t}f(Y_{t})\,{\rm d}t

(6.6)

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{0}^{Y}\tau_{B(0,1)}}\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(0,1)}}e^{-\lambda sr^{\alpha}}f(x+rY_{s})\,{\rm d}s=f(x).

In case of the limit
I
we proceed similarly: from (
1.12
),

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\big[(1-e^{-\lambda\tau_{B(x,r)}})u(Y_{\tau_{B(x,r)}})\big]}{\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\big[(1-e^{-\lambda r^{\alpha}\tau_{B(0,1)}})u(x+rY_{\tau_{B(0,1)}})\big]}{r^{\alpha}\,\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\tau_{B(0,1)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{0}^{Y}\tau_{B(0,1)}}\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\mathbb{E}_{0}^{Y}\left[\frac{1-e^{-\lambda r^{\alpha}\tau_{B(0,1)}}}{r^{\alpha}}u(x+rY_{\tau_{B(0,1)}})\right].

Note that
u(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda t}K_{t}f(x)\,{\rm d}t
is bounded, which follows from the fact that
f
is bounded. Moreover, from Proposition
2.16
, it follows that the function
\mathbb{R}_{*}\ni x\mapsto u(x)
is continuous. Hence, from the inequality
1-e^{-z}\leq z
for
z>0
, it follows that we can use the dominated convergence theorem to obtain that

(6.7)

\displaystyle I=\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{0}^{Y}\tau_{B(0,1)}}\mathbb{E}_{0}^{Y}\left[\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1-e^{-\lambda r^{\alpha}\tau_{B(0,1)}}}{r^{\alpha}}u(x+rY_{\tau_{B(0,1)}})\right]=\lambda u(x).

Combining (
6.5
), (
6.6
) and (
6.7
) we obtain the equality
\mathcal{D}u(x)=\lambda u(x)-f(x)
,
x>0
.

Now assume that
x<0
. From the construction of the process
X
and from Lemma
2.7
it follows that

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[(1-e^{-\lambda R_{1}})u(X_{R_{1}})\big]}{\mathbb{E}_{x}R_{1}}

\displaystyle=\nu(x,D)\,\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[(1-e^{-\lambda R_{1}})u(X_{R_{1}})\big]

\displaystyle=\nu(x,D)\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}s\int_{D}\mathrm{d}y\,e^{-\nu(x,D)s}\nu(x,y)(1-e^{-\lambda s})u(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(x,D)e^{-\nu(x,D)s}(1-e^{-\lambda s})\,{\rm d}s\int_{D}\nu(x,y)u(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

(6.8)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{\nu(x,D)+\lambda}\widehat{\nu}u(x).

Similarly,

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle=f(x)\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{x}R_{1}}\,\mathbb{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{R_{1}}e^{-\lambda t}\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=f(x)\nu(x,D)\lambda^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[1-e^{-\lambda R_{1}}\big]

\displaystyle=f(x)\nu(x,D)\lambda^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\nu(x,D)e^{-\nu(x,D)s}\big(1-e^{-\lambda s}\big)\,{\rm d}s

(6.9)

\displaystyle=f(x)\frac{\nu(x,D)}{\nu(x,D)+\lambda}.

Combining (
6.5
), (
6.8
) and (
6.9
) we obtain the equality

(6.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}u(x)=\frac{\lambda}{\nu(x,D)+\lambda}\widehat{\nu}u(x)-f(x)\frac{\nu(x,D)}{\nu(x,D)+\lambda},\qquad x<0.

From (
6.4
) the equation (
6.10
) takes the form

(6.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}u(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{\nu(x,D)+\lambda}\big[\mathcal{D}u(x)+u(x)\nu(x,D)\big]-f(x)\frac{\nu(x,D)}{\nu(x,D)+\lambda},

which is equivalent to the equation
\mathcal{D}u(x)=\lambda u(x)-f(x)
,
x<0
.
∎

From Proposition
6.1
, (
6.2
) and (
6.3
) we have the following corollary.

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,2)
,
\lambda>0
and
f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
. Then,
u=U_{\lambda}f
is the solution of the following Neumann problem for the fractional Laplacian

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\big[(-\Delta)^{\alpha/2}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\big]u&=f,\quad\mathrm{in}~D,\\
\phantom{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,}\big[\mathcal{N}_{\alpha/2}+\lambda\mathrm{I}\big]u&=f,\quad\mathrm{in}~\overline{D}^{c}.\end{cases}

6.3.
The
0
-potential

In this section, we assume that
f\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
, i.e.
f
is a bounded continuous function with compact support. We define the
0
-potential
or the
Green operator
of the semigroup
K=(K_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by

(6.12)

\displaystyle Gf(x):=\mathbb{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}f(X_{t})\,{\rm d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}K_{t}f(x)\,{\rm d}t,\qquad x\neq 0.

Lemma 6.3
.

If
\alpha\in(0,2)
and
\beta(\alpha-\beta-1)>0
, then
Gh_{\beta-\alpha}\lesssim h_{\beta}
on
\mathbb{R}^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
x\neq 0
. Recall that
h_{\beta}(x)=|x|^{\beta}
. From Proposition
4.15
,

(6.13)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{h_{\beta}(y)-K_{t}h_{\beta}(y)}{t}=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta,y)|y|^{\beta-\alpha}\geq\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)|y|^{\beta-\alpha},\qquad y\neq 0,

where
\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha,\beta):=\mathcal{A}_{1,\alpha}\big[\alpha^{-1}-\mathfrak{B}(\beta+1,\alpha-\beta)\big]
. For
s,t>0
, from Corollary
2.20
,
h_{\beta}-K_{t}h_{\beta}\geq 0
and
K_{s}\big[h_{\beta}-K_{t}h_{\beta}\big](x)\geq 0
.
Hence, from (
6.13
) and from Fatou’s lemma,

\displaystyle G|x|^{\beta-\alpha}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{s}(x,\mathrm{d}y)|y|^{\beta-\alpha}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\leq\big(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)\big)^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{s}(x,\mathrm{d}y)\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{h_{\beta}(y)-K_{t}h_{\beta}(y)}{t}\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\leq\big(\mathcal{C}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)\big)^{-1}\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{0}^{\infty}K_{s}\left[\frac{h_{\beta}-K_{t}h_{\beta}}{t}\right](x)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\approx\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big[K_{s}h_{\beta}(x)-K_{s+t}h_{\beta}(x)\big]\,{\rm d}s

(6.14)

\displaystyle=\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{(i-1)t}^{it}\big[K_{s}h_{\beta}(x)-K_{s+t}h_{\beta}(x)\big]\,{\rm d}s.

Again, from Corollary
2.20
, for
s,t>0
,
K_{t+s}h_{\beta}(x)=K_{s}\big(K_{t}h_{\beta}\big)(x)\leq K_{s}h_{\beta}(x)
, hence the function
s\mapsto K_{s}h_{\beta}(x)
is non-increasing. Therefore, from (
6.14
),

\displaystyle G|x|^{\beta-\alpha}

\displaystyle\lesssim\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\int_{(i-1)t}^{it}\big[K_{(i-1)t}h_{\beta}(x)-K_{(i+1)t}h_{\beta}(x)\big]\,{\rm d}s

(6.15)

\displaystyle=\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\big[K_{(i-1)t}h_{\beta}(x)-K_{(i+1)t}h_{\beta}(x)\big].

Note that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle h_{\beta}(x)+K_{t}h_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big[K_{(i-1)t}h_{\beta}(x)-K_{(i+1)t}h_{\beta}(x)\big]+K_{nt}h_{\beta}(x)+K_{(n+1)t}h_{\beta}(x),

so

(6.16)

\displaystyle h_{\beta}(x)+K_{t}h_{\beta}(x)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\big[K_{(i-1)t}h_{\beta}(x)-K_{(i+1)t}h_{\beta}(x)\big].

From (
6.15
), (
6.16
) and from Proposition
4.15
,

G|x|^{\beta-\alpha}\lesssim\liminf_{t\to 0^{+}}\big[h_{\beta}(x)+K_{t}h_{\beta}(x)\big]=2h_{\beta}(x)<\infty.\qed

Corollary 6.4
.

Let
x\neq 0
,
\alpha\in(0,2)
and
\beta(\alpha-\beta-1)>0
. If
f
is a function such that
|f(x)|\lesssim|x|^{\beta-\alpha}
, then
G|f|(x)<\infty
. In particular,

•

G(1+|x|)^{-1-\kappa}<\infty
for every
\kappa>0
,

•

if
f
is a bounded and compactly supported function on
\mathbb{R}_{*}
, then
G|f|(x)<\infty
.

Proof.

We will prove that
G(1+|x|)^{-1-\kappa}<\infty
for every
\kappa>0
.
We observe that for every
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
and
\kappa>0
there exists
\beta
such that
\beta(\alpha-\beta-1)>0
and
\beta\geq\alpha-1-\kappa.
For such
\beta
, we have

(1+|x|)^{-1-\kappa}\leq(1+|x|)^{\beta-\alpha}\leq|x|^{\beta-\alpha},\qquad x\neq 0,

and the result follows from Lemma
6.3
.
The other statements of the corollary are obvious.
∎

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,2)
,
f\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
. Then,
u:=Gf
solves
\mathcal{D}u=-f
in
\mathbb{R}_{*}
.

Proof.

From Corollary
6.4
,
u=Gf
is well-defined. From
[
34
, Theorem 5.1]
it follows that

(6.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}u(X_{\tau_{B(x,r)}})=u(x)-\mathbb{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(x,r)}}f(X_{t})\,{\rm d}t.

Hence, for
x>0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}u(x)

\displaystyle=-\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(x,r)}}f(X_{t})\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=-\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(x,r)}}\big(f(X_{t})-f(x)\big)\,{\rm d}t+f(x)\mathbb{E}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}\right]

\displaystyle=-f(x)-\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(x,r)}}\big(f(X_{t})-f(x)\big)\,{\rm d}t

(6.18)

\displaystyle=-f(x)-\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}}\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(x,r)}}\big(f(Y_{t})-f(x)\big)\,{\rm d}t.

Note that from Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}u(x)

\displaystyle=-f(x)-\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{x}\big[\big(f(Y_{t})-f(x)\big)\ind_{[t,\infty)}(\tau_{B(x,r)})\big]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=-f(x)-\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\tau_{B(0,r)}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\big[\big(f(x+Y_{t})-f(x)\big)\ind_{[t,\infty)}(\tau_{B(0,r)})\big]\,{\rm d}t.

From the scaling property of
(Y_{t},\tau_{B(x,r)})
with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{Y}
(see (
1.11
)), Fubini’s theorem and by the substitution
s=r^{-\alpha}t
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}u(x)

\displaystyle=-f(x)-\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{r^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\tau_{B(0,1)}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\big[\big(f(x+rY_{r^{-\alpha}t})-f(x)\big)\ind_{[t,\infty)}(r^{\alpha}\tau_{B(0,1)})\big]\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=-f(x)-\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{r^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\tau_{B(0,1)}}\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\int_{0}^{r^{\alpha}\tau_{B(0,1)}}\big(f(x+rY_{r^{-\alpha}t})-f(x)\big)\,{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=-f(x)-\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\tau_{B(0,1)}}\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(0,1)}}\big(f(x+rY_{s})-f(x)\big)\,{\rm d}s.

Hence, from the dominated convergence theorem,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}u(x)=-f(x)-\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\tau_{B(0,1)}}\mathbb{E}^{Y}_{0}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(0,1)}}\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\big(f(x+rY_{s})-f(x)\big)\,{\rm d}s=-f(x).

Now, consider
x<0
. From (
6.17
) and from the construction of the process
X
, it is obvious that

\mathcal{D}u(x)=-\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{x}\tau_{B(x,r)}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\int_{0}^{\tau_{B(x,r)}}f(X_{t})\,{\rm d}t=-f(x).\qed

By Lemma
6.3
and the proof of Corollary
4.14
, for
f\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R}_{*})
,
\widehat{\nu}|Gf|<\infty
.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

The result follows by Proposition
6.5
, (
6.2
), and (
6.3
).
∎
